### [1] [Do small language models know what they don't know?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20824)
*Prashant Mudgal*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper finds that standard token-level entropy is almost useless as a confidence measure for very small language models, but semantic entropy (via multiple samples and clustering) provides a strong uncertainty signal that enables effective routing to larger models and big accuracy gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small Language Models that can run on consumer hardware are attractive for cost and privacy, but their accuracy is limited. Confidence-based methods, especially entropy-based ones, are widely used with larger models for calibration, early stopping, and selective routing. It is unclear whether these methods still work at SLM scale, and whether they can meaningfully improve accuracy or efficiency. The paper aims to test and clarify the viability of entropy-based confidence signals for SLMs and how best to use them.

Method: The authors systematically evaluate seven entropy-based confidence strategies across seven SLM–expert model pairs and five standard NLU benchmarks. They measure token-level entropy of outputs for early stopping and confidence estimation, and they implement semantic entropy by sampling multiple outputs, clustering them semantically, and computing the uncertainty over clusters. They then use these confidence signals to perform uncertainty-aware routing from an SLM to a larger expert LLM, comparing within-family routing (same model family) to cross-family routing (different model families).

Result: Token-level entropy in SLMs is almost always near zero, independent of whether answers are correct, in 91% of dataset–model settings, so it cannot distinguish confident from unconfident predictions. In contrast, semantic entropy computed via multiple samples and clustering produces a useful confidence signal. Using this signal for selective routing to a larger expert model yields accuracy gains up to +50 percentage points. Cross-family routing (e.g., SmolLM 360M to Phi-3.5-mini) performs substantially better, averaging +22.0% accuracy improvement, than same-family routing, which averages +6.8%.

Conclusion: Entropy at the token level essentially fails as a confidence metric for small language models, but semantic entropy offers a robust alternative. The primary benefit of entropy-based methods at SLM scale is not to save computation but to allocate it intelligently—identifying hard queries and forwarding them to stronger expert models. High-quality expert models matter more for routing performance than staying within the same architecture family. This reframes how entropy-based uncertainty should be used with SLMs: as a mechanism for smart compute allocation rather than lightweight confidence estimation.

Abstract: We explore whether entropy-based confidence signals can be leveraged to improve the accuracy of Small Language Models (SLMs) with fewer than 3 billion parameters, running entirely on consumer hardware. We evaluate seven distinct approaches, including token-level entropy early stopping, semantic entropy estimation, and uncertainty-aware routing to larger expert models, across 7 model pairs and 5 standard NLU benchmarks. Our key finding is that token-level entropy is effectively blind in SLMs: in 91% of dataset-model combinations, mean token entropy is near zero regardless of answer correctness, rendering token-based confidence signals unusable at this scale. We demonstrate that semantic entropy, computed by generating multiple samples, clustering answers by meaning, and measuring distributional uncertainty, recovers a viable confidence signal. Using semantic entropy to selectively route uncertain queries to a larger expert model yields accuracy improvements of up to +50 percentage points. Notably, cross-family routing (e.g., SmolLM 360M to Phi-3.5-mini) averages +22.0% improvement compared to +6.8% for same-family routing, revealing that expert model quality matters more than architectural compatibility. Our results suggest that the value proposition for entropy-based methods in SLMs is not computational savings but intelligent compute allocation: spending more tokens where they matter most.

</details>


### [2] [HERMES: Contrast-Aware Knowledge Graph Reasoning from Clinical Notes for Patient Outcome Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20825)
*Gia-Bach Nguyen,Hoang-Ha Nguyen,Tuan-Cuong Vuong,Trang Mai Xuan,Duy Quoc Ngo,Tien-Cuong Nguyen,Huan Vu,Thien Van Luong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: HERMES is a graph-based framework that turns clinical notes into patient-specific knowledge graphs and uses graph attention networks to predict outcomes, outperforming text-only models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing clinical predictive models mainly use structured EHR data or treat clinical notes as flat text, which fails to capture the rich relational and temporal information naturally expressed in narratives. There is a need to exploit these relationships—such as how diagnoses, treatments, and outcomes evolve over time—to improve prediction tasks like mortality and readmission.

Method: The paper introduces HERMES, which first constructs personalized clinical Knowledge Graphs from unstructured notes via LLM-guided information extraction combined with Contrastive Logic Modeling, explicitly encoding temporal dynamics, treatment failures, and outcome changes. Then, a Graph Attention Network operates over these KGs to produce patient-level representations used for prediction tasks. The approach is evaluated on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV for in-hospital mortality and 30-day readmission.

Result: On both MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV, HERMES consistently outperforms strong baselines that rely only on text representations without explicit relational or temporal modeling for in-hospital mortality and 30-day readmission prediction.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling clinical narratives as knowledge graphs, enriched with temporal and outcome-related logic via Contrastive Logic Modeling, leads to better patient risk prediction than treating notes as flat text. This suggests that relational and temporal structure in clinical notes is a valuable signal for predictive modeling.

Abstract: Clinical predictive models often rely on structured Electronic Health Record data, such as time-series and procedure codes. While recent approaches have begun leveraging unstructured clinical notes, they typically encode them as flat sequences, which may lose explicit relational and temporal structure present in clinical narratives. In response, we propose HERMES, a graph-based framework that operates exclusively on clinical text while preserving clinical relationships. This approach builds on two key ideas. First, personalized Knowledge Graphs (KGs) are constructed through Large-Language-Model-guided extraction from clinical notes with Contrastive Logic Modeling that explicitly captures temporal dynamics and treatment failures and changes in outcomes. Second, a Graph Attention Network synthesizes patient representations through graph-based learning over the KGs. Experiments on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV for in-hospital mortality and 30-day readmission prediction show that HERMES consistently outperforms strong text-only baselines. Our findings demonstrate that explicit relational modeling with Contrastive Logic Modeling significantly advances predictive performance.

</details>


### [3] [TALON: A Temporally Aware Longitudinal Framework for Radiology Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20826)
*Nien-Tsyr Sun,Min-Chen Chen,Hui Nien Hung,Vincent S. Tseng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces TALON, a radiology report generation framework that better uses patients’ longitudinal imaging histories to produce more accurate, clinically meaningful reports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing radiology report generation systems largely rely on a single current exam or, at best, one most recent prior exam. This restricts their ability to capture subtle interval changes and make robust longitudinal comparisons, which are central to real-world radiology practice. Even newer models that use multiple prior exams typically employ a fixed-length history and do not explicitly model how relevant each prior exam is for the current reporting task, leading to suboptimal use of longitudinal information and potential redundancy.

Method: The authors propose TALON, a Temporally Aware Longitudinal RRG framework that can ingest variable-length sequences of prior exams. Its core component is the Dual-Channel Temporal Fusion Module (DCTFM), which compares the current exam with each prior exam via two complementary channels: a similarity channel to capture persistent findings and a change channel to capture interval changes. A channel-specific attention mechanism estimates how relevant each prior exam is within each channel, and a learned prior-specific gating mechanism adaptively fuses longitudinal evidence while suppressing redundant or unhelpful priors.

Result: On the MIMIC-CXR dataset, TALON achieves better performance than the current state-of-the-art methods across multiple evaluation dimensions, including clinical efficacy metrics and graph-based measures that capture the correctness and structure of reported findings. The model’s performance further improves as more prior examinations are made available, indicating that TALON effectively leverages longer and more complex longitudinal histories.

Conclusion: TALON successfully addresses limitations of prior RRG models by explicitly and adaptively modeling the relevance of multiple prior examinations through its dual-channel temporal fusion and attention/gating mechanisms. Its superior performance, especially with longer exam histories, demonstrates its strength for longitudinal radiology report generation, making it more aligned with real-world clinical workflows where trends and interval changes are critical.

Abstract: Current radiology report generation (RRG) models usually produce descriptive reports based on a single examination or only the most recent prior examination, limiting their ability to perform accurate and meaningful longitudinal comparisons and detect subtle interval changes. Although recent approaches have begun to incorporate multiple prior examinations, they usually aggregate a fixed-length history without explicitly modeling the role-dependent relevance of each prior examination before fusion. To address this, we propose TALON, a Temporally Aware LONgitudinal RRG framework that adaptively integrates variable-length patient histories. The underlying Dual-Channel Temporal Fusion Module (DCTFM) compares the current examination with each prior examination through complementary similarity and change channels to capture persistent findings and interval changes, respectively. The specially designed channel-specific attention estimates the relevance of each prior examination, while a learned prior-specific gate adaptively integrates informative longitudinal evidence and suppresses redundancy. Experiments on MIMIC-CXR show that TALON outperforms the current state-of-the-art method on various clinical efficacy and graph-based metrics. When more prior examinations become available, TALON's performance on these metrics improves even further, emphasizing the strength of TALON's DCTFM in modeling longitudinal RRG across longer and more complex patient histories than existing approaches.

</details>


### [4] [From Discharge Notes to Patient Understanding: Persona-Grounded, Open-Ended Simulation of LLMs as Discharge Educators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20827)
*Won Seok Jang,Zonghai Yao,Hong Yu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build a realistic, persona-based benchmark (DischargeBench) to evaluate how well LLMs conduct multi-turn hospital discharge education with patients, focusing on patient understanding rather than just text quality or single-turn accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hospital discharge education is crucial and highly interactive: clinicians must adapt explanations and instructions to each patient’s literacy, recall ability, and personality. Existing LLM benchmarks mostly assess static or one-shot tasks like summarization or question answering, and do not capture how well a model can guide a patient through an open-ended, multi-turn discharge conversation or ensure that the patient actually understands their care plan. The authors want a way to evaluate LLMs specifically as discharge educators, in a realistic, patient-centered manner that reflects real clinical variability and challenges.

Method: They create DischargeBench, a simulation where an LLM under evaluation plays the “educator” in a multi-turn conversation with a Virtual Patient. This Virtual Patient has a detailed persona defined along several axes (personality traits, education level, health literacy, and ability to recall past medical history), allowing stratified analysis of performance. An additional Education Monitor Agent controls and maintains the realism and consistency of the patient’s behavior/persona but does not interfere with or adapt the educator model, preserving an unbiased evaluation signal. They also construct a dataset, MIMIC-IV-Ext-DischargeBench, containing 477 discharge cases covering 24 ICD diagnostic chapters, each paired with personas. Each simulated session is evaluated along four dimensions: Conversation Quality, coverage of a Topic Checklist, Patient Comprehension, and Factual Consistency. Scoring is done by an LLM-as-a-Judge system calibrated against physician annotations to align with clinical expectations.

Result: Running both closed-source and open-source LLMs on DischargeBench, the authors find that overall average scores can look acceptable but hide important clinically relevant differences. Performance varies across ICD chapters and across different patient personas. More challenging personas (e.g., low health literacy, poor recall, or difficult personality traits) highlight where models fail to cover key topics, leave the patient with poor understanding, or diverge from the source discharge information, revealing coverage failures, comprehension gaps, and lower agreement with reference answers.

Conclusion: DischargeBench demonstrates that evaluating LLMs for hospital discharge education requires patient-centric, interactive simulations and metrics that directly measure patient understanding, not merely text fluency or answer accuracy. Persona-grounded, multi-turn evaluations uncover safety-critical weaknesses that aggregate scores and traditional benchmarks miss. Future work on LLMs for clinical discharge counseling should prioritize robust performance across diverse patient personas and conditions, with evaluation frameworks centered on comprehension and clinically relevant coverage.

Abstract: Hospital discharge education is an interactive teaching task: a clinician adapts a discharge plan to a patient's literacy, recall, and personality. Existing LLM evaluations target static or artifact-generation tasks and do not measure patient understanding under open-ended dialogue. We introduce DischargeBench, a persona-grounded simulation in which a candidate LLM educator conducts a multi-turn session with a Virtual Patient, while an Education Monitor Agent regulates patient realism without modifying the educator, protecting the evaluation signal. We curate MIMIC-IV-Ext-DischargeBench, 477 cases over 24 ICD chapters with persona axes (personality, education level, health literacy, past-medical-history recall) for stratified analysis. Each simulation is scored on four axes -- Conversation Quality, Topic Checklist, Comprehension, and Factual Consistency -- by an LLM-as-a-Judge aligned against physician annotations. Across closed- and open-source LLMs, aggregate scores conceal clinically relevant variation across ICD chapters and patient personas; difficult personas expose coverage failures, comprehension gaps, and reduced source-answer agreement. LLM evaluation for discharge education should center patient understanding, not text quality or answer accuracy alone.

</details>


### [5] [Beyond WER: Entity and Disfluency Recall in Accented Conversational ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20828)
*Fiza Husain,Ankit Pandey,Yash Singh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a three-stage pipeline that improves ASR performance on named entities and filled pauses for accented conversational English, achieving higher entity and filler recall than baselines while keeping WER low.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional ASR systems optimized for WER often fail to capture named entities and filled pauses, especially in accented conversational English. These elements are important for language-learning feedback, where correct recognition of names, places, and hesitations can influence pedagogical feedback, error diagnosis, and learner modeling. There is a gap in ASR performance for speakers from underrepresented regions such as India, Indonesia, and Latin America, particularly on these linguistically important categories, motivating a tailored approach to data curation, modeling, and evaluation.

Method: The authors design a three-stage pipeline: (1) They curate training data using heuristic SQL filters to select utterances rich in named entities and fillers, yielding a dataset with 2.8x the entity density compared to random sampling. (2) They fine-tune regional LoRA adapters on Qwen2.5-Omni-3B, creating region-specific models that generate both verbatim and corrected transcripts in a single forward pass, thus supporting ASR and grammatical correction jointly. (3) They develop a six-category error taxonomy for evaluating ASR and correction errors and validate the taxonomy with an LLM-based judge whose labels agree with human annotations at 83.8% on a 210-sample set. They also use paired bootstrap testing to isolate the impact of data curation on entity recall gains.

Result: The pipeline achieves substantial improvements on a 6,000-utterance test set from speakers in India, Indonesia, and Latin America. Entity recall improves from 53–55% to 80–85%, and filler (filled pause) recall improves from under 5% to 76–86%, while maintaining a low WER of 6–10%. The proposed system outperforms Whisper and a commercial ASR system on entity recall and matches the entity performance of a much larger 30B-parameter zero-shot model, despite using 10x fewer parameters. Paired bootstrap tests indicate that the data curation step alone contributes 2.8–4.2 percentage points of the entity recall improvement, with high statistical significance (p<0.0001).

Conclusion: The study concludes that targeted data curation combined with lightweight, region-specific adaptation can dramatically improve ASR recognition of named entities and filled pauses in accented conversational English without sacrificing overall WER. This approach provides language-learning applications with richer, more pedagogically useful transcripts while remaining computationally efficient, rivaling much larger models. The validated error taxonomy and LLM-based judging framework offer a robust methodology for evaluating and iterating on ASR systems focused on fine-grained linguistic features.

Abstract: ASR systems optimised for Word Error Rate (WER) often miss named entities and filled pauses in accented conversational English, both critical for language-learning feedback. We present a three-stage pipeline for speakers from India, Indonesia, and Latin America: (1) heuristic SQL filters curating entity-rich training data at 2.8x the entity density of random sampling, (2) regional LoRA adapters fine-tuned on Qwen2.5-Omni-3B producing both verbatim and corrected transcripts in a single forward pass, and (3) a six-category error taxonomy validated by an LLM-based judge (83.8% agreement, 210 human-labelled samples). The pipeline achieves 80-85% entity recall (up from 53-55%), 76-86% filler recall (up from <5%), and 6-10% WER across 6k test utterances, outperforming Whisper and a commercial ASR on entity recall while matching a zero-shot 30B model with 10x fewer parameters. Paired bootstrap tests confirm that curation alone accounts for 2.8-4.2 pp of entity recall gain (p<0.0001).

</details>


### [6] [SAGE: Schema-Guided LLMs for Grant Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20829)
*Erik Varapaev,Andrei Chetvergov,Stepan Ukolov,Timofei Sivoraksha,Alexander Evseev,Sergey Bolovtsov*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces SAGE, a system that turns grant review rubrics into structured, evidence-linked evaluations and shows it can align reasonably well with expert reviewers and help them review more accurately and consistently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grant reviewers must process complex applications using detailed rubrics and produce transparent, inspectable assessments. This is time-consuming, cognitively demanding, and can lead to inconsistency and limited auditability of decisions. There is a need for tools that can systematically apply rubric criteria, ground judgments in application evidence, and produce outputs that experts can easily inspect, correct, and audit.

Method: The authors design SAGE (Schema-Guided Aspect-Based Grant Evaluation), which encodes a grant rubric as a schema of structured checks and has an AI system generate criterion-level judgments explicitly linked to evidence from the application package. They evaluate SAGE on 35 nonprofit grant applications in two stages: (1) post-hoc comparison against 105 original human reviews from a real grant competition, measuring ordinal agreement (kappa); (2) a prospective, assisted review round where foundation staff re-review criteria after inspecting SAGE’s structured outputs, yielding 202 assessments. They compare SAGE’s performance to a simpler one-prompt-per-criterion LLM baseline and also run a fine-grained claim-level audit of SAGE’s structured drafts to categorize claims as confirmed, disputed, or unaddressed by experts.

Result: In the post-factum comparison to 105 original human reviews, SAGE achieves fair ordinal agreement with reviewers (kappa = 0.29). In the assisted re-review round, where experts see and correct SAGE’s outputs, the system achieves substantially higher agreement (kappa = 0.58) and outperforms a naive one-prompt-per-criterion baseline (kappa = 0.33 on the overlapping subset of criteria), with better rank correlation and lower error. The claim-level audit reveals which parts of SAGE’s structured outputs are confirmed, contested, or missing relative to expert judgments, demonstrating that its outputs are suitable as auditable drafts rather than final decisions.

Conclusion: SAGE demonstrates that grant review rubrics can be operationalized as structured, evidence-linked schemas that an AI system can use to produce detailed draft evaluations. While SAGE alone does not fully match expert reviewers, its use as an assistive tool significantly improves agreement and consistency compared to both historical reviews and simpler LLM prompting. The system’s design supports transparency and auditability by tying each judgment to specific evidence and enabling claim-level inspection and correction, suggesting a practical path for AI-augmented, expert-in-the-loop grant evaluation workflows.

Abstract: Grant reviewers must apply detailed criteria to application forms, budgets, and supporting documents while producing assessments that colleagues can inspect. We present SAGE, Schema-Guided Aspect-Based Grant Evaluation, a system that translates a grant rubric into structured checks and links its judgements to evidence from the application package. We evaluate SAGE in two stages on 35 nonprofit grant applications. A post-factum comparison with 105 reviews from the original competition shows fair ordinal agreement (kappa = 0.29). The foundation then conducted a criterion-level re-review after inspecting SAGE, producing 202 assessments. In this assisted round, SAGE reached kappa = 0.58 and outperformed a one-prompt-per-criterion baseline (kappa = 0.33 on the common subset), with higher rank correlation and lower error. A claim-level audit further identifies confirmed, disputed, and unaddressed parts of the structured draft. SAGE operationalizes the review methodology by producing a detailed, evidence-linked, and auditable draft for expert correction.

</details>


### [7] [Reviser: Revision-Capable Text Generation via Autoregressive Cursor Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20830)
*Sean Diab*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Reviser, a decoder-only Transformer that performs non-monotonic text generation by producing cursor-relative edit actions instead of tokens in final text order, achieving competitive quality with lower inference compute than existing non-autoregressive and refinement-style baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many existing non-autoregressive and edit-based text generation systems gain flexibility (like inserting or revising earlier content) by repeatedly re-processing entire sequences, which is computationally expensive and undermines efficiency. The authors want a model that genuinely supports non-monotonic generation—being able to move backward in the text and insert in the middle—while keeping inference efficient and the decoding interface simple, and still matching the quality of strong autoregressive baselines.

Method: They propose Reviser, a decoder-only Transformer that operates on a mutable text canvas with a cursor. Instead of directly emitting final text tokens in left-to-right order, it autoregressively emits edit actions over the history of edits: INSERT(token) to place a token at the cursor, MOVE(Δ) to move the cursor backward or forward by an offset, and STOP to finish generation. The model is trained and evaluated as an autoregressive policy over these actions, enabling non-monotonic generation (e.g., mid-canvas insertions and backward moves) in a single pass without sequence-level re-encoding. They compare Reviser to edit-based and diffusion-style baselines (SEDD, MDLM, multi-pass refinement) and size-matched autoregressive Transformers under a shared FLOPs accounting for fairness.

Result: On a continuation benchmark, human or arena-style evaluations show that Reviser is strongly preferred to SEDD and MDLM, indicating better continuation quality. Analysis of the generated trajectories reveals that Reviser frequently moves the cursor backward and performs insertions in the middle of the canvas, demonstrating that it truly uses non-monotonic editing rather than simply appending tokens at the end. When compared to size-matched standard autoregressive models at 100M and 300M parameters, Reviser achieves competitive performance. Under a shared FLOPs convention, Reviser also uses significantly less inference compute than multi-pass refinement and diffusion-style non-autoregressive baselines, despite similar or better quality.

Conclusion: Reviser shows that it is possible to achieve genuinely non-monotonic, edit-based text generation in a single-pass, decoder-only Transformer by autoregressing over edit actions (INSERT, MOVE, STOP) on a canvas, rather than over final text positions. This yields a model that flexibly revises earlier content, matches the quality of comparably sized autoregressive models, and is more computationally efficient at inference than multi-pass and diffusion-style approaches. The work suggests that action-based decoding with cursor-relative operations is a promising design for controllable and efficient revision-capable language generation.

Abstract: Revision-capable generation is appealing because it can insert or revise earlier content, but many non-autoregressive and edit-based approaches obtain this flexibility through repeated sequence-level computation. We propose Reviser, a decoder-only Transformer that generates a response as a sequence of cursor-relative actions on a mutable canvas. At each step, Reviser predicts exactly one action token: INSERT(token), MOVE($Δ$), or STOP, and is autoregressive over edit-history actions rather than final text order. This design enables genuinely non-monotonic generation while preserving a simple next-action interface. On a continuation benchmark, Reviser is strongly preferred to SEDD and MDLM in our arena evaluations, and trajectory statistics confirm that the model performs frequent backward moves and mid-canvas insertions rather than merely emulating end-append decoding. Against size-matched autoregressive baselines, Reviser is competitive at both the 100M and 300M scales. Under our shared FLOPs convention, Reviser also requires substantially less inference compute than representative multi-pass refinement and diffusion-style baselines.

</details>


### [8] [Recursive Language Models Generalize Out of Domain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20831)
*Chenxiao Yang,Zhiyuan Li,David McAllester,Nathan Srebro*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes when restricting a language model’s available context (via recursion) can improve learning compared to standard Chain-of-Thought (CoT) that sees the full reasoning trace.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Chain-of-Thought models can access the full context, which is very general and expressive, but may let the model exploit spurious shortcuts based on irrelevant parts of the context. The authors want to understand theoretically and empirically when limiting context—by forcing the model to solve subtasks in isolation with recursive composition—can actually lead to better, more robust reasoning, especially out-of-distribution.

Method: They compare two learning paradigms: (1) standard CoT models that read and condition on a full reasoning trace, and (2) recursive language models that decompose problems into subtasks, each solved in an isolated context, then combined. Using learning-theoretic analysis, they study how these classes differ in terms of what rules they can represent, their IID generalization guarantees, and how inductive biases (simplicity bias) influence which rules are actually learned. They focus particularly on the difference between in-distribution (IID) and out-of-distribution behavior.

Result: In IID settings, CoT is strictly more general and can simulate the recursive rule efficiently, yielding similar generalization guarantees up to a constant factor; recursion provides little advantage there. However, out-of-distribution, CoT’s access to the full context allows it to learn shortcut rules that exploit tokens outside each subtask, which fit the training data but fail when those contextual tokens change. Recursive models, by enforcing context isolation for each subtask, prevent this shortcut failure mode and thus can generalize better in these settings.

Conclusion: Simply having a hypothesis class that includes the correct, recursively compositional rule is not sufficient for robust reasoning: due to simplicity bias, a general CoT learner tends to prefer easier shortcut rules over the true recursive structure. Therefore, to move beyond mere distributional accuracy toward genuine reasoning, we must not only ensure that the right rule is representable but also constrain the learning process—e.g., via recursive context isolation—so that the learner is steered away from shortcuts. This diverges from conclusions suggested by classical learning theory, where coverage of the target rule is often considered enough.

Abstract: We study when limiting what a language model can see improves learning. We compare standard CoT, the more general learner that reads the full trace, with recursive language models, which restricts itself by solving each subtask in an isolated context. In-distribution, this generality comes for free: CoT can efficiently simulate the recursive rule, so the IID generalization guarantee changes only by a constant factor, and recursion does not offer much. But out of domain, CoT can fit training by relying on context outside the current subtask, i.e. a shortcut that breaks once those tokens change; recursive context isolation rules out this failure mode. Even though CoT's class still covers the recursive rule, simplicity bias picks the shortcut over the truth. Thus, to go beyond distributional accuracy and truly reason, covering the right rule is not enough; this contrasts with classical learning theory.

</details>


### [9] [TatBLiMP: A Benchmark of Linguistic Minimal Pairs for Tatar](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20832)
*Ilshat Saetov,Dmitry Gaynullin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TatBLiMP is a minimal-pair grammaticality benchmark for Tatar language models, covering 16 morphosyntactic phenomena via 1,248 attested/perturbed sentence pairs to evaluate whether models prefer grammatical over minimally ungrammatical sentences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There was no existing grammaticality benchmark for Tatar, a low-resource Turkic language not included in MultiBLiMP. The authors aim to enable systematic evaluation of Tatar language models, track the effect of focused Tatar training, and highlight gaps between large multilingual models and smaller, Tatar-focused ones.

Method: They construct 1,248 minimal sentence pairs spanning 16 morphosyntactic phenomena (adapted from TurBLiMP plus a Tatar-specific bare-noun number phenomenon). Each pair differs by a single morpheme: one grammatical member taken from Tatar literary prose, and one ungrammatical member generated deterministically using the apertium-tat morphological transducer. Native speakers ratify each pair, and a plausibility principle ensures ungrammatical sentences resemble realistic errors. Models are evaluated in a BLiMP-style forced-choice setup by comparing which member of each pair receives higher probability, without requiring generation or parsing, and can be applied to base models or intermediate checkpoints. The benchmark is used to evaluate from-scratch Tatar models, cross-lingual adaptations, and large multilingual LLMs.

Result: Performance correlates more with dedicated Tatar training than with parameter count. A 478M-parameter from-scratch Tatar model and a 125M monolingual model achieve near 0.97 accuracy, outperforming a 7B adapted model; frontier multilingual LLMs with 30–120B parameters score lower (roughly 0.80–0.92), and a lightly tuned multilingual model performs worst. The benchmark thus reveals that heavy parameter scaling without strong Tatar exposure does not guarantee superior grammaticality judgments in Tatar.

Conclusion: TatBLiMP offers the first focused grammaticality benchmark for Tatar and shows that smaller, well-trained Tatar-specific models can surpass much larger multilingual LLMs on Tatar morphosyntax. However, the current taxonomy, inherited from TurBLiMP, underrepresents morphophonological phenomena such as vowel harmony and consonant assimilation that are highly salient to native speakers. The authors propose adding a second, native-oriented layer to incorporate these aspects in future work.

Abstract: We introduce TatBLiMP, the first benchmark of linguistic minimal pairs for Tatar (tt, ISO 639-3 tat), a Qypchaq Turkic language written in Cyrillic. To our knowledge it is the first grammaticality evaluation for Tatar language models of any kind, since even the 101-language MultiBLiMP does not include Tatar. TatBLiMP covers 16 morphosyntactic phenomena in 1248 sentence pairs. Each pair differs by a single morpheme, one grammatical and one ungrammatical. A model passes a pair when it assigns higher probability to the grammatical member. Scoring compares probabilities the model already assigns, so the benchmark needs no text generation and no parser, and it runs on base models and on mid-training checkpoints. TatBLiMP adapts the phenomenon inventory and single-morpheme breaking operations of TurBLiMP to Tatar and adds one phenomenon specific to Tatar, bare-noun number after numerals and quantifiers. The grammatical member of every pair is an attested sentence from Tatar literary prose. The ungrammatical member is produced by a deterministic single-morpheme perturbation with the apertium-tat transducer. Every pair is ratified by a native speaker. A plausibility principle governs construction, so the ungrammatical member is a plausible real-world error rather than an arbitrary corruption. Across from-scratch Tatar models, cross-lingual adaptations, and frontier multilingual LLMs, the benchmark tracks focused Tatar training rather than parameter scale. A 478M from-scratch model and a 125M monolingual model lead near 0.97, a 7B adaptation trails, frontier LLMs of 30-120B parameters fall to 0.80-0.92, and a lightly tuned multilingual model is weakest. We close with the benchmark's main limitation. Its inherited taxonomy omits the morphophonology, vowel harmony and consonant assimilation, that is most salient to native speakers, and we sketch a native second layer that would add it.

</details>


### [10] [Transsion's Speaker-Attributed Multilingual ASR System for the MLC-SLM 2026 Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20833)
*Zhecheng Ren,Xuanji He,Xiaoxiao Li,Zhichen Han,Gaoyang Dong,Gaosheng Zhang,Minchuan Chen,Fengjie Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Cascaded system for multilingual speaker-attributed transcription that ranked 2nd in MLC-SLM 2026 Task 1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurately transcribing multilingual conversational speech with correct speaker attribution is challenging and important for applications like meeting transcription and voice assistants. Existing systems struggle with robust speaker diarization, long-form multilingual ASR, and precise alignment needed for high-quality speaker-attributed transcripts.

Method: Propose a cascaded framework with three components: (1) a speaker diarization module based on DiariZen that performs local speaker activity estimation and global clustering to generate speaker-homogeneous segments; (2) a long-form multilingual ASR module based on Qwen3-Omni to produce transcriptions, supported by an external CTC-based alignment model that yields word- and character-level timestamps; (3) a fusion module that combines diarization results with timestamped ASR outputs to produce speaker-attributed STM-format transcripts.

Result: On the official MLC-SLM 2026 Task 1 evaluation set, the system achieves a tcpMER of 15.41% and ranks second among all participating teams, indicating strong performance in multilingual speaker-attributed transcription.

Conclusion: A cascaded pipeline combining DiariZen-based diarization, Qwen3-Omni ASR, and CTC-based alignment in a fusion stage is effective for multilingual speaker-attributed transcription, as validated by competitive tcpMER and challenge ranking.

Abstract: This paper presents the Transsion Speech Team submission to Task 1 of the MLC-SLM 2026 Challenge, which focuses on speaker-attributed transcription for multilingual conversational speech. We propose a cascaded framework consisting of three components: a speaker diarization module, a long-form multilingual ASR module, and a speaker-transcription fusion module. The diarization module is built upon DiariZen and produces speaker-homogeneous segments through local speaker activity estimation and global speaker clustering. The ASR module is based on Qwen3-Omni and generates multilingual transcriptions, while an external CTC-based alignment model provides precise word- and character-level timestamps. Finally, the fusion module combines diarization outputs with timestamped transcriptions to generate speaker-attributed STM outputs. Experimental results on the official evaluation set demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework. The submitted system achieves a tcpMER of 15.41% and ranks second among all participating teams.

</details>


### [11] [Towards Secure Cloud-Native Computing: Unveiling Kubernetes Misconfigurations with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20834)
*Mostafa Anouar Ghorab,Mohamed Aymen Saied*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how Large Language Models can help detect Kubernetes misconfigurations, building a taxonomy of issues, benchmarking existing tools, and analyzing which Kubernetes objects are most vulnerable and how severe their misconfigurations are.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cloud-native systems increasingly rely on Kubernetes, but their complexity leads to frequent misconfigurations that harm security and performance. Existing tools only partially address these issues, and there is a need for a structured understanding of misconfiguration types and for more effective detection approaches. The paper is motivated by the need to improve reliability and security in Kubernetes-based infrastructures by better detecting and categorizing misconfigurations.

Method: The authors (1) construct a taxonomy of common Kubernetes misconfiguration types; (2) empirically evaluate state-of-the-art misconfiguration detection tools against this taxonomy to understand their strengths and weaknesses; (3) analyze which Kubernetes objects are most frequently misconfigured and how severe these misconfigurations are; and (4) explore the use of Large Language Models and other advanced machine learning techniques to enhance misconfiguration detection capabilities.

Result: The study produces a structured taxonomy of Kubernetes misconfiguration types, quantitative benchmarks of existing detection tools, and empirical findings about which Kubernetes objects are most prone to misconfiguration and the relative severity of these issues. It also demonstrates that LLM-based techniques can provide new capabilities or improvements in detecting misconfigurations compared to existing tools, though exact performance metrics are not specified in the abstract.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that LLMs are a promising direction for improving Kubernetes misconfiguration detection and that a well-defined taxonomy, combined with empirical evaluation, is essential for understanding current tool limitations. Their findings suggest that focusing on specific high-risk Kubernetes objects and leveraging advanced ML, including LLMs, can enhance both the accuracy and coverage of misconfiguration detection in cloud-native environments.

Abstract: In the rapidly evolving landscape of cloud-native computing, Organizations are increasingly adopting infrastructure models that emphasize scalability, flexibility, and efficiency. Kubernetes has become the de facto standard for orchestrating containerized applications in these environments. However, the inherent complexity of cloud-native ecosystems introduces significant challenges, particularly in the form of misconfigurations that can compromise both security and performance. This study explores the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) in identifying Kubernetes misconfigurations. We introduce a comprehensive taxonomy of common misconfiguration types, offering a structured framework to better understand and categorize these issues. Additionally, we conduct an empirical evaluation of state-of-the-art detection tools to benchmark their effectiveness. Furthermore, we analyze the Kubernetes objects most prone to misconfiguration and evaluate the severity of the identified issues. By leveraging advanced machine learning techniques, including LLMs, we provide novel insights into enhancing misconfiguration detection methodologies.

</details>


### [12] [A Generative Grammar Underlying the Voynich Manuscript, the Pastiche Hypothesis: Evidence from Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20835)
*Nicolas Turenne*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper uses probabilistic and AI-based multimodal analysis to argue that the Voynich Manuscript is a highly structured imitation of a natural language herbal, likely modeled on Mediterranean medieval herbals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To clarify whether the Voynich Manuscript encodes a real natural language or is instead a sophisticated pseudo-language, by going beyond simple statistical tests and incorporating modern probabilistic modeling and image-based comparison with known medieval herbals.

Method: The authors create a new transliteration of the manuscript and apply: (1) position-dependent probabilistic grammars to model word- and letter-level symbol distributions; (2) phonetic decomposition to compare patterns with Indo-European, Semitic, and Asian languages; (3) rare-event detection to study unlikely symbol sequences; and (4) multimodal image-text alignment using large language models to match plant illustrations with known herbal traditions.

Result: They find that Voynich symbols act like letters, but word lengths mimic syllabic languages. Phonetic patterns resemble consonant-heavy languages like Hebrew or Arabic more than Indo-European ones. Probabilistic models reproduce Zipf-like distributions and show very low probability of repeated initial letters, consistent with an engineered but structured system. Image analysis shows strong similarity between Voynich plants and those in Pseudo-Apuleius Mediterranean herbals, suggesting conscious imitation of medieval medicinal manuscripts.

Conclusion: The manuscript appears to be generated by a structured system that imitates key statistical properties of natural language and the visual conventions of medieval herbals, rather than transparently encoding a known language. The study illustrates how combining probabilistic modeling with AI-based multimodal analysis can advance the study of enigmatic historical manuscripts.

Abstract: Background: The Voynich Manuscript is a fifteenth-century codex written in an unknown script whose content remains undeciphered. Previous studies suggest that its statistical properties resemble those of natural languages, while its illustrations - primarily plants - recall medieval herbals.
  Methods: We present a multidisciplinary analysis combining probabilistic modeling, phonetic decomposition, rare-event detection, and multimodal image analysis, based on a newly transliterated corpus. Word- and letter-level distributions are modeled using position-dependent probabilistic grammars, while phonetic patterns are compared across Indo-European, Semitic, and Asian languages. Image-text alignment methods based on large language models are applied to identify potential botanical correspondences.
  Results: The results indicate that Voynich symbols behave as letters rather than syllabic units, while word-length distributions resemble syllabic structures. Phonetic analyses show closer alignment with consonant-heavy languages such as Hebrew or Arabic than with Indo-European languages. Probabilistic modeling reproduces Zipf-like distributions and reveals extremely low probabilities for repeated initial-letter sequences, indicating a structured imitation of natural language. Image analysis suggests strong correspondences between Voynich plant illustrations and those found in Pseudo-Apuleius herbals from the Mediterranean tradition, consistent with an imitation of medieval medicinal books.
  Perspectives: These findings support the hypothesis that the Voynich Manuscript follows a structured generative system combining linguistic regularities and herbal knowledge, and demonstrate the value of integrating probabilistic and AI-assisted approaches in the analysis of historical manuscripts.

</details>


### [13] [PhysioBench: A Unified Benchmark for Physiological Signal Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20836)
*Mengxuan Li,Junfa Chen,Jinze Xia,Yundan Chen,Lixin Fan,Ke Liu,Keyue Shi,Haishuai Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces PhysioBench, a large, unified benchmark for evaluating how well models can answer natural-language questions about physiological signals across many datasets, tasks, and model types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physiological signals (e.g., ECG, EEG, PPG) are used in many clinical and monitoring tasks, but existing foundation models for these signals typically require separate, task-specific adaptation. Natural language could serve as a unified interface to specify varied prediction goals, potentially enabling one model to handle many tasks via instructions. However, there has been no systematic and large-scale evaluation of how well current models can follow natural-language instructions across different physiological modalities and tasks. This lack of a common benchmark hinders progress and comparison in the field.

Method: The authors build PhysioBench, a unified benchmark for physiological signal question answering. They collect and harmonize annotations from 22 public physiological datasets, turning them into 61.4 million question–answer pairs that span 30 different tasks. Each QA pair is grounded in a specific physiological signal segment and linked back to its original annotation. Using this benchmark, they systematically evaluate 21 representative models—including general-purpose large language models, vision–language models, time-series language models, and specialized physiological signal foundation models—under three complementary experimental settings that probe their ability to follow natural-language instructions and perform unified prediction across tasks and modalities.

Result: Across the 21 evaluated models, none achieves consistently strong performance on all physiological signal modalities and tasks. While using natural-language questions makes it possible to set up a unified prediction interface across diverse tasks, model performance is highly sensitive to how these questions are formulated. This indicates existing models have limited and brittle instruction-following capabilities when applied to physiological signals.

Conclusion: PhysioBench provides the first large, unified, and extensible benchmark focused on natural-language question answering over physiological signals. Current models, even strong general-purpose and specialized ones, are far from robust, general performance across tasks and modalities, and are sensitive to prompt wording. PhysioBench thus exposes key limitations of present approaches and offers a standardized platform for detailed analysis, comparison, and future research aimed at improving instruction-following and generalization in physiological signal understanding models.

Abstract: Physiological signals support diverse clinical and monitoring tasks, yet existing physiological signal foundation models typically require task-specific adaptation for each task. Natural language provides a common interface for specifying different prediction objectives, but the ability of current models to follow such instructions across physiological signal modalities remains insufficiently evaluated. To address this gap, we introduce PhysioBench, a unified benchmark for physiological signal question answering. PhysioBench harmonizes annotations from 22 public datasets into 61.4 million questions across 30 tasks. Each question-answer pair is grounded in a signal segment and traceable to its source annotation. We evaluate 21 representative models, including large language models, vision-language models, time-series language models, and physiological signal foundation models under three complementary settings. The results show that none of the evaluated models achieves consistently strong performance across physiological signal modalities and tasks. The incorporation of natural language supports unified prediction across tasks, although performance remains sensitive to question formulation. Beyond these findings, PhysioBench offers an extensible platform for fine-grained analysis and future research on physiological signal understanding. Our codes are available at https://github.com/Leanna97/PhysioBench.

</details>


### [14] [From Generation to Detection: Exploration of Discourse Driven Scenario based LLM Generated Fake News](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20838)
*Zeynep Özdemir,Murat Osmanoğlu,Sevgi Yiğit-Sert,Ömer Özgür Tanrıöver,Yılmaz Ar*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how modern large language models (LLMs) both generate and detect fake news across several controlled manipulation scenarios, finding large variation between models and that refined detection prompts often hurt rather than help performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used in information environments where fake news is a concern. They can both generate convincing misinformation and potentially be used to detect it, but we lack a systematic understanding of how well current models perform in these dual roles and how different prompting strategies affect detectability. This gap makes it hard to assess real-world risks and the reliability of LLM-based detection systems.

Method: The authors define four fake-news manipulation scenarios grounded in a journalistic discourse framework: open-ended generation, rewriting, manipulation prompts, and attribute-based prompts. Using seven widely used LLMs, they generate a synthetic fake-news corpus of 14,000 articles across those scenarios. They then (1) analyze linguistic, structural, and semantic properties of the generated texts versus real news, and (2) run detection experiments where each model is prompted to judge whether news items are fake. Detection starts from a basic prompt and is then iteratively refined using misleading patterns extracted from real–fake news pairs, and performance is compared across models and scenarios.

Result: (1) There is substantial variation between LLMs in both the quality and style of fake news they generate and in their ability to detect fake news. (2) The strategy used to generate fake news has a strong impact on how detectable it is—some manipulation scenarios yield fake news that models detect relatively well, while others make detection much harder. (3) Contrary to expectation, refined detection prompts built from learned misleading patterns generally do not improve detection accuracy and often reduce it.

Conclusion: LLMs exhibit heterogeneous and sometimes limited capability in detecting fake news generated by themselves and other models, and the nature of the generation scenario critically shapes detectability. Naively refining prompts with pattern-based guidance is not a reliable way to enhance detection and can even be detrimental. The study offers a systematic benchmark and cautions against over-reliance on prompt engineering alone for robust LLM-based fake-news detection.

Abstract: In this study, we examine how modern LLMs generate and detect fake news under controlled settings across four manipulation scenarios. These are open-ended generation, rewriting, manipulation prompts and attribute based prompts grounded in the journalistic discourse framework. Firstly, using seven widely adapted models, we created a synthetic fake news corpus with 14000 generated articles across these four scenarios. Then we analyzed its linguistic properties to assess how closely model-generated news resembles real news structurally and semantically. Finally, to evaluate detection performance, we conducted experiments where each model judges generated fake news, starting with a basic detection prompt and improved prompts developed through an iterative refinement process that extracts misleading patterns from real-fake pairs. Our results revealed substantial variation across models in both generating and detecting misinformation, demonstrated that the generation strategy strongly influences detectability, and show that the refined prompt does not improve and often harms detection performance. Therefore, the study provides a systematic assessment of LLMs detection capability of LLMs generated fake news across typical generation scenarios.

</details>


### [15] [Curriculum-Based Noise Adaptation for Phoneme-to-Text Reconstruction in Visual Speech Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20839)
*Matthew Kit Khinn Teng,Haibo Zhang,Takeshi Saitoh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a curriculum learning framework (PECT) to make phoneme-to-text reconstruction more robust for phoneme-centric visual speech recognition, by progressively training on increasingly realistic phoneme error patterns, leading to consistent WER reductions on LRS2/LRS3 across multiple visual speech frontends.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In phoneme-centric visual speech recognition, the final text output depends heavily on the phoneme-to-text reconstruction model. Existing reconstruction models are usually trained on clean or simply corrupted phoneme sequences, which do not match the complex, realistic errors produced by phoneme predictors at inference time. This train–test mismatch degrades robustness and limits overall system performance. The authors are motivated to design a training strategy that better reflects realistic phoneme prediction errors and thereby improves robustness without sacrificing accuracy.

Method: They introduce Progressive Error Curriculum Training (PECT), a curriculum learning framework for training an NLLB-based phoneme-to-text reconstruction model. PECT gradually increases the realism of errors seen during training through three main stages: (1) synthetic phoneme perturbations applied to clean sequences; (2) multi-domain pseudo-labels that approximate more realistic phoneme sequences from diverse domains; and (3) target-domain pseudo-labels generated directly by a visual speech recognizer, matching the error distribution at inference. The reconstruction model is progressively adapted across these stages to learn to decode text reliably under increasingly realistic phoneme noise conditions.

Result: On LRS2 and LRS3 benchmarks, PECT yields consistent improvements in word error rate (WER) across several phoneme-based visual speech recognition frontends: V-ASR, PV-ASR, and HP-VSR variants. Notably, PECT reduces WER for HP-VSR-FiLMFuse (L4) from 23.3% to 22.2% on LRS2 and for HP-VSR-ResFiLM from 30.3% to 29.7% on LRS3. Ablation studies and qualitative analyses show that the progressive curriculum design is key to the observed robustness gains.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that PECT is an effective and generalizable curriculum learning strategy for phoneme-to-text reconstruction within phoneme-centric visual speech recognition systems. By gradually exposing the reconstruction model to increasingly realistic phoneme prediction errors, PECT improves robustness and reduces WER across diverse visual speech frontends, addressing the train–test mismatch inherent in prior reconstruction training approaches.

Abstract: Phoneme-centric visual speech recognition reconstructs sentences from intermediate phoneme predictions, making overall recognition performance highly dependent on the robustness of the phoneme-to-text reconstruction model. Existing reconstruction approaches are commonly trained on clean phoneme sequences or synthetically corrupted inputs, leading to a mismatch between training conditions and the realistic phoneme prediction errors encountered during inference. To address this limitation, this paper proposes progressive error curriculum training (PECT). This curriculum learning framework progressively adapts a No Language Left Behind (NLLB)-based phoneme-to-text reconstruction model using synthetic phoneme perturbations, multi-domain pseudo-labels, and target-domain pseudo-labels generated by a visual speech recognizer. By gradually exposing the reconstruction model to increasingly realistic phoneme prediction errors, the proposed framework improves robustness while preserving sentence-reconstruction accuracy. Experiments on the LRS2 and LRS3 benchmarks demonstrate that PECT consistently improves reconstruction performance across multiple phoneme-based visual speech recognition frontends, including visual automatic speech recognition (V-ASR), point visual automatic speech recognition (PV-ASR), and head-pose-aware visual speech recognition (HP-VSR) variants. In particular, PECT reduces the word error rate (WER) of HP-VSR-FiLMFuse (L4) from 23.3% to 22.2% on LRS2 and reduces the WER of HP-VSR-ResFiLM from 30.3% to 29.7% on LRS3. Comprehensive ablation studies and qualitative analyses further demonstrate the effectiveness of progressively adapting the reconstruction model to realistic phoneme prediction errors. These results show that PECT provides an effective and generalizable curriculum learning strategy for phoneme-to-text reconstruction in phoneme-centric visual speech recognition.

</details>


### [16] [COAL-SQL: Coverage-Guided Augmentation and Failure-Driven Learning for Text-to-SQL Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20842)
*Qifeng Cai,Xuanguang Pan,Hao Liang,Chang Xu,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: COAL-SQL is a post-training framework for open-source LLMs on Text-to-SQL that improves SQL structural coverage and addresses failure cases via coverage-guided augmentation and failure-driven learning, achieving strong performance with a small number of examples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-source LLMs still struggle with complex, real-world Text-to-SQL tasks unless they receive tailored post-training. Existing datasets and augmentation strategies do not systematically cover all needed SQL structures, and pure SFT or RL cannot adaptively fix weaknesses exposed during training. The paper aims to create a unified framework that both fills structural coverage gaps in training data and dynamically focuses learning on the model’s failure modes, all under realistic data/compute constraints.

Method: The authors introduce COAL-SQL, which combines: (1) Coverage-Guided Augmentation (CGA): it analyzes the original corpus to detect missing SQL structures and greedily selects and constructs complementary examples that cover those structural gaps; and (2) Failure-Driven Learning (FDL): training is primarily optimized with GRPO-style RL, but for examples the model fails, it adds targeted supervision. At the step level, it applies SFT on trusted reasoning traces produced by a stronger LLM for those failed cases. At the epoch level, it retrieves structurally similar examples to the accumulated failures to form focused practice batches, teaching the model the missing SQL capabilities more effectively.

Result: Using only 12,600 distinct post-training examples, models trained with COAL-SQL reach 64.9% execution accuracy on the BIRD development set and surpass other methods trained with comparable scales of data and compute. This shows that the framework can deliver substantial Text-to-SQL performance gains with relatively modest post-training resources.

Conclusion: Systematically improving structural coverage of SQL and coupling RL optimization with failure-targeted supervision yields more efficient and effective post-training for Text-to-SQL LLMs. COAL-SQL demonstrates that a combination of coverage-guided data augmentation and failure-driven learning can significantly boost execution accuracy on a challenging benchmark using limited data, suggesting a practical path to upgrading open-source models for complex, real-world SQL generation.

Abstract: Text-to-SQL translates natural-language questions into executable SQL queries, but open-source large language models still require task-specific post-training for complex, real-world SQL generation. Effective post-training requires both training data that cover the capabilities demanded by the target task and a learning strategy that enables the model to acquire them. Existing datasets provide valuable supervision but incompletely cover SQL structures, while augmentation methods typically expand data without identifying structural gaps. Moreover, supervised fine-tuning (SFT) or reinforcement learning (RL) alone cannot dynamically address weaknesses exposed during training. We propose COAL-SQL, a unified framework combining Coverage-Guided Augmentation (CGA) and Failure-Driven Learning (FDL). CGA uses greedy selection to identify SQL structures missing from the original dataset and constructs complementary examples, improving structural coverage. FDL retains GRPO as the main optimization objective while supplying targeted supervision for unsolved examples. At the step level, it applies SFT to verified reasoning traces generated by a strong LLM for accumulated failures. At the epoch level, it retrieves structurally related examples based on accumulated failures to create targeted practice, helping the model acquire the corresponding SQL capabilities. With only 12,600 distinct post-training examples, COAL-SQL achieves 64.9% execution accuracy on the BIRD development set and outperforms baselines trained at comparable scale. The code is available at https://github.com/TechNomad-ds/COAL-SQL.

</details>


### [17] [VISPATH: Visual-Intent-Guided Path Reasoning for Multimodal Knowledge Graph Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20843)
*Jinke Wu,Zhengpin Li,Mengzhe Jia,Yang Li,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: VISPATH is a multimodal, visual-intent-guided path reasoning framework for knowledge graph question answering that uses visual and textual cues at every hop of multi-hop KG reasoning, with a new benchmark (VISPATH-Bench) showing it outperforms strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal KGQA methods only exploit multimodal information (e.g., images) for initial entity grounding or retrieval, after which reasoning over the knowledge graph becomes purely text-based. This design fails to leverage multimodal cues that are crucial at intermediate reasoning steps, leading to suboptimal multi-hop reasoning performance. There is also a lack of benchmarks specifically targeting multimodal multi-hop KG reasoning over 2–4 hops.

Method: VISPATH introduces a visual-intent-guided path reasoning framework. (1) It identifies a reliable starting entity by combining multimodal grounding with graph-structural signals. (2) It conducts intent-guided path discovery: at each hop it recomputes a hop-specific multimodal intent vector from the question, the input modalities, and the current partial reasoning path, so that graph expansion is conditioned on the current reasoning state. (3) It applies reasoning-chain pruning, evaluating full candidate paths as complete evidence chains using their consistency with the question, a reasoning sketch, and hop-specific intents. (4) It includes an evidence sufficiency check before answer generation. Additionally, the authors construct VISPATH-Bench, a dataset of KG questions requiring 2–4 hop multimodal reasoning.

Result: On VISPATH-Bench and three existing multimodal QA benchmarks, VISPATH consistently surpasses strong baselines. Using GPT-4o as the underlying model, VISPATH outperforms a stronger baseline denoted GPT-5.4 on VISPATH-Bench, yielding a 10.6% relative gain in average accuracy and a 13.1% gain on 2-hop reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: Incorporating hop-specific multimodal intent into each step of KG path reasoning significantly improves multimodal multi-hop question answering. VISPATH demonstrates that multimodal cues should guide not only initial grounding but also intermediate hops, and the newly proposed VISPATH-Bench provides a focused evaluation of 2–4 hop multimodal KG reasoning. The framework sets a new state of the art on several MM-KGQA benchmarks and highlights the importance of full-path, intent-aware reasoning and evidence sufficiency checking for robust answer generation.

Abstract: Knowledge graph question answering (KGQA) enables models to answer natural-language questions through structured graph reasoning and has achieved substantial progress across many benchmarks and applications. Recently, multimodal KGQA (MM-KGQA) has attracted increasing attention because many questions require jointly using multimodal inputs and KG evidence. However, existing MM-KGQA methods typically use multimodal information only for starting entity grounding or evidence retrieval, after which multi-hop reasoning degenerates into text-only graph search. As a result, they cannot exploit multimodal cues that become important at intermediate hops. To address this limitation, we propose VISPATH, a visual-intent-guided path reasoning framework for MM-KGQA. VISPATH first identifies a reliable starting entity by combining multimodal grounding with graph-structural cues. It then performs intent-guided path discovery by recomputing hop-specific multimodal intent from the input, question, and current partial paths, so that each expansion is guided by the current reasoning state. The discovered paths are further refined through reasoning-chain pruning, which evaluates candidate paths as complete evidence chains based on their consistency with the question, reasoning sketch, and hop-specific intent. Finally, VISPATH checks whether the selected evidence is sufficient for answer generation. We further construct VISPATH-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating multimodal multi-hop reasoning over KGs, covering questions that require two to four hops over KG paths. Extensive experiments on VISPATH-Bench and three additional multimodal QA benchmarks show that VISPATH consistently outperforms strong baselines. Notably, with GPT-4o as the backbone, VISPATH surpasses GPT-5.4 on VISPATH-Bench, achieving a 10.6% relative improvement in average accuracy and a 13.1% improvement at 2-hop reasoning.

</details>


### [18] [Boosting Deepresearch and LongContext Ability with Self-Generated Deepresearch Rollouts Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20844)
*Zihan Wang,Hao Wang,Boyuan Jiang,Yiqun Zhang,Shi Feng,Xiaocui Yang,Yiwen Ye,Jianghang Lin,Xiaozhong Ji,Jinghao Lin,Kai Wu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a training pipeline that converts DeepResearch agent rollouts into long-context QA data to explicitly train and improve the model’s long-context understanding, leading to better performance on both deep web research and long-context benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deepresearch agents accumulate very long contexts from multi-step web search and browsing, yet existing DR Agentic RL still leaves many errors due to poor long-context handling, such as hallucinations and failure to integrate evidence across multiple documents. The authors want to remove this bottleneck by directly strengthening the model’s ability to reason over long, multi-document contexts without incurring additional annotation costs.

Method: They introduce DR-to-Long, a data construction method that takes DR-RL trajectories (search histories, visited pages, extracted snippets, and final answers) and replaces the short snippets/summaries with the full content of the linked webpages, creating long multi-document QA examples while preserving the original evidence structure. On top of this, they propose DLD-RL (DR -> LongQA -> DR), a three-stage RL pipeline: (1) run a short DR-RL phase to gather trajectories; (2) transform those into LongQA instances using DR-to-Long and train with LongQA-RL to enhance long-context reasoning; (3) run another full DR-RL phase to re-optimize the agent in the real Deepresearch setting.

Result: DLD-RL achieves a 7.3% improvement over standard DR-RL across three Deepresearch benchmarks and a 13.5% gain on three long-context benchmarks, indicating that explicitly training on DR-derived long-context QA substantially boosts both deep research and long-context performance.

Conclusion: Reusing DR rollouts as long-context QA data is an effective and annotation-free way to improve long-context understanding in Deepresearch agents. The proposed DLD-RL pipeline successfully breaks the long-context bottleneck in DR-RL, yielding consistent gains in both web research tasks and generic long-context benchmarks.

Abstract: Deepresearch (DR) agents interact with real-world web environments through multi-turn search and visit, causing their contexts to grow rapidly over time. We observe that, even after DR Agentic Reinforcement Learning (DR-RL), 61.6% of the model's remaining prediction errors can still be attributed to insufficient long-context understanding, including longcontext hallucination and failures in cross-document evidence integration. It motivates us to further break the bottleneck of DR-RL by strengthening the model's long-context ability. However, effective LongContext training requires more than simply increasing context length. To bridge the data gap, we propose `DR Rollouts to LongContext-QA (DR-to-Long)'. The method repurposes DR-RL trajectories, which naturally contain search histories, visited webpages, evidence snippets, and final-answer supervision. It then replaces the compact snippets and webpage summaries in each trajectory with the full contents of their corresponding URLs, producing substantially longer multi-document contexts while preserving the original evidence relationships. Building on DR-to-Long, we introduce DLD (DR -> LongQA -> DR)-RL. DLD-RL first performs a short DR-RL stage to collect rollout trajectories, which are then converted into LongQA instances at zero annotation cost. The model is subsequently optimized with LongQA-RL to strengthen LongContext ability, followed by full DR-RL to continue improving its DR capability. Experiments show that DLD-RL outperforms standard DR-RL by 7.3% on three Deepresearch benchmarks and improves performance by 13.5% on three long-context benchmarks.

</details>


### [19] [Reading Less While Writing: A Closed-Form Bandwidth Dial for Streaming Multimodal Decoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20845)
*Yasir Mehmood,Kashif Javed*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A new parameterized scheduling method (ZENDAYA) for streaming decoders lets a single model handle both offline and real-time captioning, improving quality and latency trade-offs versus fixed wait-k schedules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional decoders for speech/video-to-text either operate offline (seeing the full input) or use rigid streaming rules like wait-k, which are suboptimal or infeasible for real-time captioning and do not adapt to input length or pace. There is a need for a unified, principled way to control latency while preserving or improving generation quality.

Method: They introduce ZENDAYA, a scheduling policy controlled by a single continuous parameter γ that defines, in closed form, how much of the source input is visible as decoding progresses. The visible source prefix scales with the predicted total input length, so offline and streaming become endpoints of one parametric family. They analytically derive that the mean consumed source fraction per emitted word is approximately 1/(1+γ), giving an interpretable latency/reading budget. They also prove a structural dependency theorem showing that, for any fixed, non-decreasing schedule, generated tokens cannot depend on unseen input, even in asynchronous and unbounded streaming settings.

Result: Experiments across three public datasets (Charades-STA, ActivityNet Captions, LibriHeavy) and two modalities (video and audio) show that a 29M-parameter decoder using ZENDAYA matches or surpasses fixed wait-k-style baselines while consuming less of the source input. Gains are strongest at low latency, where fixed schedules degrade. Streaming METEOR scores improve significantly on all datasets, and the surprising finding is that limiting source visibility can actually improve text quality by preventing attention dilution early in decoding.

Conclusion: ZENDAYA provides a simple, unified, and theoretically grounded schedule for streaming and offline decoding that uses a single scalar parameter γ as a latency and budget control. It not only maintains causal correctness under broad streaming conditions but also empirically improves the quality–latency trade-off over fixed wait-k schedules, demonstrating that appropriately restricting source access can yield better captions than fully or rigidly reading the input.

Abstract: A decoder that turns video or audio into text conventionally consumes the entire input before emitting a word. Offline this is merely more than the task requires; live it is impossible, since a caption cannot wait for a match to end. Streaming systems bolt on a fixed rule such as wait-$k$, which waits for the same number of input tokens before every word, regardless of the input's length or pace.
  We replace the fixed offset with ZENDAYA, a schedule governed by a single continuous parameter $γ$. It makes the visible source prefix a closed-form function of generation progress, scaled by the input's own predicted length, so an ordinary offline decoder and a real-time streaming decoder become two endpoints of one family rather than separate models. The same scalar fixes, in closed form, the mean fraction of source consumed per emitted word, $\bar{E}(γ) \approx 1/(1+γ)$, making it at once a latency dial and an interpretable budget. We prove a structural dependency theorem: under any schedule fixed in advance and non-decreasing, no emitted token can depend on input that has not yet arrived. The guarantee holds for trained and untrained weights alike, and extends to unbounded streams under arbitrary asynchronous arrival.
  The empirical result is counterintuitive: seeing less can produce better text, because a flood of source dilutes attention exactly when the model has the least of its own output to anchor on. Trained from scratch across two modalities and three public corpora (Charades-STA, ActivityNet Captions, LibriHeavy), a compact 29M-parameter decoder matches or beats the fixed schedule while reading less of the source, with the sharpest gains at the lowest latencies, where a fixed offset collapses. Streaming METEOR gains are statistically significant on all three corpora.

</details>


### [20] [Rewarding Efficient Reasoning Improves Abstention on Underspecified Tasks in Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20846)
*Polina Tsvilodub,Max Höth,Michael Franke,Björn Deiseroth,Carina Kauf*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that large reasoning models are inefficient at deciding when to abstain on unanswerable questions, and proposes a new reward (GRPO) that trains models to reason efficiently about abstention, improving both abstention accuracy and chain-of-thought efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern large reasoning models can produce correct answers on many tasks but lack a reliable ability to detect when an input is unanswerable and to abstain. Unlike humans, which limit their reasoning effort on unanswerable tasks, these models tend to generate even longer chains of thought for such cases, wasting computation and still often failing to abstain. The motivation is to better align model behavior with human-like, resource-rational reasoning so that models can recognize missing information, abstain appropriately, and use computational resources more efficiently.

Method: The authors first empirically compare human behavior and LRM behavior on answerable vs. unanswerable tasks, focusing on the length/effort of reasoning. They find that humans cap their effort on unanswerable items while LRMs lengthen their chains of thought. To address this, they design a GRPO (a novel reward function in a reinforcement or preference optimization framework) that explicitly rewards models for efficient reasoning about whether the task is solvable with the given information.
