They fine-tune several 4B-parameter LRMs with this reward, encouraging them to shorten chains of thought when an input appears unanswerable and to abstain instead of hallucinating.

Result: After fine-tuning with the GRPO reward, 4B-parameter models show an average 12.8% improvement in human-like abstention performance. At the same time, their answer quality on solvable tasks is preserved, and their computational efficiency improves substantially: their chains of thought become on average 44% shorter, especially on unanswerable prompts, indicating more resource-rational behavior.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that current large reasoning models are not resource-rational in their treatment of unanswerable tasks: they over-invest in reasoning and still fail to abstain reliably. By introducing a GRPO-based reward that trains models to decide whether tasks are solvable with available information, the authors achieve more human-like abstention behavior and significantly improved efficiency without sacrificing answering performance. This suggests that explicitly modeling and rewarding abstention decisions is a promising direction for making LRMs more practical, robust, and computationally efficient.

Abstract: While modern large reasoning models (LRMs) excel at providing correct answers in many tasks, we provide additional evidence for the observation that they often struggle with a critical capability: knowing when to abstain from answering. We analyze this gap by comparing LRM behavior to results from a human study, revealing that human reasoning effort on unanswerable tasks is upper-bounded by answerable tasks, whereas LRMs waste computational resources by generating longer Chains of Thought (CoTs) on unanswerable than on answerable prompts. To overcome this inefficiency, we take inspiration from a resource-rational perspective on human cognition and introduce a novel GRPO reward that encourages efficient reasoning about whether the task contains all the information needed to solve it. Fine-tuning several 4B LRMs with this reward leads to human-like abstention performance gains (+12.8% on average) while retaining answering capabilities and boosting the models' efficiency (44% shorter CoTs on average).

</details>


### [21] [Reading Anxiety or Reading the Label? Comparing Fine-Tuned and Frontier Models for Anxiety Detection on Social Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20847)
*Cris Huynh,Arlene Pham*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates different approaches to detecting anxiety in Reddit posts, exposes a strong keyword confound, and shows that smaller domain-adapted models can nearly match frontier models while avoiding over-reliance on explicit anxiety terms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Anxiety is prevalent and people often express it online before seeking help, making automatic detection important for early support. Practitioners must choose between expensive frontier APIs, in-house fine-tuned medium models, or traditional classifiers, but current evaluations are biased because many labeled posts literally contain the word “anxiety,” allowing shallow keyword-based methods to appear strong.

Method: The authors compare six model setups: frontier commercial models (zero-shot), fine‑tuned medium-sized LMs (including a 3B LoRA variant), and conventional TF‑IDF-style classifiers, all evaluated under a unified protocol on a held-out Reddit anxiety dataset. They identify that 69.3% of anxiety-labeled posts explicitly mention “anxiety” (or variants) and treat this as a confound. Each model is therefore evaluated twice: once on original text and once with anxiety keywords removed; the performance gap is defined as lexical dependence. They also examine the effect of mental-health-specific pretraining for a 110M-parameter encoder.

Result: A frontier model achieves the best raw F1 for anxiety detection (0.846). A 110M-parameter domain-adapted encoder attains a close F1 of 0.831 without relying on external APIs, and only about 0.7 F1 of this gain is attributable to mental-health domain pretraining. Lexical dependence—the drop in F1 when anxiety words are removed—ranges from 8.6 to 25.4 points across models and does not correlate with overall model strength. Surprisingly, a LoRA fine-tuned 3B model shows the highest keyword dependence, even higher than a TF‑IDF baseline, whereas the frontier zero-shot model is the least keyword-dependent.

Conclusion: Reported performance on this anxiety detection corpus is systematically inflated by a strong keyword confound, particularly for fine-tuned models, so many published scores should be treated as upper bounds. Smaller, domain-adapted encoders can nearly match frontier models’ performance while eliminating API reliance, but evaluations must control for lexical dependence (e.g., via keyword removal) to accurately assess a model’s ability to understand the language of mental health conditions rather than just match explicit condition terms.

Abstract: Anxiety is among the most common mental health conditions, and people often write about it online well before seeking clinical help. Practitioners building detection tools face a concrete choice: call a frontier commercial model, fine-tune a smaller model in-house, or deploy a conventional classifier. We compare six conditions spanning all three on a held-out Reddit test set under a single controlled protocol. We also identify a confound in how this task is evaluated. In the corpus used here, 69.3% of anxiety-labelled posts contain the word "anxiety" or a variant, roughly twice the rate of comparable conditions, so a classifier can score well by keyword matching rather than by modelling the language of the condition. We therefore evaluate every model twice, on original text and with those terms deleted, and report the difference as lexical dependence. A frontier model leads on anxiety F1 (0.846), but a 110M-parameter domain-adapted encoder reaches 0.831 with no external API dependency, and mental-health domain pretraining accounts for only 0.7 of those points. Lexical dependence spans 8.6 to 25.4 points and does not track model capability: the LoRA fine-tuned 3B model is the most keyword-dependent condition tested, above even a TF-IDF classifier, while the frontier zero-shot model is the least. Published figures on this corpus are therefore upper bounds, and the inflation is largest for the fine-tuned models such figures typically report.

</details>


### [22] [Enhancing Audio Reasoning via Semantic Summary Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20849)
*Francesco Bonzi,Pooneh Mousavi,Cem Subakan,Mirco Ravanelli*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose SPARE, a method that aligns a special register token with the final answer’s meaning to fix reasoning gaps in Large Audio Language Models, improving zero-shot reasoning without extra inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Audio Language Models are good at complex QA but often get worse when asked to produce explicit Chain-of-Thought, suggesting that long reasoning distracts them from the original audio. The authors want to understand and mitigate this reasoning gap and maintain strong attention to audio throughout reasoning.

Method: They introduce SPARE, which adds a dedicated register token to the model’s input or internal state. This token is trained to be semantically aligned with the final conclusion by using a cosine similarity loss against a Sentence-BERT embedding of the correct answer. By doing so, the model’s latent space is conditioned with the target semantic goal before it starts generating the reasoning steps. They test this within the SALMONN framework on audio reasoning benchmarks (MMAU, MMAR).

Result: On MMAU and MMAR benchmarks, integrating SPARE into SALMONN leads to better zero-shot reasoning performance compared to baselines. Analysis of attention patterns shows that models with SPARE maintain stronger attention to the audio input earlier in the reasoning process, while incurring no additional inference-time computational cost.

Conclusion: Conditioning LALMs with a semantically aligned register token prior to reasoning helps overcome the reasoning gap that arises from explicit Chain-of-Thought. SPARE improves zero-shot audio reasoning and preserves attention to audio without slowing inference, indicating that shaping the latent goal representation is an effective way to guide multimodal reasoning.

Abstract: Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) perform well on complex question answering but often show a reasoning gap, where explicit Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reduces accuracy compared to direct answers. We hypothesize that long reasoning sequences shift attention away from the audio input. To address this, we propose SPARE (Semantic Prediction for Audio REasoning), which introduces a register token aligned with the final conclusion using a cosine similarity loss with a Sentence-BERT embedding. This conditions the model's latent space with the target semantic goal before reasoning begins. Experiments on MMAU and MMAR with SALMONN show improved zero-shot reasoning and stronger early attention to audio without additional inference cost.

</details>


### [23] [MME-Safety: A Fine-grained Benchmark for Safety Evaluation of MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20850)
*Yueming Lyu,Yilian Shi,Haoxiang Tan,Linzhuang Zou,Qihao Wang,Guihua Yu,Jie Qin,Xin Gao,Chenyang Si,Jing Dong,Caifeng Shan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces MME-Safety, a benchmark to rigorously evaluate and profile safety and robustness of multimodal large language models, with fine-grained, multi-dimensional safety annotations and a hierarchical evaluation framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal LLMs can be more vulnerable than unimodal models because cross-modal inputs (e.g., text+image) can hide harmful intent and bypass standard safety filters. Existing safety benchmarks are too coarse, lack detailed intent and risk annotations, and use simple, one-dimensional metrics, making it hard to thoroughly assess robustness and safety behavior of MLLMs.

Method: The authors construct MME-Safety, a benchmark with a four-dimensional annotation schema that categorizes risk scenarios, harm severity, and modality-specific stealth levels, along with another dimension (e.g., intent or task type). They define and apply a hierarchical evaluation framework that separately measures: (1) baseline response reliability, (2) actual risk exposure (how much harmful content is produced), and (3) the structural integrity of safety and defense behaviors. They then run extensive zero-shot evaluations of 17 state-of-the-art MLLMs on this benchmark, systematically varying cross-modal input configurations and analyzing the impact of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning on safety performance.

Result: The benchmark enables detailed, multi-faceted safety profiling of MLLMs. The experiments show how different models behave under diverse risk scenarios, where they fail, and how cross-modal inputs and CoT reasoning can introduce or exacerbate safety vulnerabilities. The study reveals specific safety weaknesses across models and illuminates patterns in how and when they are more easily jailbroken or induced to produce risky outputs.

Conclusion: MME-Safety demonstrates that current MLLMs have substantial, nuanced safety vulnerabilities that are not captured by existing benchmarks. Cross-modal interactions and CoT reasoning can significantly affect risk exposure. The authors argue that safety alignment for multimodal systems must be reasoning-aware and evaluated with fine-grained, multi-dimensional benchmarks like MME-Safety to ensure robust defenses in real-world multimodal applications.

Abstract: While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) show remarkable advancements, their cross-modal capabilities introduce complex vulnerabilities that easily bypass unimodal filters. Existing benchmarks lack fine-grained intent-related annotations and rely on unidimensional metrics, hindering comprehensive robustness evaluation. To address this, we propose MME-Safety, a rigorously verified benchmark featuring a unique four-dimensional annotation schema that categorizes risk scenarios, harm severity, and modality-specific stealth levels. Furthermore, we introduce a hierarchical evaluation framework to assess fundamental response reliability, actual risk exposure, and the structural integrity of defensive behaviors. Extensive zero-shot evaluations across 17 state-of-the-art MLLMs provide a comprehensive safety profile of current multimodal systems. Our analysis systematically investigates cross-modal input configurations and uncovers safety implications associated with Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. These multifaceted findings underscore the urgent need for robust, reasoning-aware safety alignment in the multimodal landscape.

</details>


### [24] [Generative Artificial Intelligence Chatbots for Motivational Interviewing: A Scoping Review From System Design to Intervention Outcomes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20902)
*Runze Hu,Jingqi Kong,Yang Yang,Yihang Yang,Jingyao Liu,Haizhou Tang,Shanghang Zhang,Zheng Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Scoping review of generative-AI-based motivational interviewing (MI) chatbots finds they can produce MI-consistent, well-liked conversations, but evidence for long-term behavior change and safety practices is still weak and uneven.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivational interviewing is effective for supporting health behavior change, but access to trained human counselors is limited. Generative AI chatbots could scale MI delivery, yet existing evidence about how these systems are designed, how safe and MI-consistent they are, how users experience them, and whether they actually improve health outcomes is scattered and incomplete. A structured overview is needed to clarify what is known, where the gaps are, and how to guide future development and evaluation of GenAI-based MI tools.

Method: The authors conducted a PRISMA-ScR scoping review across nine databases, including studies from January 1, 2015 to June 2, 2026 that used generative AI to create MI chatbot responses or counselor utterances. They screened and selected relevant studies, extracted data with a predefined framework, and synthesized findings descriptively across key dimensions: system design, safety reporting, MI quality, user perceptions, and intervention outcomes.

Result: They identified 47 reports describing 48 studies. About 42% focused on system design without user interaction, while 58% involved direct user use. Most chatbots were text-only and without embodied avatars, and nearly half included dynamic adaptation to users. Safety procedures (e.g., risk management, guardrails) were inconsistently reported; only 75% of user-facing studies mentioned informed consent or user education. Around 63% of studies evaluated MI quality, typically finding that GenAI systems produced MI-consistent dialogue. User perceptions were generally positive regarding empathy, usability, helpfulness, and intention to use, though the measures used varied widely. Only 18 studies examined intervention outcomes, mostly after a single session, showing more consistent gains in short-term motivation than in durable behavioral or functional changes.

Conclusion: Generative-AI-driven MI chatbots appear capable of producing MI-consistent, user-accepted conversations, but current evidence is insufficient to conclude that they reliably produce sustained behavior change or functional improvement. Research needs include more robust runtime safety monitoring and reporting, standardized metrics for MI quality, and longer-term, comparative study designs that directly measure behavioral and functional outcomes rather than only short-term motivational effects.

Abstract: Motivational interviewing (MI) is a collaborative approach to elicit autonomous motivation for health behavior change. Generative AI (GenAI) offers new ways to deliver MI via conversational systems, but evidence on their design, assessment, and translation into interventions remains fragmented. This scoping review characterized evidence on GenAI-MI chatbots across system design, safety, MI quality, user perceptions, and intervention outcomes. We conducted a PRISMA-ScR scoping review. Nine datasets were searched for studies published or publicly available from January 1, 2015 to June 2, 2026 that used GenAI to generate MI chatbot responses or counselor utterances. Data were extracted using a predefined framework and synthesized descriptively. Forty-seven reports (48 studies) were included. Twenty (41.7%) focused on system design without direct participant use; 28 (58.3%) involved direct interaction. Most systems were text based and disembodied; 23 (47.9%) incorporated dynamic adaptation. Safety measures were unevenly reported. Among studies with direct use, 21/28 (75.0%) reported informed consent or user education. Thirty (62.5%) assessed MI quality, generally suggesting MI-consistent interactions. User perceptions were favorable, especially empathy, usability, helpfulness, and intention to use, though measures were heterogeneous. Eighteen (37.5%) reported intervention outcomes, mostly after a single session. Positive findings were more consistent for short-term motivation than sustained behavioral or functional change. GenAI-MI chatbots can deliver MI-consistent interactions perceived favorably, but evidence for sustained behavioral or functional change is limited. Future research should strengthen runtime safety monitoring, standardize MI quality assessment, and use longer-term comparative designs with behavioral and functional outcomes.

</details>


### [25] [$μ^2$-Bench: A Multilingual Machine Unlearning Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20945)
*Kyomin Hwang,Hyeonjin Kim,Hyunho Lee,Yearim Kim,Yeji Song,Nojun Kwak*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces μ²-Bench, a benchmark to evaluate how well methods can unlearn harmful or private information from multilingual LLMs across many languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Undesired information, like harmful content or private data, can be memorized by multilingual LLMs and spread across languages via direct training and cross-linguistic transfer. Existing multilingual machine unlearning (MMU) methods lack rigorous, standardized evaluation, so it is unclear whether unlearning actually removes the targeted knowledge in all relevant languages. The authors want a controlled, realistic way to test memorization, unlearning, and verification of removal in multilingual settings.

Method: They design μ²-Bench, a benchmark that simulates the full lifecycle: (1) injecting or identifying specific knowledge in multilingual LLMs (memorization phase), (2) applying MMU methods to erase that knowledge (unlearning phase), and (3) systematically testing whether that knowledge persists. The benchmark covers a broad set of languages, evaluates both languages seen during unlearning and unseen (hold-out) languages, and measures how target knowledge is distributed and potentially re-emerges via cross-lingual transfer. They then apply various MMU methods to the benchmark and compare their performance.

Result: Experiments on μ²-Bench show that many existing or naive MMU methods fail to fully remove targeted knowledge across all languages, especially in hold-out languages or when knowledge is dispersed cross-lingually. Methods that explicitly account for multilingual properties—such as shared representations and cross-lingual transfer—perform better and achieve more consistent unlearning across languages. The benchmark highlights specific failure modes and gaps in current MMU techniques.

Conclusion: μ²-Bench provides a systematic way to evaluate multilingual machine unlearning, revealing that effective unlearning in multilingual LLMs must explicitly handle cross-lingual knowledge sharing rather than treating languages independently. The benchmark and analyses can guide the development of more robust MMU methods and serve as a standard evaluation tool for future work on safely removing information from multilingual models.

Abstract: Undesired information such as harmful content and private data propagates through Multilingual Large Language Models (LLMs) via direct training and indirect cross-linguistic spread. Multilingual Machine Unlearning (MMU) aims to remove such information, yet its evaluation remains underexplored, leaving unclear whether unlearning truly eliminates target knowledge across all languages. To bridge this gap, we introduce $μ^2$-Bench, an MMU benchmark that simulates the full pipeline of memorization, unlearning, and evaluation across diverse languages. It 1) spans a broad set of languages, 2) evaluates on both training and hold-out languages, and 3) assesses knowledge as dispersed across multiple languages. We show that successful MMU requires methods that reflect multilingual characteristics, and conduct analysis to provide deeper insights into MMU.

</details>


### [26] [Aligning with Lived Experience: Heterogeneous Benefits of Fine Tuning in Mental Health Support Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21075)
*Mohit Chandra,Nabin Kim,Eli Min,Aamogh Sawant,Tanmay Sutar,Munmun De Choudhury*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build a Reddit-based dataset and evaluation framework to see how well LLMs provide peer-style mental health support, and show that community-specific training helps but introduces uneven improvements and shifts in coping advice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many people lack access to professional mental healthcare and instead seek help on Reddit, but many posts get no replies. Existing LLM work focuses on clinical benchmarks, not on generating peer support that reflects lived experiences and community norms. The authors want to understand and improve how LLMs can fill these gaps in a way that aligns with what communities actually do and value.

Method: They construct COPES, a community-centered dataset of mental health support-seeking posts and community replies from Reddit, and design a three-axis evaluation framework for assessing how aligned LLM-generated responses are with community perspectives. They then evaluate both zero-shot general-purpose LLMs and models post-trained on COPES using supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and direct preference optimization (DPO), comparing their performance on strategy alignment and emotion/tone alignment across subreddits and coping strategy types.

Result: Post-training LLMs on COPES greatly improves how well their suggested coping strategies match community strategies (over 50% improvement for general models) and improves emotional and tonal alignment with community responses. However, these gains are not uniform: performance varies widely by subreddit and requested coping strategy, and post-training shifts the distribution of advice toward more problem-focused and away from emotion-focused strategies.

Conclusion: Community-driven datasets like COPES and targeted post-training can substantially improve LLM alignment with peer support norms in online mental health communities, but the resulting models are still uneven across sub-communities and mental health needs and can introduce systematic shifts in the style of coping strategies they recommend. Designers must therefore be cautious about overgeneralizing performance and should account for community heterogeneity and unintended distributional changes when deploying LLMs for mental health peer support.

Abstract: As access to professional mental healthcare remains limited, many individuals turn to online platforms such as Reddit to seek peer support situated within human lived experience. However, a significant portion of such queries go unanswered, presenting an opportunity for using Large Language Models (LLMs) to fill this gap. While LLMs have demonstrated strong performance on clinical benchmarks, their ability to generate lived-experience informed and community-aligned peer support is underexplored. Addressing this gap, we introduce the COmmunity-centered Peer Engaged Support (COPES) dataset and a three-axis evaluation framework to assess LLM alignment with community perspectives to mental health support seeking queries. Evaluating zero-shot and post-trained (SFT and DPO) models, we show that post-training on COPES significantly improves Strategy Alignment (>50% for general-purpose models) and alignment in Emotion & Tone. However, we also observe that such improvements are heterogeneous and alignment improvements vary significantly across subreddits and requested coping strategies. Furthermore, post-training induces distributional shifts, heavily favoring problem-focused recommendations while suppressing emotion-focused strategies. Together, this work shows that while curating community-driven data improves the alignment of LLM responses, model performance remains disparate across distinct sub-communities and specific mental health needs.

</details>


### [27] [Geometry of Values: Task Vector Composition for Ethical Preference Alignment in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21094)
*Utkarsh Agarwal,Monojit Choudhury*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Dataset and method to measure and control LLM moral value tradeoffs and biases across languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are used in settings that require moral tradeoffs, but they display hidden value biases, option-order bias, and brittle instruction following, especially across languages. There is a need for systematic benchmarks and methods to both measure and steer these moral preferences in a controlled way.

Method: Construct a 12k-instance, two-option dilemma dataset covering three pairwise moral value conflicts (Honesty vs. Justice, Justice vs. Autonomy, Autonomy vs. Honesty) in five languages (English, Hindi, Arabic, Spanish, Chinese). Benchmark different LLMs on these dilemmas, analyze their default value preferences and biases (e.g., first-option bias). Apply plain fine-tuning and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to reduce option-order bias. Then compute task vectors capturing directions of specific value preferences in representation space, orthogonalize these to a general instruction-following vector, and use task vector arithmetic to flip or modify the model’s stance on these values while decoupling it from dataset correlations.

Result: GPT-5-mini systematically prefers Honesty over Autonomy in all languages when no explicit policy is provided. Llama-3.2-1/3B models exhibit strong first-option bias on the dilemmas. Both standard fine-tuning and DPO successfully remove this first-option bias and achieve over 98% accuracy on the benchmark. The proposed task vector transfer experiment shows that it is possible to isolate a direction corresponding to a specific value preference, orthogonal to the general instruction-following direction, and that adding or subtracting this vector effectively flips the model’s stance on the targeted value tradeoff.

Conclusion: The paper offers (1) a multilingual moral dilemma benchmark to probe value tradeoffs in LLMs, (2) evidence of systematic moral and option-order biases in current models, and (3) a task-vector-based method to isolate and manipulate specific moral value preferences independently of general instruction following. This enables more controlled steering of LLM moral behavior across languages and reduces confounding from dataset artifacts.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in applications that must weigh clashing moral values, yet even strong models exhibit hidden biases and brittle instruction-following across languages. We introduce a 12,000-instance dataset of two-option dilemmas covering pairwise three value conflicts: Honesty vs. Justice, Justice vs. Autonomy, and Autonomy vs. Honesty, along with their translations into Hindi, Arabic, Spanish, and Chinese, to probe cross-lingual behavior. Benchmarking on GPT-5-mini reveals that it consistently favors Honesty over Autonomy across all five languages when no policy is given. The Llama-3.2-1/3B models exhibit strong first-option bias; however, both plain fine-tuning and Direct Preference Optimization fine-tuning effectively remove this bias, increasing accuracy to greater than 98%. In order to decouple the effect of learning correlations in the dataset from abstract values, we propose a task vector transfer based experiment where after computing the task vectors for a direction of value preference we orthogonalize it with respect to the general instruction following vector. Our experiment shows that this method is effective in isolating the direction of the specific value preference that can successfully be used to conduct task arithmetic to obtain a model with the opposite stance.

</details>


### [28] [From Task Success to Productive Success: Evaluating Human-AI Collaboration by Quality and Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21117)
*Saki Imai,Mert İnan,Malihe Alikhani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Framework to evaluate human-AI collaboration productivity by relating outcome quality to interaction cost, revealing that similar-quality outcomes can have vastly different interaction costs and that productive sessions show distinct interaction patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI productivity metrics focus on outcome quality, time, or economic value but ignore how much interaction effort is required to achieve those outcomes. Inspired by economics, the authors want to make interaction costs explicit to better understand and evaluate human-AI collaboration.

Method: They propose a productivity-oriented evaluation framework that defines productivity as the ratio of outcome quality to interaction cost. Using two datasets with four different collaborative tasks, they analyze human-AI interaction logs, quantify interaction cost, compare sessions with similar quality but different costs, and study quality-cost trade-offs, subjective user ratings, and conversational patterns.

Result: They find that sessions with the same quality can differ in interaction cost by up to 70x; the optimal balance between interaction length and quality depends on the task; subjective user ratings do not reliably reflect productivity; and highly productive sessions tend to involve the AI agent probing earlier and users needing to spend less effort on repairing or steering the interaction.

Conclusion: The framework distinguishes between high-quality outcomes that are achieved efficiently versus those that are costly in interaction terms. This makes interactional cost visible, demonstrates that productivity is not captured by quality or satisfaction alone, and shows how analyzing dialogue structure can guide the evaluation and design of more productive human-AI systems.

Abstract: AI productivity is often measured by task completion time, economic value, or improvements in outcome quality. However, these measures usually treat collaboration as a black box where they capture what output was produced, but not the interaction cost required to produce it. Motivated by economics literature, we introduce a productivity-oriented framework for evaluating human-AI collaboration as outcome quality relative to interaction cost. Across two datasets spanning four tasks, we show that: (1) sessions with identical quality ratings can differ by up to 70 times in interaction cost; (2) quality-cost relationships vary by task, with some tasks rewarding extended interaction and others favoring fast convergence; (3) subjective user ratings are not reliable substitutes for productivity; and (4) productive sessions are characterized by agents probing earlier and users spending less effort repairing the interaction. By distinguishing productive success from costly success, our framework makes interactional cost visible and shows how dialogue analysis can inform the evaluation and design of AI systems.

</details>


### [29] [Scaling Forced Alignment to End-User Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21145)
*Lawry Sorenson,Michael Crandall,Eric K. Ringger,Stephen D. Richardson*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper improves forced audio-text alignment by making it both memory-efficient and faster while keeping accuracy high.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Viterbi-based forced alignment systems have quadratic time and space complexity, which makes them impractical for long audio sequences (e.g., hours of speech) when mining training data from large online resources. The authors want an alignment method that can handle very long inputs with modest computational resources while maintaining accuracy, and that is robust to transcription errors.

Method: They introduce two main optimizations for Viterbi-style forced alignment. (1) They apply the Hirschberg algorithm to perform the dynamic-programming-based alignment in place with linear memory instead of quadratic, effectively re-structuring the DP computation to require only O(n) space. (2) They reinterpret the speech-text alignment as a constrained random walk in the alignment lattice, which allows them to prune unlikely paths adaptively with a controllable confidence level and with explicit modeling of transcription errors, reducing the search space while preserving correct alignments.

Result: Using the Hirschberg optimization, they cut memory usage from about 140 GB to 5 MB for three-hour audio inputs and run in about one-third the time of torchaudio’s CPU implementation while producing identical alignments. On top of that, the pruning based on the constrained random walk yields an additional 2x speedup for inputs longer than 20 minutes, with alignment accuracy maintained in over 98% of evaluated cases.

Conclusion: The proposed optimizations make Viterbi-based forced alignment practical and efficient for very long audio sequences. Linear-memory Hirschberg alignment drastically reduces memory requirements and even improves runtime, while the constrained-random-walk-based pruning further accelerates alignment with negligible loss of accuracy, enabling large-scale mining of audio-text training data from long recordings.

Abstract: The Viterbi algorithm has been previously used to perform forced alignment of audio to text to mine training data from online resources. However, many existing implementations have quadratic time and space complexity, scaling poorly to long input sequences. We propose two optimizations to address this issue. First, we apply the Hirschberg algorithm to perform the alignment in place using linear memory. Second, we model the alignment between speech and text as a constrained random walk, allowing us to prune the search space with arbitrary confidence while accounting for transcription errors. The Hirschberg optimization reduces memory usage from 140 GB to 5 MB for three-hour inputs while producing identical alignments in one-third the time of torchaudio when both run on a CPU. We achieve an additional 2x speedup with pruning on inputs longer than 20 minutes while preserving alignment accuracy in more than 98% of tested cases.

</details>


### [30] [CoLearn: An Agentic Tutor that Learns its Learner in a Human--AI Co-Learning Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21154)
*Kailai He,Zhihao Wu,Linhai Zhang,Runcong Zhao,Yulan He,Jiazheng Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CoLearn is an interactive tutoring system that uses an evidence-based memory of each learner’s knowledge and misconceptions to generate personalised follow-up questions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing tutoring tools use fixed question banks and treat each answer (especially wrong ones) as a simple right/wrong signal, failing to capture nuanced misconceptions and changing mastery. The authors want a tutor that can adapt continuously to the learner, model their knowledge in a richer way, and visibly justify its personalisation.

Method: They design CoLearn with three key components: (1) a persistent learner-state memory that tracks topic-level mastery using a soft-evidence version of Bayesian Knowledge Tracing, where an LLM acts as a continuous observation function over student responses; (2) an adaptive question generation module that uses this memory to target weak topics and recurring misconceptions; and (3) an evidence view that visualises progress and supports blind A/B testing between personalised and non-personalised questions. They then evaluate via blind human preference tests and simulated learners with known ground-truth mastery.

Result: In blind A/B tests, users prefer questions conditioned on the learner-state memory over non-personalised questions 68–69% of the time. In persona-based simulations where the learner’s true mastery is known, the system’s belief state converges toward the true mastery levels, indicating the learner model is tracking knowledge reasonably well.

Conclusion: CoLearn can build and update a nuanced learner model using soft evidence from an LLM and leverage it to generate personalised, higher-quality questions. The evidence view allows its adaptivity to be inspected and tested. Empirical results suggest that this memory-conditioned personalisation improves question quality and that the system’s mastery estimates converge toward learners’ true abilities.

Abstract: Good tutoring adapts to the individual: it tracks what a learner knows, notices why they go wrong, and asks the next question that will help most. Most deployed tutoring tools instead serve fixed item banks and treat a wrong answer as a single bit of signal. We present CoLearn, an interactive, agentic tutor that supports an iterative tutoring loop: the learner practises, and the system builds an evidence-grounded memory of the learner's mastery and misconceptions. This memory is updated as evidence accumulates and is used to generate the next personalised question. CoLearn has three components: (i) a persistent learner-state memory that updates per-topic mastery with a soft-evidence variant of Bayesian Knowledge Tracing, where a large language model acts as a continuous observation function; (ii) adaptive question generation that targets the learner's weakest topic and recurring misconceptions; and (iii) an evidence view that makes personalisation visible and testable through live progress visualisation and blind A/B comparison. In blind A/B evaluation, questions conditioned on this memory are preferred over non-personalised ones 68-69% of the time, and in persona simulations with hidden ground-truth mastery the agent's belief converges toward the learner's true mastery.

</details>


### [31] [Not All Irregularity Is Equal: Causally Isolating a Rare Failure Mode in Japanese Morphological Inflection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21179)
*Wen Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that very rare, structurally specific verb types cause a large share of errors in otherwise high-performing Japanese past-tense generation models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural models of morphology often report high overall accuracy, but this can mask systematic failures on specific morphological subclasses, especially rare ones. For Japanese past-tense verb inflection, orthography (hiragana) encodes morphophonological patterns that may interact with model learning in non-obvious ways. The authors want to uncover which subclasses are actually causing errors and why, beyond aggregate scores.

Method: They analyze Japanese past-tense verb inflection using two character-level Transformer architectures, each trained and evaluated over five random seeds. They treat hiragana orthography as encoding morphophonological structure rather than as a simple phonetic transcription. They perform detailed subclass error analysis focusing on irregular verb types, particularly a subtype whose stems end in /e/ and require gemination before the past-tense suffix. They then run controlled ablation experiments where they remove specific verb subtypes from the training/evaluation data to isolate their causal contribution to model performance.

Result: Both Transformer systems achieve over 97% overall accuracy, but a single very rare irregular subtype—/e/-final stems that undergo gemination before the past-tense suffix, comprising less than 1% of the corpus—accounts for 30–43% of the remaining errors. This subtype contributes roughly 34–48 times its frequency to the total error mass. Ablation experiments show that removing this single subtype yields a larger gain in accuracy than removing all irregular verbs combined.

Conclusion: The core difficulty for neural morphological generation in this setting is not irregularity in general but specific low-frequency morphological patterns that interact with orthographic processes like gemination. Error mass can be heavily concentrated in such subclasses while being invisible in aggregate metrics. Therefore, morphological evaluation should include fine-grained subclass-based analysis. These insights have implications for designing more data-efficient and cognitively plausible language model pretraining strategies that explicitly account for rare, structurally defined subclasses and orthographic structure.

Abstract: Neural morphological generation systems often achieve high aggregate accuracy on benchmark datasets, yet such performance can conceal systematic errors clustered in rare morphological subclasses. We present an orthography-aware diagnosis of Japanese past-tense verb inflection, treating hiragana not merely as a transcriptional medium but as a representational system that encodes morphophonological structure. Using two character-level Transformer architectures evaluated across five random seeds, we show that although both systems exceed 97% aggregate accuracy, a single structurally specific irregular subtype, verbs whose stems end in /e/ and require gemination before the past-tense suffix and make up fewer than 1% of the data, accounts for a disproportionate 30-43% share of residual errors and contributes roughly 34-48x its prevalence to total errors. We then move from diagnosis to causal isolation: controlled ablation experiments show that removing this subtype alone produces larger accuracy gains than removing all irregular verbs combined. These findings indicate that error concentration in neural morphological learning is not driven by irregularity per se, but by the interaction between extreme low-frequency morphological patterns and specific orthographic processes. We argue that morphological evaluation should incorporate fine-grained subclass analysis, and discuss implications for data-efficient, developmentally plausible language model pretraining.

</details>


### [32] [When Better Turns Do Not Make Better Agents: Diagnosing the Gap Between Next-Turn Metrics and Workflow Success](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21187)
*Md Tahmid Rahman Laskar,Xue-Yong Fu,Gundeep Singh,Karol Chang,Kevin Sanders,Shi Zong,Tania Habib,Julien Bouvier Tremblay,Shayna Gardiner,Harsh Saini,Matthias Lee,Elena Khasanova,Quinten McNamara,Shashi Bhushan TN*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that standard next-turn evaluation of agent models does not predict real autonomous workflow performance in multi-turn customer-support tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In agent modeling, models are often evaluated by how well they choose the next action given a gold interaction history, but it is unclear whether improvements in this setting actually translate into better end-to-end autonomous performance on complex workflows.

Method: The authors compare pre-supervised and supervised fine-tuned (SFT) versions of Qwen3 (4B, 14B) and Gemma 3 (4B, 12B) models on multi-turn customer-support workflows. They measure text-turn success and next-turn success under gold-history evaluation, and then contrast these with autonomous workflow execution performance, including strict trajectory completion and tool-specific metrics.

Result: SFT consistently improves text-turn success and boosts next-turn success in gold-history evaluation for all models. However, these gains largely fail to transfer to autonomous workflow execution: tool-specific improvements are inconsistent across metrics and models, and none of the four SFT models achieves strong holistic workflow performance, with strict trajectory completion capped at 10.4% success.

Conclusion: Next-turn, gold-history evaluation is not a reliable proxy for end-to-end workflow success in agent models. The authors advocate for a more granular and comprehensive evaluation framework that separately reports text quality, local action correctness, tool execution quality, and overall task completion to accurately assess agent capabilities.

Abstract: Agent models are frequently evaluated one decision at a time, where the model predicts the next action based on the gold interaction history, which is scored against a reference. We investigate whether improvement under this protocol is predictive of improved autonomous workflow execution. We study pre-SFT and supervised fine-tuned (SFT) Qwen3 models at 4B and 14B parameters and Gemma 3 models at 4B and 12B parameters on multi-turn customer-support workflows. We find that SFT consistently improves text-turn success, and that overall next-turn success increases for every model under gold-history evaluation. However, these improvements do not transfer to autonomous workflow execution. Tool-specific gains also vary across metrics and models. None of the four SFT models succeeds under holistic workflow evaluation, with strict trajectory completion reaching at most 10.4% workflow success. Our results show that next-turn evaluation is not a reliable proxy for workflow success, motivating separate reporting of text quality, local action correctness, tool execution, and end-to-end task completion.

</details>


### [33] [Hallucination-R1: Robustness-Oriented Paraphrase Generation for Factual Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21227)
*Wenhan Yu,Wenxin Wu,Hao Wang,Lei Sha*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces HALLUCINATION-R1, a paraphrase-generation framework that creates meaning-preserving yet challenging paraphrases to expose and reduce factual hallucination in QA models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Factual hallucination is usually defined as outright incorrect factual outputs, but many models show a more subtle weakness: they answer a question correctly in one wording yet fail on a semantically equivalent paraphrase. Existing paraphrase generation for robustness is unsatisfactory: near-copies do not stress the model enough, while overly diverse paraphrases may change the meaning and thus no longer test true robustness. The authors aim to systematically generate paraphrases that are both semantically faithful and effective at revealing such latent factual instability, in order to better diagnose and improve model robustness.

Method: The authors propose HALLUCINATION-R1, a two-stage robustness-oriented paraphrase generation framework. In the first stage, the model is optimized to produce diverse paraphrases that still preserve the original meaning, stabilizing high-quality paraphrasing. In the second stage, they introduce a reward signal that favors paraphrases which cause downstream QA models to change correct answers into incorrect ones, i.e., which expose factual consistency degradation. This is framed as learning to generate paraphrases that are simultaneously faithful, diverse, and adversarial with respect to factual consistency. They then apply this framework to generate paraphrases for several QA benchmarks and evaluate across multiple model families.

Result: On SimpleQuestions, PopQA, and TruthfulQA, HALLUCINATION-R1 generates paraphrases that achieve a strong trade-off between semantic consistency and lexical diversity, while more effectively revealing robustness failures than general-purpose paraphrase methods. The generated paraphrases induce factual inconsistency in various QA models, showing that the framework can systematically find paraphrases that turn correct answers into incorrect ones. Additional analyses suggest that these failures are not merely due to superficial text changes or subtle meaning drift but reflect genuine factual instability. A lightweight fine-tuning experiment shows that training on HALLUCINATION-R1-generated paraphrase data improves robust accuracy under paraphrase variations.

Conclusion: HALLUCINATION-R1 is an effective framework for generating semantically faithful yet robustness-challenging paraphrases that expose and help mitigate factual hallucinations in QA models. By explicitly optimizing paraphrase generation for both meaning preservation and the ability to degrade factual consistency, the method uncovers non-trivial instability in existing models and can be used to improve robustness via additional training. This establishes robustness-oriented paraphrase generation as a practical tool for diagnosing and strengthening factual reliability in language models.

Abstract: Factual hallucination is commonly defined by incorrect factual outputs. We study a paraphrase-induced hallucination setting, where a model answers a factual question correctly in its original form but generates an incorrect answer under a semantically equivalent paraphrase. Such inconsistencies expose latent factual instability under semantic invariance. However, general-purpose paraphrases are often insufficient as robustness-oriented supervision: near-copy paraphrases provide weak signals, while overly diverse paraphrases may break semantic equivalence. In this paper, we propose HALLUCINATION-R1, a robustness-oriented paraphrase generation framework that learns to produce semantically faithful yet robustness-challenging paraphrases for factual consistency. Through two-stage optimization, it first stabilizes meaning-preserving and diverse paraphrasing, then rewards paraphrases that reveal factual consistency degradation in downstream QA models. Experiments on SimpleQuestions, PopQA, and TruthfulQA show that HALLUCINATION-R1 achieves a strong consistency--diversity trade-off and exposes robustness failures across multiple model families and datasets. Further analyses indicate that these failures are not reducible to surface-level artifacts or semantic drift, but reveal non-trivial factual instability under meaning-preserving variation. A lightweight fine-tuning study also shows that HALLUCINATION-R1-generated data improves robust accuracy under paraphrase variations, suggesting its utility for robustness-oriented training. Our code and models are publicly available at https://github.com/yuwenhan07/Hallucination-R1.

</details>


### [34] [Beyond Reference-Based Evaluation: Reward Models for Meta-Evaluation of Grammatical Error Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21231)
*Ruotian Wu,Bill E. Johnson,Gene Saunders,Osama Hamzeh,Ankit Vadehra,Pascal Poupart*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes RM-EVAL, a reference-free reward model for grammatical error correction (GEC) that evaluates and guides generation without relying on gold references, improving both evaluation quality and system performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GEC evaluation metrics like M^2 and ERRANT depend on limited human reference corrections and assume they cover all valid edits. This leads to unfair penalties for grammatical, meaning-preserving alternatives that differ from the references, misaligning automatic evaluation with human judgments and making it hard to fairly compare or train GEC systems.

Method: The authors introduce RM-EVAL, a reward model trained on human preference data from the SEEDA dataset. RM-EVAL predicts human-like quality judgments for both full outputs and segments (partial sequences). They then use this learned reward in a Reward-Guided Text Generation (RGTG) framework, where a base GEC model is kept frozen and decoding is steered online to maximize the RM-EVAL reward, effectively using the reward model as a meta-evaluator and training signal without gold references.

Result: RM-EVAL shows high agreement with human rankings on SEEDA, indicating it reliably approximates human preferences for GEC quality. When used in the RGTG framework, it consistently improves both the RM-EVAL reward scores and external validation metrics across experiments, suggesting that the reward-guided decoding produces objectively better GEC outputs.

Conclusion: A single reward model can serve as both a reference-free evaluator and a learning signal for GEC systems. By aligning with human preferences and avoiding dependence on gold reference corrections, RM-EVAL and RGTG provide a unified, reference-free framework for more accurate evaluation and effective improvement of grammatical error correction models.

Abstract: Reference-based metrics for Grammatical Error Correction (GEC) such as M$^2$ and ERRANT assume that the reference set enumerates all valid edits, and therefore often penalize corrections that are grammatical and meaning-preserving but phrased differently. We introduce RM-EVAL, a reward model trained on human preference data from SEEDA, as a reference-free meta-evaluator that predicts human-like quality judgments at both full-sequence and partial-sequence levels. Beyond evaluation, we show that the same reward model can be used as a learning signal to improve GEC generation via Reward-Guided Text Generation (RGTG), which keeps a base GEC model frozen and performs online, reward-driven decoding. Across SEEDA, RM-EVAL achieves strong agreement with human rankings, and RGTG yields consistent gains in reward and external validation, demonstrating a unified framework for both assessing and enhancing GEC systems without relying on gold references.

</details>


### [35] [When Does Reasoning Help in Machine Translation? A Hierarchical Analysis of LRM Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21247)
*Yuxiang Liu,Jiaming Luo,Eleftheria Briakou,Colin Cherry*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies when and how explicit reasoning traces help or hurt in machine translation, and introduces a method to analyze their structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Reasoning Models often use step-by-step reasoning for tasks like machine translation, but it is not well understood under which conditions this reasoning improves translation quality, what languages or styles of reasoning are best, and what structural patterns these traces follow. A systematic analysis is needed to guide how to control and design reasoning for MT.

Method: The authors empirically analyze reasoning traces from different models, languages, domains, and datasets, focusing on the reasoning language, the length of reasoning, and structural patterns in the traces. They propose Hierarchical Meta-Summarization (HMS), a scalable framework that automatically induces coarse- and fine-grained structures of reasoning traces without relying on predefined taxonomies, effectively summarizing and clustering patterns of reasoning behavior.

Result: They find that the optimal language for reasoning is specific to each model, that the effect of reasoning length on translation quality is non-monotonic (more reasoning is not always better), and that reasoning traces show recurring functional patterns. Using HMS, they identify a shared high-level organization of reasoning (understanding/planning, translating/drafting, refining/verifying) as well as variations that depend on the domain.

Conclusion: Explicit reasoning in MT should not be encouraged uniformly. Instead, it needs to be controlled in a way that is aware of the specific model, sensitive to the length of reasoning, and informed by typical structural patterns of reasoning that occur in a given context or domain.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models increasingly use intermediate traces for machine translation, but it remains unclear when such reasoning helps or hurts. We analyze reasoning traces across models, languages, domains, and datasets, focusing on reasoning language, length, and structure. We find that the best reasoning language is model-specific, reasoning length has a non-monotonic relationship with quality, and traces exhibit recurring functional patterns. To uncover these patterns, we introduce Hierarchical Meta-Summarization (HMS), a scalable framework that induces coarse- and fine-grained reasoning structures without predefined taxonomies. HMS reveals a shared organization--understanding/planning, translating/drafting, and refining/verifying--alongside domain-specific variation. Our results suggest that MT reasoning should be controlled in a model-aware, length-aware, and pattern-aware manner rather than uniformly encouraged.

</details>


### [36] [How Many Humans Is a Judge Panel Worth?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21277)
*Chao Li,Yingying Yu,Yunfeng Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how many distinct human-like perspectives a panel of language-model judges effectively represents, and shows that this “effective size” depends strongly on how you measure it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models are increasingly used as automatic judges for evaluation, moderation, and data labeling, often with multiple models forming a panel to mimic a group of humans. However, prior work usually treats panel size or agreement as a simple proxy for “how many humans” they replace, collapsing human disagreement into a single gold label. The authors are motivated to create a more principled way to quantify how many diverse human-like judgments a model panel actually represents, especially when human labels are inherently noisy and distributed rather than single ground truths.

Method: The authors audit panels of categorical LLM judges against empirical human label distributions using ChaosNLI datasets, which contain full distributions over human labels. Instead of comparing to a single gold label, they retain disagreement by working at the distributional level. They introduce two effective-size measures: (1) a spectral residual diversity measure nu_H, computed by matching the participation ratio of a normalized residual Gram matrix to that of conditionally independent human draws, and (2) nu_MSE, an effective size that matches the distributional mean-squared error between panel predictions and human label distributions. They analyze the panels’ eigen-spectra, member “energies” (variance contributions), and averaging-direction weights to understand how diversity and error arise. They also construct realizable hard-label panels to show how spectral properties relate to distribution recovery and examine how adding or removing panel members changes rankings across tasks and item splits. Finally, they quantify the share of centered residual variance aligned with the consensus (averaging) direction, denoted gamma_co.

Result: On three ChaosNLI tasks (including MNLI-m and SNLI), the same 32-model judge panels exhibit much larger spectral diversity than their distribution-matching performance would suggest: nu_H ranges from 4.24 to 6.50, whereas nu_MSE is only 2.30 to 3.75. This demonstrates that diversity in residual directions (as captured spectrally) and accuracy in recovering the true human label distribution can diverge significantly. A derived spectral identity separates contributions from eigenvalues, member energies, and averaging-direction weights, clarifying how these factors jointly determine panel error. Experiments with realizable hard-label panels show that higher spectral diversity can even correspond to worse recovery of the human label distribution, even when all members have equal energy and nonnegative correlations. In empirical panels, the ordering of model contributions to effective size depends heavily on the task, and adding certain members can yield conflicting effects that persist across data splits. The consensus-direction share of residual variance is substantial—about 43.8% for MNLI-m and 33.7% for SNLI—indicating that a large fraction of the residual variation is shared and thus not reduced by averaging.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the effective number of human-like judges represented by a language-model panel is not a single, task-independent quantity and strongly depends on the evaluation criterion. Spectral diversity (nu_H) and distributional accuracy (nu_MSE) capture different facets of panel behavior and should not be conflated as interchangeable measures of panel quality or as direct multipliers for “how many humans” a panel replaces. High spectral diversity does not guarantee faithful recovery of human label distributions, and averaging can leave substantial shared residual variance. Therefore, effective panel size is inherently target- and metric-specific, and any claims about LLM panels substituting for humans must specify which notion of effective size is being used. The authors release aligned votes and analysis tools to support more nuanced auditing of LLM judge panels.

Abstract: How many human judgments does a panel of language models represent? The answer depends on what is matched. We audit categorical judge panels against empirical human label distributions, retaining disagreement that binary errors relative to one gold label collapse. We measure spectral residual diversity by matching the participation ratio of a normalized residual Gram matrix to conditionally independent human-reference draws, giving nu_H. We separately match distributional squared error, giving nu_MSE. Across three ChaosNLI tasks, the same 32-judge panels have nu_H=4.24--6.50 but nu_MSE=2.30--3.75. A spectral identity separates the eigenvalues, member energies, and averaging-direction weights that determine error. Realizable hard-label panels show that greater spectral diversity can accompany worse distribution recovery even with equal member energies and nonnegative correlations. In the observed panels, within-size ranking agreement varies sharply by task; some member additions produce conflicting changes that persist across two item halves. The consensus-direction share of centered residual variance is gamma_co=43.8% on MNLI-m and 33.7% on SNLI, quantifying shared variation retained by averaging. We provide aligned votes and analysis protocols for auditing these distinctions. Effective size is therefore a target-specific measurement: spectral diversity and distribution recovery should not be treated as interchangeable measures of panel quality or as general human-replacement rates.

</details>


### [37] [From Memory to Behavior: A Behavior-Aware Role-Playing Framework for Social Media Influencers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21349)
*Ji-Lun Peng,Yi-Zhen Zhang,Chun-Nan Chou,Yun-Nung Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new method and evaluation protocol to improve how large language models impersonate real or fictional individuals in a role-playing setting, especially under different situations on social media.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although large language models can act as role-playing agents for specific people, existing in-context learning approaches struggle to faithfully capture how a person would behave across varying situations. Moreover, evaluating impersonation quality with LLM judges is difficult for lesser-known individuals, because the models lack sufficient reference information about them. The authors aim to solve both the behavioral fidelity problem and the evaluation challenge.

Method: They introduce the Situation–Internal state–Behavior Persona method, which explicitly integrates situation-dependent behavioral strategies into the persona representation, modeling how an individual’s internal state mediates between situations and observable behavior. Additionally, they design an evaluation protocol for LLM-based judges that supplies them with reference information about the target individual, making it easier to assess impersonation accuracy. They test their method on a new social-media reply-generation dataset and on benchmarks of fictional characters.

Result: On the social media reply-generation task, their method outperforms state-of-the-art in-context-learning-based baselines in terms of impersonation quality. Their new LLM-based evaluation protocol shows moderate correlation with human judgments, indicating it is reasonably aligned with human perception. Experiments on fictional-character benchmarks further show that the approach generalizes beyond social media and to non-real individuals.

Conclusion: Incorporating structured behavioral information—via the Situation–Internal state–Behavior Persona framework—significantly improves the fidelity of LLM-based role-playing for both real individuals in social media contexts and fictional characters. Providing LLM evaluators with explicit reference material about the impersonated individual yields a more reliable automatic evaluation that moderately reflects human judgment.

Abstract: Large language models have shown strong potential as role-playing agents for real individuals, yet faithful impersonating remains challenging. Existing in-context learning-based methods fail to capture how individuals react under different situations. In addition, LLM-based evaluation is difficult for obscure individuals. To address these challenges, we propose Situation--Internal state--Behavior Persona method to incorporate situation-dependent behavioral strategies. We further design an evaluation protocol that provides LLM evaluators with references about the impersonated individual. We evaluate our approach on a newly constructed dataset for the task of generating replies on social media. Experimental results show that our proposed method outperforms state-of-the-art ICL-based baselines, while our evaluation protocol achieves moderate correlation with human judgment. Besides, experiments on fictional-character benchmarks demonstrate that our proposed method is applicable beyond the social media setting. These findings suggest that incorporating behavioral information broadly improves the fidelity of role-playing for real individuals on social media or fictional characters.

</details>


### [38] [Beyond Atomic Tokens: Factorizing Syllables for Language Model Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21362)
*Nghia Hieu Nguyen,Thai Bao Huynh,Binh-An Dinh-Le,Phu Gia Hoang,Dat Tien Nguyen,Kiet Van Nguyen,Ngan Luu-Thuy Nguyen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a linguistically motivated phonemic tokenizer (and associated BERT models) for Vietnamese and Chinese that factorizes each syllable into onset, rime, and tone, yielding compact vocabularies and competitive or better downstream performance than standard tokenizers and models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tokenizers for languages like Vietnamese and Chinese rely on characters or statistically derived subwords, which ignore the internal phonological structure of syllables and often require large, corpus-dependent vocabularies. This leads to redundancy, inefficiency, longer sequences, and reduced interpretability. The authors aim to design a tokenizer that leverages phonemic structure to obtain a smaller, linguistically meaningful vocabulary that can share representations among phonologically related syllables while maintaining or improving NLP performance.

Method: The authors design a deterministic Phonemic Tokenizer that converts each syllable to its IPA representation and factorizes it into three phonological components: onset, rime, and tone. These three components are treated as a single contextual position in sequences, enabling parameter sharing across syllables with similar components while preserving syllable-level length. Non-phonological or unsupported units fall back to character-level tokens. The tokenizer is evaluated intrinsically using metrics such as Rényi efficiency, Fertility, and sequence length. They then build PhonemicBERT models for Chinese and Vietnamese, which use separate embeddings for onset, rime, and tone and reconstruct masked syllables via three prediction heads in a masked language modeling setup. The models are evaluated under controlled pretraining setups against character, subword, and SubChar baselines on standard language understanding tasks.

Result: The Phonemic Tokenizer yields very compact vocabularies: 112 entries for Chinese and 256 for Vietnamese, without any corpus-dependent learning. Intrinsic evaluations show substantially higher Rényi efficiency for both languages, exact Fertility of 1 for all entries in a standard Vietnamese syllable dictionary, and generally shorter Vietnamese token sequences compared to existing pretrained tokenizers. In downstream experiments, PhonemicBERT-Zh matches or surpasses character, subword, and SubChar tokenization baselines across diverse Chinese language understanding benchmarks. PhonemicBERT-Vi is competitive with or better than existing Vietnamese-specific and multilingual pretrained models.

Conclusion: Phonemic factorization of syllables into onset, rime, and tone provides a compact, efficient, and interpretable alternative to conventional tokenization schemes for Vietnamese and Chinese. The deterministic, IPA-based Phonemic Tokenizer achieves strong intrinsic efficiency metrics and smaller vocabularies while preserving syllable sequence length. When used in PhonemicBERT models, this representation leads to downstream performance that is competitive with, or superior to, established character- and subword-based baselines. This supports the view that linguistically grounded phonemic units can serve as effective building blocks for modern NLP systems, especially in syllable-based languages.

Abstract: Conventional tokenizers represent text as characters or statistically derived subwords, overlooking the internal phonological structure of syllables and often requiring large vocabularies. We introduce \textbf{Phonemic Tokenizer}, a linguistically motivated tokenizer for Vietnamese and Chinese that converts each syllable into IPA and factorizes it into three phonological components: onset, rime, and tone. The three components jointly occupy one contextual position, preserving syllable-level sequence length while enabling representation sharing across phonologically related syllables. Non-phonological and unsupported units are handled through character-level fallback. This deterministic design requires no corpus-dependent vocabulary learning and yields vocabularies of only 112 entries for Chinese and 256 for Vietnamese. Intrinsic evaluation shows that the tokenizer achieves substantially higher Rényi efficiency in both languages, represents every entry in a standard Vietnamese syllable dictionary with a Fertility of exactly one, and generally produces shorter Vietnamese sequences than existing pretrained tokenizers. We further instantiate the tokenizer in \textbf{PhonemicBERT}, which combines factorized component embeddings and reconstructs complete masked syllables using three prediction heads. Under a controlled Chinese pretraining setup, PhonemicBERT-Zh is competitive with or outperforms character, subword, and SubChar alternatives across diverse language-understanding tasks. PhonemicBERT-Vi also achieves competitive or superior results to established Vietnamese and multilingual pretrained models. These results establish phonemic factorization as a compact, efficient, and interpretable alternative to atomic and statistically segmented text representations.

</details>


### [39] [ArenaFlow: From Trajectory Ranking to Hierarchical Credit Propagation for Open-Ended Agent RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21378)
*Qiang Zhang,Ruixue Ding,Fanrui Zhang,Xi Chen,Boli Chen,Shihang Wang,Yinfeng Huang,Yi Zheng,Pengjun Xie,Kaipeng Zhang,Jiawei Liu,Zheng-Jun Zha*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ArenaFlow is a hierarchical credit assignment and skill-learning framework that uses tournament-based pairwise evaluation with reflective feedback to improve open-ended LLM agents, propagating rewards to pivotal steps and reusable skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning for LLM agents works well in verifiable, narrow domains but struggles in open-ended tasks where solutions are diverse and scalar rewards are hard to define. Existing pairwise preference methods reduce reward collapse but still condense rich comparative feedback into a single trajectory-level score, losing information about which intermediate steps mattered and which strategies are reusable. This prevents systematic credit assignment and skill consolidation, limiting the agent’s ability to build a library of transferable behaviors for complex, open-ended tasks.

Method: ArenaFlow introduces a hierarchical credit propagation framework built around tournament-based relative ranking of trajectories. First, it runs tournaments among agent trajectories to derive trajectory-level advantage signals. Then, for each comparison, it applies structured reflective evaluation that annotates three supervision types: (1) pivotal success steps within trajectories, (2) reusable strategy skills underlying success, and (3) attribution of when and how retrieved skills were used. At the step level, ArenaFlow propagates trajectory-level advantages down to high-confidence pivotal steps based on their tournament survival depth, thereby refining local reasoning updates. At the skill level, it estimates the utility of each skill using group-level usage attribution, and maintains a global skill memory that is updated, pruned, and retrieved in a utility-aware manner. High-utility skills are then used as priors to guide future exploration and policy improvement.

Result: Experiments on multiple open-ended agent benchmarks show that ArenaFlow improves performance over baselines that use only trajectory-level rewards or simpler pairwise preference learning. The framework results in better local reasoning, more effective skill discovery, and stronger reuse of successful strategies, leading to higher success rates and more robust behavior across tasks.

Conclusion: By decomposing tournament-based comparative feedback into step-level and skill-level signals, ArenaFlow enables more precise credit assignment and systematic skill consolidation for open-ended LLM agents. This hierarchical treatment of feedback yields a growing library of high-utility skills that guide exploration and learning, thereby overcoming limitations of conventional scalar reward or trajectory-only preference approaches in diverse, hard-to-reward environments.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has substantially improved large language model (LLM) agents in verifiable domains, but remains difficult to apply to open-ended agent tasks, where solutions are diverse and reliable scalar rewards are hard to obtain. Recent pairwise evaluation methods alleviate reward discrimination collapse by replacing pointwise scoring with relative preferences. However, they still compress rich comparative feedback into a single trajectory-level reward, obscuring decisive intermediate steps and preventing successful behaviors from being consolidated into reusable skills. We propose ArenaFlow, a hierarchical credit propagation framework for open-ended agent reinforcement learning. ArenaFlow leverages tournament-based relative ranking to derive trajectory-level reward signals. Each comparison is further equipped with structured reflective evaluation, which reveals three types of supervision: pivotal success steps, reusable strategy skills, and usage attribution of retrieved skills. At the step level, ArenaFlow propagates trajectory-level advantages to high-confidence pivotal steps according to tournament survival depth, enabling more targeted optimization of local reasoning behaviors. At the skill level, ArenaFlow estimates skill utility from group-level usage attribution and maintains a global skill memory through utility-aware updating, pruning, and retrieval. The resulting high-utility skills further serve as policy priors for future exploration. Extensive experiments validate ArenaFlow's effectiveness on open-ended agent tasks.

</details>


### [40] [Prediction Dynamics in Depth-Recurrent Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21383)
*Xinyue Luo,Fei Yu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes why depth-recurrent language models can maintain the same final answer even as internal scores continue to change, providing a geometric margin-based explanation of this stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Depth-recurrent language models iteratively refine predictions layer-by-layer or step-by-step in depth. Empirically, intermediate predictions can already match the final answer while internal scores and margins keep evolving. This raises the theoretical question: how can answers remain stable despite ongoing, sometimes substantial, score updates? Existing magnitude-based bounds on score changes are conservative and do not fully explain when and why answers are preserved at finite depth. The paper aims to provide a sharper, more interpretable characterization of these dynamics.

Method: The authors derive a sharp margin characterization of score dynamics in depth-recurrent models. They decompose the traditional conservative magnitude bound into three geometrically meaningful components: (1) a common translation term shared by all candidates, (2) the direction of updates relative to the current winning candidate, and (3) the specific pairing between each competitor’s update and its score gap to the winner. They analyze model trajectories for Huginn-3.5B and Ouro-1.4B under full answer-text scoring and label scoring, measuring how accounting for direction and pairing affects the earliest depth at which an answer is provably stable. For shared predictive distributions, they further orthogonally decompose motion into common vs. contrast components and relate the common component to candidate-set total probability mass and within-set concentration, defining corresponding “energies.”

Result: Empirically, after removing only the common translation component, many trajectories still appear conservative: score updates suggest later stability than actually observed. When the decomposition also accounts for update direction and competitor-specific pairing, the mean earliest qualifying depth (the earliest depth at which the final answer can be certified) is reduced by an additional 22.5–34.4% of the total depth beyond what translation removal alone achieves, under full answer-text scoring, using completed trajectories from Huginn-3.5B and Ouro-1.4B. Similar, though somewhat smaller, gains are observed under label scoring. In the shared-distribution setting, the common and contrast energies are shown to attenuate at different rates along depth, making it possible for the relative preference for the final answer to keep changing even as absolute update magnitudes shrink.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the stability of answers in depth-recurrent language models can be explained by the geometry and composition of score changes, not just their magnitudes. A sharp margin characterization, decomposing translation, direction, and competitor pairing, yields much tighter and more realistic explanations of when answers are preserved at finite depth. The orthogonal common-vs-contrast decomposition further clarifies how preference changes can grow while overall update magnitudes decrease.
