Together, these results provide a more accurate theoretical account of depth-recurrent refinement and help interpret internal dynamics in modern language models.

Abstract: Depth-recurrent language models refine predictions through repeated latent updates. Why can intermediate answers agree with the endpoint while their scores continue to change? We derive a sharp margin characterization that decomposes the conservatism of a magnitude bound into common translation, direction relative to the winner, and the pairing of each competitor's update with its score gap. Across Huginn-3.5B and Ouro-1.4B, accounting for update direction and competitor pairing reduces the mean earliest qualifying depth by a further 22.5-34.4% of the total depth beyond translation removal under full answer-text scoring. This retrospective comparison uses completed trajectories. Substantial contributions also occur under label scoring. For shared predictive distributions, we separate common and contrast motion orthogonally and express the common component through candidate-set mass and within-set concentration. Common and contrast energies can attenuate at different rates, allowing a growing preference-change share to coexist with shrinking absolute updates. These findings explain finite-depth answer preservation through the geometry and composition of observed score changes.

</details>


### [41] [Consistent Relexicalization of Clinical Documents using Graph-Based Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21387)
*Dipankar Das,Atri Mandal,Sandeep Singh,Tushar Shandhilya*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes G-RELIC, a graph-based contextual relexicalization method that uses LLMs and graph mappings to generate privacy-preserving yet structurally and temporally consistent clinical text, strongly improving relational and temporal coherence over prior methods without weakening privacy protections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical text must often be de-identified to protect patient privacy, but naive or independent replacement of entities (e.g., names, times, places) breaks longitudinal consistency, relationships among entities, and temporal ordering across records. This harms the usefulness of resulting datasets for research and downstream NLP tasks. The authors aim to create a relexicalization approach that more faithfully preserves the structure, relationships, and temporal patterns of real-world clinical data while still robustly masking sensitive information.

Method: The authors introduce G-RELIC (Graph Based Contextual Relexicalization with Improved Consistency), which integrates large language models with a graph-based mapping framework. They construct graphs that encode entities and their relationships across documents, then learn or enforce a one-to-one mapping from original entities to surrogate entities to maintain global consistency. Additionally, they design a deterministic temporal repositioning algorithm that adjusts temporal expressions while preserving the relative temporal structure (ordering and intervals) of events, ensuring temporal coherence across longitudinal records.

Result: On multiple real-world clinical datasets, G-RELIC is empirically shown to outperform existing relexicalization baselines. It improves relational integrity from 62.1% to 92.5% (a 30.4 percentage point gain) and temporal coherence from 46% to 91.9% (a 45.9 percentage point gain), while maintaining standard privacy metrics comparable to state-of-the-art de-identification/relexicalization methods.

Conclusion: G-RELIC effectively balances privacy protection with data utility in clinical NLP. By using graph-based entity mapping and deterministic temporal repositioning, it preserves structural, relational, and temporal consistency to a far greater degree than prior approaches, significantly enhancing the analytical utility of relexicalized clinical datasets without increasing re-identification risk.

Abstract: Relexicalization is a pivotal technique in clinical NLP, as it facilitates robust masking of sensitive information while synthesizing datasets that retain high-fidelity, real-world characteristics. However, preserving structural integrity, relational coherence, and temporal consistency during transformation remains a significant challenge. Existing approaches frequently rely on independent entity replacement, which results in clinical inconsistencies across longitudinal records. This reduces the value of such relexicalized datasets for downstream scientific analysis. To address these limitations, we introduce G-RELIC (Graph Based Contextual Relexicalization with Improved Consistency) which combines the power of LLMs with graphs. G-RELIC implements a graph-based mapping mechanism which optimizes for one-to-one correspondence between original and surrogate entities. It also introduces a deterministic temporal repositioning algorithm to preserve temporal consistency. Empirical evaluations on diverse, real-world clinical datasets validate that G-RELIC significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. G-RELIC yields a 30.4 percentage point improvement in relational integrity (62.1% to 92.5%) and 45.9 percentage point improvement in temporal coherence (46% to 91.9%) without compromising on the recognized privacy benchmarks for clinical datasets. This maximizes the analytical utility of relexicalized datasets while minimizing re-identification risk.

</details>


### [42] [Omni Demand Understanding: A Benchmark for Contextual User-Intent Inference in Multimodal Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21392)
*Qi Chen,Yunfei Chu,Haolin He,Yifan Yang,Zihan Liu,Yuxuan Wang,Ziyang Ma,Ruiyang Xu,Meng Gao,Yinsong Yan,Ling Wang,Hui Wang,Wen Huang,Yiheng Chen,Guanrou Yang,Qiuqiang Kong,Jin Xu,Xie Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Omni Demand Understanding (ODU), a new benchmark to test whether multimodal language models can correctly detect and interpret users’ actual demands from natural audio‑visual interactions, revealing large gaps in current models’ abilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most current benchmarks for AI assistants focus on how good responses are once a clear query is given, but in real-world audio‑visual interactions user demands are often implicit, underspecified, noisy, or even absent despite request-like speech. There is little systematic evaluation of whether multimodal models can first decide if a genuine user demand exists and then infer it from speech, vision, and dialogue context. This paper aims to fill that gap by defining and measuring this foundational capability.

Method: The authors formalize Omni Demand Understanding (ODU) as a multimodal contextual inference task: given an interaction stream (speech, video, and dialogue history), a model must detect whether a user demand is present and, if so, infer the user’s intent. They design ODU along five evaluation dimensions covering single-turn and multi-turn interactions. To build the benchmark ODU‑Bench, they use (1) a challenge-driven taxonomy of demand-understanding phenomena, (2) taxonomy-guided agentic video generation, (3) human-recorded interactions, followed by (4) media-grounded annotation and (5) human verification. They then evaluate 14 native multimodal LLMs on this benchmark.

Result: On ODU‑Bench, current MLLMs perform poorly at contextual demand understanding. The strongest model tested, Gemini 3.1 Pro, recovers only 44.7% of the key information that requires inference from visual, acoustic, or conversational cues. Additionally, 11 out of 14 models show false-trigger rates above 50% when exposed to interactions that contain no genuine user demand, indicating a strong tendency to misinterpret non-demand speech as actionable requests.

Conclusion: The experiments demonstrate a systematic capability gap in existing multimodal language models: they struggle both to reliably detect whether a user demand exists and to infer its content from natural, noisy, and ambiguous audio‑visual interactions. The authors propose ODU and ODU‑Bench as tools to establish and drive progress on this previously underexplored but crucial aspect of multimodal interaction—correctly understanding user demands before producing responses.

Abstract: Natural audio-visual interaction is emerging as an important interface for AI assistants, allowing users to communicate through speech and vision rather than carefully composed text prompts. However, existing benchmarks of interactive capabilities still focus primarily on response quality, leaving a more fundamental question underexplored: can a model correctly infer the user's underlying demand from complex multimodal interaction? Real-world user demands are often underspecified in speech and must be inferred from multimodal cues and dialogue history. This inference is further complicated by ambiguous or disfluent expression and noisy acoustic environments. Conversely, request-like speech may not constitute a demand to the assistant, leading to false triggers. We establish Omni Demand Understanding (ODU) as a distinct multimodal contextual inference problem: given an interaction stream, a model must detect whether a user demand is present and infer intent from multimodal and conversational context. ODU evaluates this capability along five dimensions, covering both single-turn and multi-turn interactions. We construct ODU-Bench using a challenge-driven taxonomy, taxonomy-guided agentic video generation, and human-recorded interactions, followed by media-grounded annotation and human verification. We evaluate 14 native MLLMs. Even the strongest, Gemini 3.1 Pro, recovers only 44.7% of key information that must be inferred from visual, acoustic, or conversational context. Moreover, 11 of the 14 models exhibit false-trigger rates above 50% on non-demand scenarios. These results reveal a systematic capability gap in current MLLMs' ability to infer contextual user demands. We hope ODU can establish the evaluation of a previously underexplored yet essential capability in multimodal interaction: correctly understanding user demands before generating an appropriate response.

</details>


### [43] [Talking Past the Machine: Morality, Politeness, and Alignment in Human-AI Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21401)
*Marina Mitiaeva,Lu Xiao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that current conversational AIs imitate cooperative dialogue on the surface but lack the underlying mutual adaptation that characterizes genuine human cooperation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether conversational AI engages in genuinely cooperative communication or only simulates its appearance, which has implications for how these systems should be evaluated, trusted, and designed.

Method: The authors compare 15,881 human-ChatGPT and 10,784 human-human multi-turn dialogues, using mixed-effects statistical models to examine how features related to morality, politeness, and alignment predict turn-to-turn linguistic alignment in each setting.

Result: They find a dissociation between surface cooperation and deeper social processes: AI’s moral responses seem preconfigured rather than negotiated; it expresses warmth without sensitivity to social face; and linguistic convergence decreases over time. Cooperative mechanisms that promote accommodation in human-human dialogue can have the opposite effect with AI—for instance, AI hedging is linked to reduced alignment, and moral purity framing from AI leads users to converge toward the system rather than diverge as humans would.

Conclusion: Conversational AI currently reproduces the surface patterns of cooperative communication without the mutual adaptation that grounds cooperation in human interaction. Moreover, certain mechanisms that sustain human-to-human accommodation may operate in reverse in human-AI dialogue, implying that evaluating cooperation at the level of individual turns is insufficient to ensure genuinely successful, collaborative interactions.

Abstract: Conversational AI systems produce fluent, socially appropriate responses, yet whether they participate in cooperative communication or merely simulate its surface forms remains unclear - a question central to how these systems are evaluated, trusted, and designed. This study investigates how morality, politeness, and alignment - three dimensions central to cooperative dialogue - function in human-AI interaction compared to human-human conversation. We analyze 15,881 human-ChatGPT and 10,784 human-human multi-turn dialogues, using mixed-effects models to identify which features predict turn-to-turn alignment. We observe a consistent dissociation: AI produces the surface features of cooperative communication without the underlying social architecture. Moral output appears preconfigured rather than negotiated; warmth is generated without face sensitivity; linguistic convergence declines persistently. Most strikingly, the cooperative mechanisms themselves reverse direction: hedging and softening associated with greater accommodation between humans are associated with reduced alignment when produced by AI, and purity framing associated with human divergence coincides with users converging toward the AI. Agency - giving users room to shape the exchange - is the most consistent predictor of alignment across both interaction types, while lower moral assertiveness in more recent models is not accompanied by better cooperation. Together these patterns suggest that AI reproduces the surface of cooperation without the mutual adaptation that grounds it between humans - and, more surprisingly, that mechanisms sustaining human accommodation can run in reverse with AI, suggesting a turn-level view may be insufficient for interaction-level success.

</details>


### [44] [Benchmarking Gender Bias in Machine Translation Evaluation Metrics across Occupations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21490)
*Orfeas Menis Mastromichalakis,Giorgos Filandrianos,Wafaa Mohammed,Giuseppe Attanasio,Chrysoula Zerva*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates gender bias in machine translation evaluation by analyzing how masculine vs. feminine translations are scored across multiple languages using an occupation-balanced dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gender bias in MT is known to affect both outputs and evaluation, but there is limited systematic analysis of how automatic evaluation systems and shared-task submissions treat masculine vs. feminine realizations when the source text is gender-neutral. Understanding whether evaluation systems systematically favor one gender and how this varies by occupation and language is crucial for fair and unbiased MT assessment.

Method: The authors use an occupation-balanced subset of the GAMBIT+ dataset, extended with German, containing 1,308 masculine/feminine translation pairs per target language (three per each of the 436 ISCO-08 occupational groups) for seven English→X language pairs (Arabic, Czech, Greek, Icelandic, Russian, Ukrainian, German). They analyze submissions to the WMT 2026 Automated Translation Quality Evaluation Systems Shared Task, as well as baseline systems, for both score prediction and error annotation. They then quantify the direction, magnitude, and frequency of score differences between masculine and feminine translations and relate these to occupational stereotypes.

Result: Across evaluators and languages, masculine translations tend to be assigned higher quality scores than their feminine counterparts, and occupations display bias patterns aligned with stereotypical gender associations. However, the intensity and consistency of these masculine preferences vary considerably between evaluators and target languages, showing non-uniform bias rather than a single global effect.

Conclusion: Gender bias persists in MT evaluation: masculine forms are often favored and stereotypical patterns by occupation emerge. Yet this bias cannot be fully captured by a single aggregate metric; a nuanced analysis considering multiple dimensions of evaluator behavior (e.g., per-occupation, per-language, and per-system differences) is needed to properly characterize and eventually mitigate gender bias in MT quality evaluation.

Abstract: Gender bias remains a persistent concern in machine translation (MT), affecting both generated translations and their automatic evaluation. When a source text leaves a person's gender unspecified, translations may realize that person using masculine or feminine forms, and both MT systems and evaluation metrics may exhibit systematic preferences between these alternatives despite the source providing no basis for such a distinction. We study this behavior in the WMT 2026 Automated Translation Quality Evaluation Systems Shared Task using an occupation-balanced subset of GAMBIT+. We consider seven English-source language pairs, six from the original dataset, targeting Arabic, Czech, Greek, Icelandic, Russian, and Ukrainian, and extend the original resource with German. The subset contains 1,308 masculine/feminine translation pairs per target language, with three examples for each of the 436 ISCO-08 occupational groups. We evaluate shared-task submissions and baselines for score prediction and error annotation, examining the direction, magnitude, and frequency of gender-related differences. We find an overall tendency for masculine translations to receive higher scores, as well as differences per occupation following stereotypical gender representations, although the strength and consistency of this preference vary considerably across evaluators and languages. Our results show that gender bias remains present in MT evaluation, but that capturing its extent requires looking beyond a single aggregate measure to complementary dimensions of evaluator behavior.

</details>


### [45] [MIRAGE: Multi-Perspective Creative Language Model Reasoning with Reinforcement Learning Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21554)
*Arash Lagzian,Srinivas Anumasa,Dianbo Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces MIRAGE, an inference-time framework that improves large language models on complex reasoning tasks by using multiple conceptual perspectives guided by an agent-like controller.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are strong at many language tasks but remain weak on complex mathematical, scientific, and logical reasoning. Existing techniques like basic Chain-of-Thought or prompt ensembles offer limited gains or incur large inference costs. The authors are motivated to design a more cognitively inspired, flexible reasoning process that can dynamically shift perspectives like humans do, while remaining computationally efficient for practical use.

Method: The authors propose MIRAGE (Multi-perspective Inference-time Reasoning via Agent-Guided Exploration). It consists of: (1) a Selector module that chooses and prioritizes conceptual perspectives (e.g., algebraic, probabilistic, different solution strategies) likely to be effective for a given problem, and (2) a Reasoner module that uses the LLM to sequentially attempt solutions from these perspectives. The system monitors confidence; if a single perspective yields a confident answer, it is used, otherwise the framework aggregates reasoning traces or outputs from multiple perspectives to form a final answer. MIRAGE operates purely at inference time, without retraining the base model.

Result: On benchmarks including GSM8K (grade-school math word problems), MATH500 (harder math problems), MMLU-Pro (professional-level multi-task evaluation), and Game-of-24 (arithmetic game), MIRAGE consistently outperforms baselines such as vanilla Chain-of-Thought and diverse prompting ensembles. The authors report significant accuracy gains while adding only minimal inference overhead compared to standard multi-sample or ensemble methods.

Conclusion: MIRAGE demonstrates that agent-guided, multi-perspective reasoning at inference time can substantially improve LLM performance on complex reasoning tasks without modifying model parameters. The cognitive inspiration—dynamically exploring and combining different mental perspectives—offers a scalable, practical path to more reliable reasoning behavior in real-world applications.

Abstract: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have revolutionized artificial intelligence and how human interact with AIs. Despite impressive advancements, LLMs struggle with complex mathematical, scientific, and logical tasks. Inspired by human cognitive flexibility - our ability to dynamically switch mental perspectives - we propose MIRAGE (Multi-perspective Inference-time Reasoning via Agent-Guided Exploration), a novel inference-time creative thinking framework. MIRAGE includes a Selector that prioritizes effective conceptual perspectives (e.g., algebraic, probabilistic) and a Reasoner that sequentially solves tasks until a confident solution emerges, otherwise aggregating multiple perspectives. Tested on GSM8K, MATH500, MMLU-Pro, and Game-of-24 benchmarks, MIRAGE consistently outperforms methods like Chain-of-Thought and diverse prompting ensembles, significantly boosting accuracy with minimal inference overhead, providing a scalable solution for practical applications.

</details>


### [46] [Evaluating In-Context Learning and Retrieval Strategies for Devanagari Post-OCR Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21595)
*Abhishek Bhandari,Gaurav Harit*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates large language models for training-free post-OCR correction in Hindi and Marathi Devanagari text, introducing a simple character n‑gram BM25 retrieval method (CharBM25) that, when combined with 12B+ parameter LLMs, yields strong error-rate reductions without task-specific fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-OCR text for Devanagari languages like Hindi and Marathi contains many errors, and building custom correction models typically requires task-specific training, labeled data, and GPU resources. In-context learning with general LLMs offers a way to correct OCR outputs without training, but its effectiveness for Devanagari scripts and the impact of different example-retrieval strategies had not been systematically studied. The authors aim to close this gap and to find a simple, low-compute retrieval method that works well in practice.

Method: The authors evaluate a range of LLM sizes (3B–32B, including Gemma-3-27B) on a 20,000-sentence post-OCR correction benchmark in Hindi and Marathi, spanning five news domains. They test three ways of selecting few-shot in-context examples: (1) domain-random selection (random examples from the same domain), (2) dense semantic retrieval, and (3) their proposed CharBM25, which retrieves examples by BM25 similarity computed over character n-grams of the OCR input, aiming to match similar error patterns. They compare character unigrams, bigrams, and trigrams for CharBM25 and measure performance using word error rate (WER) against gold text.

Result: Retrieval strategy strongly affects correction quality. CharBM25 with character trigrams consistently outperforms domain-random retrieval by about 2.8–4.0 percentage points absolute WER on Hindi and 2.9–3.8 on Marathi, and matches or exceeds dense retrieval while being cheaper to compute. Model scale is crucial: under CharBM25-5, Gemma-3-27B achieves WER reductions of 55.0% on Hindi and 33.3% on Marathi. Models below 8B parameters generally do not surpass the raw OCR baseline, and the smallest models (3B) can harm Marathi performance by degrading more sentences than they improve. Marathi remains more difficult to correct than Hindi at all scales, likely due to higher morphological complexity.

Conclusion: A simple character n-gram BM25 retriever (CharBM25) is an effective, GPU-free way to select in-context examples for Devanagari post-OCR correction, rivaling dense retrieval at negligible cost. When paired with sufficiently large (≥12B) general-purpose LLMs, it enables reliable, training-free OCR error correction for Hindi and Marathi without task-specific fine-tuning. The work provides the first systematic in-context learning evaluation for Devanagari OCR correction and introduces a reusable benchmark dataset for future research.

Abstract: In-context learning using Large Language Models (LLMs) offers a compelling path to training-free post-OCR correction, yet its effectiveness for Devanagari script remains entirely unexplored. We present the first systematic evaluation of LLMs (3B-32B) for post-OCR correction in Hindi and Marathi, comparing three in-context example retrieval strategies: domain-random selection, dense semantic retrieval, and our proposed CharBM25, which retrieves examples by character n-gram BM25 similarity over OCR inputs to target shared error patterns with the test sentence. Across a 20,000-sentence benchmark spanning five news domains, retrieval strategy is the decisive factor in correction quality: CharBM25 outperforms domain-random selection by 2.8-4.0pp absolute WER on Hindi and 2.9-3.8pp on Marathi, using character trigrams, which consistently outperform bigrams and unigrams. Scale dominates performance: Gemma-3-27B achieves WER reductions of 55.0% for Hindi and 33.3% for Marathi under CharBM25-5. Few-shot gains are capacity-gated: models below 8B do not reliably improve over the OCR baseline, and on Marathi the smallest models (3B) degrade more sentences than they improve. Marathi is persistently harder to correct than Hindi across all scales, reflecting its greater morphological complexity. These findings establish CharBM25 as an effective, GPU-free retrieval strategy that matches or exceeds dense retrieval at negligible computational cost, and show that combining it with a general-purpose LLM of 12B+ parameters delivers reliable, training-free Devanagari post-OCR correction without task-specific fine-tuning. Dataset: https://huggingface.co/datasets/AbhishekBhandari/Devanagari-OCR-ICL-Benchmark

</details>


### [47] [Steering LLMs Responses Towards Moral Foundations on the Norwegian MFQ-30](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21636)
*Hans Andersen,David Dichas*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates whether moral-value questionnaires produce stable, steerable “moral profiles” in LLMs and tests two interventions to move model responses toward a target human population.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Psychometric tools like the Moral Foundations Questionnaire are increasingly used on LLMs to infer their moral or value profiles, often treating these profiles as if they were stable traits that can be aligned to specific human groups. However, it is unclear whether questionnaire answers actually reflect consistent internal properties of models, whether models truly ‘engage’ with items, and how effectively we can steer their responses toward a concrete human reference population. This work addresses that gap using a nationally normed questionnaire and real human data.

Method: The authors administer the Norwegian version of the Moral Foundations Questionnaire (MFQ-30) to six open-weight language models. They compare each model’s moral foundation scores to a large sample of 1,282 Norwegian human respondents using Mahalanobis distance on the foundation profile. They implement an attention check to distinguish models that meaningfully engage with questionnaire content from those that default to generic or central answers. Two steering methods are tested: (1) prompt-level persona steering via a neutral “Nordic respondent” persona constructed without access to the human sample’s distribution, and (2) activation-level steering using one-pair ActAdd at a fixed mid-layer. They then examine how each intervention changes the models’ moral foundation profiles and their distance to the human sample mean.

Result: Half of the evaluated models pass the attention check and show content-sensitive responding to MFQ items; the other half largely produce flat or central-tendency answer patterns that appear near-human on aggregate but do not track item content. For the engaging models, adding a neutral Nordic-respondent persona in the prompt substantially reduces Mahalanobis distance to the Norwegian human mean, by 44–77%, indicating effective steering toward the target population. In contrast, the one-pair mid-layer ActAdd intervention does not selectively adjust specific moral foundations but instead tends to flatten the entire foundation profile, reducing differentiation across foundations. In at least one model, the Nordic persona not only shifts the moral profile but also causes the model to start engaging with the questionnaire where it previously did not.

Conclusion: The study shows that psychometric questionnaires do not uniformly measure stable, trait-like moral profiles in current LLMs: some models genuinely track item content, while others default to superficially human-looking but content-insensitive responses. Prompt-level persona steering can substantially align engaging models’ moral-foundation profiles with those of a target human population, whereas a simple mid-layer ActAdd approach is not fine-grained enough and instead compresses the profile. Moreover, the fact that the same persona can both change the measured profile and turn non-engagement into apparent engagement illustrates “cognitive phantoms”: steering can create the illusion of underlying psychological structure where none existed before. The authors caution that psychometric evaluations of LLMs, and interventions meant to align them, must account for engagement, response strategies, and the possibility that steering induces seemingly human traits without corresponding internal cognition.

Abstract: Recent work applies human psychometric questionnaires to large language models to elicit moral and value profiles, but it is not clear whether these instruments measure anything stable in models or whether the resulting profiles can be moved toward a target human population. We administer the Norwegian Moral Foundations Questionnaire (MFQ-30) to six open-weight LLMs and compare their foundation profiles to a sample of N = 1,282 Norwegian respondents. We test two steering interventions, prompt-level persona steering and activation-level ActAdd. Half the models engage with the questionnaire under our attention check. The other half default to flat or central-tendency outputs that look near-human on average without tracking item content. A neutral Nordic-respondent persona, written without any distributional information from the human sample, brings the engaging models 44-77% closer to the Norwegian mean in Mahalanobis $d^2$. One-pair ActAdd at a fixed mid-layer flattens the foundation profile rather than steering individual foundations. For at least one model the same persona that shifts the profile also induces engagement that was absent at baseline, a concrete instance of the cognitive phantoms that Peereboom et al. (2025) warn about.

</details>


### [48] [Chinese Competitive Debating Dataset and Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21637)
*Zongrui Yang,Haoyuan Li,Zhongsheng Wang,Zhirui Zeng,Pengqian Han,Yi Zhou,Yuting Wang,Jiamou Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build a new Chinese competitive debate dataset with professional judge annotations and define benchmarks to test how well LLMs can track debates and match human adjudication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing debate datasets lack fine-grained, interaction-level transcripts that are paired with standardized, professional adjudication conducted in real competitions. This gap makes it hard to evaluate whether LLMs truly understand interactive argumentation or align with expert judges’ reasoning. The authors want a realistic, well-annotated setting to study and benchmark LLMs’ debate understanding.

Method: They organized real Chinese-language debate competitions: 182 matches with 120 professional judges, each match judged independently by three judges under a predefined rubric. After filtering, they compiled 148 matches with 2,698 stages and 20,542 exchange units. They manually verified transcripts and segmentation and retained detailed annotations such as stage scores, match votes, best-debater ballots, and written rationales. On top of this dataset, they define three prediction tasks—winner-tendency, stage-score, and best-debater prediction—and run zero-shot evaluations of multiple LLMs on these tasks.

Result: The final dataset includes 148 matches, 2,698 stages, and 20,542 exchange units with high-quality annotations from professional judges. In zero-shot experiments, the best LLM achieves 66.2% accuracy on predicting the match winner, a Pearson correlation of 0.250 between predicted and human stage scores, and 56.8% accuracy on identifying the best debater, showing partial but limited alignment with human adjudication.

Conclusion: The new dataset and benchmark offer a realistic and fine-grained testbed for assessing LLMs’ understanding of competitive debate and interactive argumentation in Chinese. Initial zero-shot results show that current LLMs do not yet match professional judges particularly well, highlighting both the challenge of the tasks and the value of this resource for future research on debate understanding, modeling of interaction, and judge-aligned evaluation.

Abstract: Debate adjudication requires tracking how arguments develop through interaction, yet existing datasets rarely combine fine-grained debate transcripts with professional judgments collected during real competitions under a shared rubric. We introduce a dataset and benchmark for evaluating large language models' understanding of competitive Chinese-language debate at the match, stage, and speaker levels. We organized 182 matches and recruited 120 professional judges, with each match independently adjudicated by three judges using a predefined rubric. After excluding matches with incomplete records, the dataset contains 148 matches, 2,698 stages, and 20,542 exchange units, with manually verified transcripts and segmentation. It preserves original stage scores, match votes, best-debater ballots, and adjudication rationales. We define three tasks: winner-tendency prediction, stage-score prediction, and best-debater prediction. Zero-shot evaluation of multiple large language models yields a highest winner-prediction accuracy of 66.2%, a highest Pearson correlation of 0.250 between model stage scores and mean human ratings, and a highest best-debater prediction accuracy of 56.8%. The dataset and benchmark provide a testbed for studying large language models' understanding of interactive argumentation and their agreement with professional judges.

</details>


### [49] [Analysing the Linearity of Linguistic Relations in Language Model Embedding Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21655)
*Vasudevan Nedumpozhimana,Fathima Thekkekara,John Kelleher*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A framework to measure how linearly different linguistic relations are encoded in embedding spaces, showing strong linearity for inflectional/derivational relations and weaker for lexicographic/encyclopedic ones, with contextual LMs outperforming GloVe.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While word embeddings and language models are known to capture many linguistic relations, it remains unclear which specific relations are encoded in a linearly accessible way and how to compare this across models. Existing probing methods often focus on particular tasks or require complex classifiers, making it hard to isolate linear structure of relations themselves. The authors aim to provide a simple, unified, and quantitative way to assess and compare how different types of linguistic relations are represented in embedding spaces.

Method: They formalise “linear encoding” of a relation as the ability of a constrained linear mapping to approximate the transformation from one word embedding to another for that relation. Using an extended BATS dataset that includes inflectional, derivational, lexicographic, and encyclopedic relations, they construct related and unrelated word pairs and fit linear approximations under constraints. They apply this probing framework to the embedding spaces of GloVe (static embeddings) and two transformer-based models, RoBERTa and ModernBERT, and measure approximation errors as a function of relation type and cardinality (one-to-one, one-to-many, many-to-many).

Result: Inflectional and derivational relations (e.g., tense changes, morphological derivations) show near-perfect linear encodings across models, indicated by low approximation error. Lexicographic and encyclopedic relations (e.g., synonyms, hypernyms, world knowledge associations) exhibit substantially higher errors, particularly for relations involving one-to-many and many-to-many mappings. Across the board, RoBERTa and ModernBERT represent relations more linearly than GloVe, suggesting that contextual transformers organise relational geometry more linearly in their embedding spaces.

Conclusion: The proposed framework effectively identifies how linearly different linguistic relations are embedded, serving as a compact probing tool for relational geometry. It reveals a clear hierarchy: morphological relations are strongly linearly encoded, while more semantic and knowledge-based relations are less so, especially when they involve complex cardinalities. Moreover, modern contextual models tend to have more linearly structured relational encodings than classic static embeddings like GloVe. This offers a principled way to compare models and to understand what kinds of relational information are directly accessible via linear operations in their embedding spaces.

Abstract: We propose a framework to analyse how strongly different linguistic relations are linearly encoded in language model embedding spaces. We formalise linear encoding via a constrained linear approximation over related and unrelated word pairs and apply this to an extended BATS dataset covering inflectional, derivational, lexicographic, and encyclopedic relations in GloVe, RoBERTa, and ModernBERT. Our experiments show near-perfect linear encodings for inflectional and derivational relations, but substantially higher errors for lexicographic and encyclopedic relations, especially for one-to-many and many-to-many associations. We also find that RoBERTa and ModernBERT generally encode relations more linearly than GloVe. These results indicate that our framework can reveal which relational structures are most linearly accessible in embeddings, offering a compact tool for probing and comparing relational geometry across models.

</details>


### [50] [When Steering Fails in Latent Reasoning: A Latent-to-Language Transition Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21662)
*Gaoxiang Huang,Lei Qi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates why activation steering in latent chain-of-thought (CoT) representations has weaker control over language model outputs than steering explicit CoT, and identifies the latent-to-language transition boundary as the key bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Activation steering is successful for manipulating explicit chain-of-thought tokens in language models, so it is natural to extend these methods to latent (non-linguistic) CoT. However, empirical attempts show that interventions in continuous thought space lead to much weaker changes in generated text than comparable interventions in explicit CoT. This discrepancy raises the question of where and why control is lost when moving from latent reasoning to language generation.

Method: The authors compare activation steering in two settings: (1) explicit CoT, where chain-of-thought is represented as actual text tokens, and (2) latent CoT, where internal continuous representations encode reasoning without being verbalized. They measure the effect of moving hidden representations along task-related directions by comparable magnitudes in both settings, and examine (a) whether task information is still identifiable in continuous thoughts, (b) how the model’s output distribution changes at the interface between latent representations and language output, and (c) the strength and symmetry of control (bidirectional steering) in both explicit and latent CoT.

Result: They find: (1) Task information remains detectable in continuous thought representations, so the weak control is not due to loss of information. (2) There is an abrupt change in the model’s output distribution at the boundary where latent representations are converted into language, indicating a sharp transition interface. (3) Directions associated with tasks provide much weaker bidirectional control over outputs in latent CoT than in explicit CoT, even when the magnitude of intervention in representation space is similar.

Conclusion: The authors propose a "latent-to-language transition gap": interventions that meaningfully alter latent reasoning states do not reliably propagate to changes in generated language. Their analyses suggest that the main bottleneck for effective latent steering lies at the transition interface between continuous thoughts and language output. They argue that this interface should be a primary focus for evaluating and designing future methods for latent CoT steering and controllable language model behavior.

Abstract: Activation steering has become a widely used approach for controlling language models during explicit chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, motivating its extension to latent CoT. However, we find that steering continuous thoughts produces substantially weaker effects on subsequent language generation than steering explicit CoT, even when the hidden representations are moved by comparable amounts. We first show that task information remains identifiable in continuous thoughts. Hence, we hypothesize a \textbf{latent-to-language transition gap}, in which an intervention effect in latent space fails to transfer to language generation. Two further results support this hypothesis: the output distribution changes abruptly at the transition boundary, and task-related directions exert much weaker bidirectional control in latent CoT than in explicit CoT. These findings identify the transition interface as a central target for evaluating and designing future latent-steering methods.

</details>


### [51] [Rethinking Human-Aligned Evaluation: An Analysis of Semantic Metrics Beyond WER](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21663)
*Hritika Sharma,Thibault Bañeras-Roux,Alessandra Pinto,Petr Motlicek,Hyunggu Jung,Esaú Villatoro-Tello,Somang Nam*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how well common automatic speech recognition (ASR) metrics align with human judgments and finds that character error rate (CER) and semantic-distance-based metrics outperform word error rate (WER).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: WER treats all word-level errors equally, even when they do not affect meaning, raising doubts about whether it reflects human perceptions of ASR quality. The authors want to understand which automatic metrics best approximate human judgments and to provide a human-centered benchmark for ASR evaluation.

Method: The authors construct HATS-en, an English dataset with human judgments of ASR transcript quality. They then systematically compare traditional lexical metrics (WER, CER) with semantic metrics (BERTScore and SemDist), exploring different language models, layers, and pooling strategies for the latter. Agreement between metric scores and human judgments is measured and compared across configurations.

Result: WER shows the lowest agreement with human judgments among all evaluated metrics. Certain SemDist configurations achieve the highest agreement, outperforming CER and BERTScore, though no single semantic model is universally best. CER, despite being simple and cheap to compute, performs surprisingly close to the best SemDist variants.

Conclusion: WER is not an adequate proxy for human perception of ASR quality. The authors recommend shifting ASR evaluation toward CER as a primary, interpretable, and low-cost metric—especially suitable for English and morphosyllabic writing systems—and using SemDist-based semantic metrics as complementary tools for capturing meaning preservation.

Abstract: Word Error Rate (WER), the most commonly used metric for Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR), treats every lexical deviation from the reference as equally costly, regardless of whether it changes meaning. This raises the question: does WER actually track how humans judge ASR transcript quality? We introduce HATS-en, an English dataset for human-centered ASR evaluation. Using this dataset, we benchmark lexical metrics against several configurations of BERTScore and SemDist, varying the language model, layer, and pooling strategy. We find that WER agrees least with human judgment among all metrics tested, that the best-performing SemDist configurations achieve the highest overall agreement, ahead of CER and BERTScore, and that no single model is best across settings. CER, despite its simplicity and low cost, remains remarkably close to these best configurations. In line with prior recommendations, our results support shifting ASR evaluation toward CER both for English and for morphosyllabic writing systems as it is a more interpretable and low-cost metric for what evaluation should actually capture, and using SemDist as a complementary evaluation.

</details>


### [52] [PRISM-BN: A Controlled Corpus and Benchmark for Text-to-Parameterized Bayesian Network Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21673)
*Amartya Bhattacharya,Nikhil Singh,Neeti Pokhriyal,Soroush Vosoughi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces PRISM-BN, a new benchmark dataset and evaluation framework for mapping text descriptions to fully specified Bayesian Networks, enabling fine-grained assessment of structure and parameter recovery by large language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neurosymbolic AI needs systems that can translate natural language descriptions into explicit probabilistic graphical models such as Bayesian Networks. However, training and evaluating such systems requires large-scale paired data of text and fully specified BNs, which does not exist. The authors aim to fill this gap with a controlled corpus and rigorous metrics to separately measure structural and probabilistic accuracy.

Method: The authors construct PRISM-BN, a corpus of 5054 instances where each instance pairs a controlled textual description with a discrete Bayesian Network including variables, states, directed edges, root priors, and complete conditional probability tables (CPDs). They start from 50 Wikipedia-inspired backbone graphs spanning five domains. Using their PRISM pipeline, they first elicit marginal and local joint distributions, then analytically recover normalized CPDs and build reparameterized subgraphs to form the ground-truth BNs. They define an evaluation benchmark that scores models on semantic alignment of nodes and states, conditional structural accuracy of edges, and strict full-CPD agreement. Six LLMs are evaluated as extractors from text to BNs under these metrics.

Result: On the PRISM-BN benchmark, six large language models achieve Node F1 scores between 0.56 and 0.83, and conditional Edge F1 scores between 0.90 and 0.97, indicating strong recovery of variables and directed structure. However, CPD-KL divergence ranges from 1.11 to 3.14, showing that recovering full conditional probability tables remains difficult. The strong performance on conditional states and edges but weaker full-CPD agreement holds even when using independently generated GPT-5.5 references, and a human pilot study confirms the recoverability of structure and broadly similar probabilistic interpretations.

Conclusion: PRISM-BN provides a scalable, controlled resource and benchmark for learning and evaluating text-to-Bayesian-Network systems. It enables separate assessment of structural recovery and parameter estimation, revealing that current LLMs are good at inferring BN structure and qualitative probabilistic relations from text but still struggle with precise quantitative CPD estimation. This dataset and framework are intended to support future neurosymbolic AI research on grounding language in explicit probabilistic models.

Abstract: Probabilistic Graphical Models (PGMs), especially Bayesian Networks (BNs), expose directed structure and probabilistic parameters, making them natural symbolic targets for neurosymbolic AI. Yet training text-to-parameterized-BN systems requires paired text-to-BN resources unavailable at scale. We introduce PRISM-BN, a controlled corpus of 5054 BN-grounded descriptions paired with discrete reference BNs containing variables, states, directed edges, root priors, and full multi-parent CPDs across five domains. The instances are derived from 50 Wikipedia-seeded backbones, and their probabilities are internally constructed benchmark targets rather than externally validated causal estimates. PRISM-BN is built with PRISM, a marginal-first pipeline that elicits marginal and local joint distributions, analytically recovers normalized CPDs, and constructs locally reparameterized subgraphs. We define a benchmark with semantic node and state alignment, conditional structural scoring, and strict full-CPD evaluation. Across six LLM extractors, Node F1 ranges from 0.56 to 0.83, conditional Edge F1 from 0.90 to 0.97, and CPD-KL from 1.11 to 3.14. Conditional state and edge recovery remain consistently strong, whereas strict full-CPD agreement remains challenging. These trends persist with independently generated GPT-5.5 references, and a human pilot corroborates structural recoverability and similar probabilistic interpretations. PRISM-BN supports separate evaluation of structural recovery and probabilistic parameter estimation.

</details>


### [53] [CIBuzzBench: A Benchmark for Cross-Lingual Understanding of Chinese Internet Buzzwords](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21722)
*Yifan Wang,Junyu Lu,Qifan Wang,Shun Zhang,Chaozhuo Li,Jiahao Liu,Zhijun Cao,Lingbin Bu,Fanliang Bu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces CIBuzzBench, a benchmark to test how well large language models understand and translate Chinese internet buzzwords into English, especially for meaning, equivalents, and harmfulness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chinese internet buzzwords are culturally rich, non-literal, and evolve quickly. While prior work has analyzed these expressions within Chinese, it is unclear whether LLMs can accurately transfer their nuanced, culture-specific meanings into English. This gap matters both for cross-lingual understanding (e.g., translation, intercultural communication) and for safety, because harmful or offensive content is often disguised through euphemism, homophony, irony, or coded slang that may evade naive detection systems.

Method: The authors build CIBuzzBench, a benchmark of 3,001 Chinese internet buzzwords. Each item is annotated with: (1) detailed English meaning explanations, (2) English equivalent expressions, (3) semantic/pragmatic category labels, and (4) harmfulness labels. Using these annotations, they design three evaluation tasks: (a) Meaning Explanation: generating or choosing accurate English explanations; (b) Cross-lingual Equivalent Matching: selecting correct English equivalents under perturbed options; and (c) Culturally Grounded Harmfulness Detection: determining whether a buzzword is harmful based on its intended use. They then evaluate multiple state-of-the-art proprietary and Chinese LLMs under both English and Chinese prompting conditions on these tasks.

Result: Across all three tasks, current LLMs underperform. They particularly struggle with: (1) precisely capturing fine-grained, non-literal meanings of buzzwords; (2) robustly matching buzzwords to their correct English equivalents when distractor options are perturbed; and (3) reliably identifying harmful buzzwords while maintaining calibrated judgments (i.e., avoiding both misses and over-flagging). Performance gaps persist for both proprietary and Chinese LLMs, and across prompting languages.

Conclusion: CIBuzzBench reveals that multilingual LLMs still face substantial challenges in cross-lingual understanding of culturally grounded Chinese internet buzzwords, especially for nuanced semantics and safety-relevant harmfulness detection. The benchmark provides a new, publicly available resource (with dataset and code) for evaluating and improving LLMs’ handling of culturally specific, non-literal language and for developing more reliable safety evaluation methods in multilingual settings.

Abstract: Chinese social media has generated a vast and continually evolving lexicon of internet buzzwords whose meanings are often non-literal and deeply rooted in local cultural and pragmatic contexts. Existing research has primarily focused on interpreting these buzzwords within Chinese, leaving largely unexplored whether LLMs can transfer such culturally grounded knowledge across languages and accurately convey the intended meanings in English. This cross-lingual capability is also critical for safety, as harmful expressions may obscure their offensive content through culture-specific homophony, euphemism, irony, or coded language. In this paper, we investigate the ability of advanced LLMs to understand Chinese internet buzzwords across languages. To this end, we introduce CIBuzzBench, the first benchmark for cross-lingual Chinese-to-English understanding of Chinese internet buzzwords. CIBuzzBench comprises 3,001 Chinese internet buzzwords annotated with English meaning explanations, English equivalents, category labels, and harmfulness labels. Based on these annotations, we design three evaluation tasks: Meaning Explanation, Cross-lingual Equivalent Matching, and Culturally Grounded Harmfulness Detection. We evaluate representative state-of-the-art proprietary and Chinese LLMs under both English- and Chinese-prompting settings. Our results show that LLMs continue to struggle with the cross-lingual understanding of Chinese internet buzzwords, particularly in fine-grained non-literal interpretation, robust equivalent matching under option perturbations, and calibrated harmfulness detection. These findings highlight the persistent challenges posed by culturally grounded language phenomena for multilingual LLMs and safety-oriented evaluation. The dataset and code are available at https://github.com/SuperYFan/CIBuzzBench.

</details>


### [54] [Per-Aetiology Contrastive Severity Embeddings with Phonological Pseudo-Labelling for Multilingual Dysarthric Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21789)
*Bernard Muller,Antonio Armando Ortiz Barrañón,LaVonne Roberts*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper compares mixed-aetiology vs aetiology-specific multilingual dysarthria severity models built on HuBERT embeddings, showing that separate aetiology-specific label spaces perform substantially better and that adding pseudo-labelled data further improves performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multilingual dysarthria-severity assessment systems often either focus on a single disorder-language pair or pool different neurological aetiologies into a single label space, potentially conflating distinct speech-impairment patterns. There is limited controlled evidence on whether this pooling assumption hurts performance, especially in a multilingual setting. The authors aim to rigorously test if models benefit from being trained separately per aetiology and whether leveraging training-free phonological profiling to generate pseudo-labels can improve performance despite heterogeneous, low-resource clinical data across languages.

Method: The authors build four HuBERT-base contrastive embedding models that share the same backbone architecture, training procedure, corpus registry, and evaluation protocol. One model is trained on mixed aetiologies (baseline), while three are trained separately for specific aetiologies: cerebral palsy (CP), Parkinson’s disease (PD), and amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS). Training data include clinically labelled speech and ordinal pseudo-labels derived from an existing, training-free phonological profiling method. Models are evaluated on speaker-disjoint, leakage-filtered held-out subsets, with macro F1 as the main metric. For CP, additional pseudo-labelled speakers from SAP and CDSD datasets are used to quantify the impact of extra pseudo-labelled data.

Result: Across all three aetiologies, the aetiology-specific models achieve higher macro F1 than the mixed-aetiology baseline: CP 0.829 vs 0.676 (+22.6% relative), PD 0.715 vs 0.511 (+40.0%), and ALS 0.788 vs 0.596 (+32.3%). For CP, augmenting the clinically labelled dataset with 144 SAP and 44 CDSD pseudo-labelled speakers increases macro F1 from 0.786 (clinical-only CP model) to 0.829 (+4.3 percentage points). Training data cover three to seven languages per aetiology, demonstrating that improvements hold in a multilingual setting.

Conclusion: Pooling heterogeneous dysarthria aetiologies into a single label space degrades model performance; aetiology-specific models are better suited for multilingual dysarthria-severity assessment when using HuBERT-based embeddings. Incorporating pseudo-labelled data via phonological profiling can yield additional performance gains, at least for CP. The study serves as a controlled test of label-space design choices and highlights several limitations and open issues for future work, such as calibrating pseudo-labels, ensuring rigorous split hygiene to avoid data leakage, and designing confidence-thresholded deployment strategies for real-world clinical use.

Abstract: Most multilingual dysarthria-severity systems either train on a single aetiology-language pair or pool heterogeneous aetiologies into one label space. We test that pooling assumption with four matched HuBERT-base contrastive embedding models under a shared backbone, training recipe, corpus registry and held-out evaluation: one mixed-aetiology baseline and three aetiology-specific models for cerebral palsy (CP), Parkinson's disease (PD) and amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS). Training combines clinically labelled speech with ordinal pseudo-labels from a training-free phonological profiling method [1], [2]. On speaker-disjoint, leakage-filtered held-out subsets, the per-aetiology models outperform the mixed baseline across all three target aetiologies: CP (macro F1 0.829 vs 0.676, +22.6 % relative), PD (0.715 vs 0.511, +40.0 %) and ALS (0.788 vs 0.596, +32.3 %). On CP, adding 144 SAP and 44 CDSD pseudo-labelled speakers lifts macro F1 from 0.786 to 0.829 over a clinical-only CP model (+4.3 percentage points). Training data span three to seven languages per aetiology. We position this as a controlled comparison of label-space design choices and discuss pseudo-label calibration, split hygiene, and confidence-thresholded deployment as important limitations for future work.

</details>


### [55] [RheoSampling: Resolving the One-Hot Dilemma in Stochastic Dynamic-Tree Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21827)
*Qiao Hu,Yepeng Weng,Bo Zhang,Takehisa Yairi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces RheoSampling, a speculative decoding method that keeps both efficiency and true stochastic sampling for LLMs by decoupling tree construction probabilities from sampling probabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing speculative decoding methods with dynamic trees (like EAGLE-3) achieve high efficiency only under greedy decoding. When temperature > 0, their deterministic top-K expansion collapses the distribution, drastically reducing acceptance rate and breaking proper stochastic sampling. Static-tree methods preserve randomness but lose context-aware structure and efficiency. There is no method that is both dynamic-tree, context-aware, and fully stochastic without loss of correctness.

Method: The authors propose RheoSampling, which decouples the roles of probabilities used in speculative decoding. For each drafted token sampled from the model’s distribution, RheoSampling assigns: (1) a proxy probability used solely for tree expansion and global pruning, and (2) the true sampling probability used for verification to ensure unbiased stochastic decoding. Concretely, they inject a sampled token into the deterministic top-K slots used to grow the dynamic tree, but treat its probability differently for construction vs. verification. They analyze stochastic tree spaces via equivalence classes to establish losslessness, introduce an optimal-transport-based verification strategy, and add a sparse draft mechanism to align theoretical gains with real-world efficiency.

Result: Through theoretical analysis, they show that RheoSampling remains lossless: it produces exactly the same output distribution as standard stochastic decoding while exploiting speculative trees. Empirically, across various LLMs and benchmarks, RheoSampling yields higher token acceptance rates and better end-to-end speedups compared to state-of-the-art dynamic-tree speculative decoding methods under stochastic settings.

Conclusion: RheoSampling is the first dynamic-tree speculative decoding method that simultaneously supports context-aware top-K tree construction and fully stochastic sampling while remaining lossless. By decoupling probabilities for tree building and verification and using OT-based verification and sparse drafts, it improves acceptance rates and speed in practice. The framework and its equivalence-class analysis offer a general template for understanding and designing efficient stochastic tree-based decoding methods for LLMs.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by drafting multiple tokens in parallel, with tree-based methods further improving efficiency through hierarchical structures. Dynamic-tree methods such as EAGLE-3 perform well under greedy decoding via deterministic top-K expansion and global pruning. However, in stochastic decoding (T>0), this mechanism collapses the draft distribution into one-hot probabilities, causing a severe drop in acceptance rate. This creates a dilemma: dynamic-tree methods sacrifice stochastic sampling to preserve context-aware topology, while static-tree methods preserve stochastic sampling with context-agnostic structures. The issue arises because the same probability distribution is used for two conflicting tasks: constructing the tree and verifying tokens. This coupling makes direct injection of randomness challenging due to the resulting stochastic process. We resolve this by decoupling these roles: RheoSampling assigns a token sampled from the draft distribution a proxy probability for tree expansion and pruning alongside its true sampling probability for verification. Specifically, we inject a sampled token among the deterministic top-K slots and treat it with different probabilities during construction and verification, making RheoSampling the first dynamic-tree method with both context-aware top-K construction and stochastic sampling while maintaining losslessness. We establish the lossless guarantee through an equivalence-class analysis that compresses the stochastic tree space into tractable classes. An OT-based verification strategy and a sparse draft mechanism ensure that theoretical gains translate into practical efficiency. Experiments across LLMs and benchmarks demonstrate improvements in acceptance rate and speedup over state-of-the-art dynamic tree methods. This framework may provide a template for analyzing stochastic tree structures.

</details>


### [56] [Reusing Latent Speech Representations for Query-Conditioned Topic Localization in Transcripts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21844)
*Steffen Freisinger,Philipp Seeberger,Thomas Ranzenberger,Tobias Bocklet,Korbinian Riedhammer*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a method to find the most relevant sentence span in long speech transcripts for a given topic query by reusing ASR encoder states together with text embeddings, improving accuracy over text-only baselines, especially when exact span boundaries matter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long spoken transcripts are expensive to process and often contain large amounts of irrelevant information for a user’s specific information need or topic. Existing text-only methods for span localization may ignore useful acoustic or structural cues embedded in the speech signal, and running a separate audio model can be computationally costly. Hence, there is a need for an efficient way to leverage speech information to more accurately locate the transcript span that best addresses a query or topic title.

Method: They study query-conditioned topic localization, where the task is to predict the sentence span in a transcript that best answers a topic-title query. Their method reuses encoder hidden states from an automatic speech recognition (ASR) system as sentence-level representations and fuses these with standard textual embeddings of the transcript. By doing so, a lightweight span localization model gains access to speech-informed features (e.g., prosody, segmentation, structural cues) without running an additional audio encoder. They then evaluate this fused representation approach against text-only baselines on two public datasets, using boundary-matching metrics to assess how well the predicted spans align with the gold spans.

Result: Across two public datasets, the proposed approach consistently outperforms text-only baselines for the span localization task. The improvements are particularly noticeable under strict boundary-matching criteria, indicating better precision in identifying the exact start and end of relevant spans. Cross-dataset transfer experiments reveal that the gains are most pronounced for structured or semi-structured speech (e.g., talks, lectures), whereas for more spontaneous speech the benefits are smaller and mixed, suggesting that speech structure influences the utility of ASR encoder states for localization.

Conclusion: Reusing ASR encoder states as sentence-level representations and fusing them with textual embeddings is an effective and computationally efficient strategy for query-conditioned topic localization in long speech transcripts. This approach yields more accurate and precise span predictions than text-only baselines, especially when the speech is structured or semi-structured and when strict boundary accuracy is required. However, the method’s advantages are less consistent for highly spontaneous speech, indicating that further work is needed to better handle unstructured conversational audio.

Abstract: Long transcripts are costly inputs for downstream NLP systems and often contain irrelevant context. We study query-conditioned topic localization: predicting the sentence span in a transcript that best addresses a topic-title query. To improve span localization, we reuse ASR encoder states as sentence-level representations and fuse them with textual embeddings. This lets lightweight span locators exploit speech information without running a separate audio encoder. Experiments on two public datasets show consistent gains over text-only baselines, especially under strict boundary-matching criteria. Cross-dataset experiments further indicate that the benefits are strongest for structured or semi-structured speech, while gains on spontaneous speech are limited and mixed.

</details>


### [57] [Do Personality-Tuned LLMs Make Better Social Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21857)
*Tim Krabbe,Xiaodan Shi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies whether fine-tuning small open LLMs on personality-labelled data improves consistent, controllable personality role-play in social simulations, and finds little to no advantage over strong instruction-tuned baselines, except some gains in linguistic diversity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are used as agents in social simulations and social robots, where controllable and stable personalities are important for realism and human-likeness. Existing instruction-prompted LLMs can role-play but still feel "alien" and may be inconsistent in personality over time. The authors want to know if personality-aware fine-tuning on domain-relevant data can close this human-likeness gap and give better controllable, faithful personality-conditioned dialogue than prompting alone.

Method: They fine-tune two small open-weight instruction-tuned LLMs (Qwen2.5‑7B‑Instruct and Ministral‑8B‑Instruct) on a custom corpus that combines personality-labelled social media posts with dialogues, aiming to teach the models to generate dialogue conditioned on specific personalities. They then evaluate the base and fine-tuned models in multiple social interaction scenarios. Three independent LLM-based judges score personality fidelity and provide textual, evidence-based behavior interpretations. The authors also compute inter-rater agreement among the LLM judges and analyze lexical properties (e.g., diversity) of generated texts to compare fine-tuned vs baseline models.

Result: Fine-tuned models do not clearly outperform the baseline instruction-tuned models at role-playing distinct personalities; personality fidelity is not improved in a robust way. Inter-rater agreement among the three LLM judges is low, which weakens the reliability of the evaluation. In terms of general text quality, fine-tuned models are mostly on par with baselines, with notable improvements in linguistic diversity for the Qwen-based models.

Conclusion: Personality-aware fine-tuning on the used corpus does not yet provide a reliable advantage over strong instruction-tuned baselines for personality-consistent dialogue in social simulations, although the generated outputs remain usable and in some respects (e.g., diversity in Qwen) improved. Given the low agreement between LLM judges and the limited gains in personality fidelity, the authors argue that future work should focus on higher-quality, better domain-aligned personality training data and more robust evaluation methods to achieve accurate and controllable personality role-playing in LLM-based social agents.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly used in social simulations for socially interactive agents and robots, offering more flexibility than rule-based systems. However, even though they mimic human behaviour very well, there is a persistent alienness to them. This work investigates whether personality-aware fine-tuning can reduce this gap by improving the consistency and controllability of personality-conditioned dialogue generation compared with instruction prompting alone. We fine-tune two small open-weight LLMs, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Ministral-8B-Instruct, using a corpus that combines personality-labelled social media posts and dialogues to create a personality-based dialogue engine for social simulation. The resulting models are evaluated across multiple social interaction scenarios using three independent LLM judges, which assess personality fidelity and provide evidence-based behavioral interpretations. We additionally quantify inter-rater agreement and lexical characteristics of the generated dialogue. Results indicate that fine-tuned models are not better at role-playing different personalities than their respective baseline models. However, low inter-rater agreement limits the confidence with which these results can be interpreted. Concerning the quality of generated texts, fine-tuned models are mostly comparable to the baselines, with fine-tuning improving the linguistic diversity of the Qwen models. While the results appear generally usable and the baseline models offer the best overall performance, future studies should place greater emphasis on the quality and domain alignment of training data for accurate personality role-playing.

</details>


### [58] [TrialAtlas: Multi-Agent Research Organization for Clinical Trial Design and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21859)
*Jiacheng Lin,Zifeng Wang,Zheng Chen,Erick Scott,Ziwei Yang,Fanyang Yu,Sheng Zhong,Jimeng Sun*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TrialAtlas is an AI-driven, multi-agent system that supports clinical development planning by detecting trial design issues, suggesting improvements, and predicting technical/regulatory success, outperforming strong baselines and other advanced systems on a benchmark built from FDA Complete Response Letters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Drug development has an extremely high failure rate (about 90% of drugs in clinical development fail) and is very costly. Clinical development planning and estimating probability of technical and regulatory success are critical but currently rely on labor-intensive, subjective expert processes that must integrate heterogeneous evidence from clinical science, statistics, regulatory precedent, and competitive intelligence. There is a need for an automated, data-driven, and scalable system that can replicate and enhance this multidisciplinary reasoning to reduce development risk and improve decision quality.

Method: The authors propose TrialAtlas, a memory-augmented multi-agent system that emulates a research organization. It coordinates specialized agents for: (1) literature synthesis, (2) competitive clinical trial intelligence, (3) regulatory precedent analysis using historical trial and NDA data, and (4) integrated reasoning over trial design and development risks. The system is trained and grounded on historical clinical trials, regulatory outcomes, and prior NDAs. To rigorously evaluate it, the authors construct TrialAtlasBench from 291 FDA Complete Response Letters, defining three tasks: detecting trial design deficiencies, generating actionable design-improvement recommendations, and predicting the probability of technical and regulatory success. They compare TrialAtlas against strong baselines, including advanced AI research agents (e.g., DeepResearch variants), on quantitative metrics and expert judgment.

Result: On TrialAtlasBench, TrialAtlas achieves an F1 score of 50.0% for detecting trial design deficiencies, outperforming the strongest baseline by 6.1 points. For predicting technical and regulatory success, it attains 85.3% balanced accuracy and 84.7% F1, improving over the best baselines by 6.7 points in balanced accuracy and by 12.0 points in Cohen’s kappa. In expert evaluation of generated concerns, 86.4% of TrialAtlas’s concerns are deemed valid, surpassing OpenAI DeepResearch (83.1%) and Gemini DeepResearch (59.3%).

Conclusion: TrialAtlas effectively automates key components of clinical development planning by coordinating specialized AI agents and leveraging historical regulatory experience, improving both detection of trial design weaknesses and prediction of technical and regulatory success over strong baselines. The newly introduced TrialAtlasBench provides a realistic, regulatory-grade benchmark based on FDA Complete Response Letters. These results suggest that multi-agent, memory-augmented systems can meaningfully support and potentially enhance expert decision-making in drug development planning and risk assessment.

Abstract: Nearly 90% of drugs entering clinical development ultimately fail, despite billions of dollars in investment. Pharmaceutical companies therefore rely on clinical development planning (CDP) and probability of technical and regulatory success assessment to anticipate development risks, yet these decisions remain labor-intensive and subjective, requiring experts across clinical science, statistics, regulatory affairs, and competitive intelligence to jointly acquire, synthesize, and reason over heterogeneous evidence. Here, we introduce TrialAtlas, a memory-augmented multi-agent research organization for CDP that mirrors this collaborative process by coordinating specialized agents for literature synthesis, competitive trial intelligence, regulatory precedent analysis, and integrated reasoning over trial design and development risk. TrialAtlas further learns from historical clinical trials and regulatory outcomes, including prior New Drug Applications (NDAs), to ground its decisions in accumulated development experience. To evaluate these capabilities in an authentic regulatory setting, we introduce TrialAtlasBench, constructed from 291 FDA Complete Response Letters and spanning three practical tasks: detecting trial design deficiencies, recommending actionable design improvements, and predicting technical and regulatory success. TrialAtlas achieves an F1 score of 50.0% for deficiency detection, outperforming the strongest baseline by 6.1 points, and reaches 85.3% balanced accuracy and 84.7% F1 for prediction of technical and regulatory success, improving over the best baselines by 6.7 points in balanced accuracy and 12.0 points in Cohen's kappa. In expert evaluation, 86.4% of TrialAtlas-generated concerns were judged valid, compared with 83.1% for OpenAI DeepResearch and 59.3% for Gemini DeepResearch.

</details>


### [59] [NemotronLabs VoiceChat: An Open Full-duplex Speech-to-Speech Model with Tool Calling Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21967)
*Jagadeesh Balam,Travis Bartley,Edresson Casanova,Sanjay Chauhan,Chen Chen,Zhehuai Chen,Zijia Chen,Francesco Ciannella,Slyne Deng,Mikyas Desta,Harishchandra Dubey,Slim Essid,Nourchene Ferchichi,Boris Ginsburg,Mariana Graterol Fuenmayor,Negar Habibi,Kevin Hu,Anand Joseph,Viraj Karandikar,Myungjong Kim,Viacheslav Klimkov,Seelan Lakshmi Narasimhan,Lily Lee,Jason Li,Eileen Long,Ameya Mahabaleshwarkar,Aditya Malte,Adi Margolin,Sasha Meister,Valentin Mendelev,Oluwatobi Olabiyi,Ankita Pasad,Yifan Peng,Elena Rastorgueva,Jayda Ritchie,Jason Roche,Nikhil Srihari,Yuanhang Su,Yoshi Suhara,Viet Anh Trinh,Jinhan Wang,Piotr Zelasko,Hui Wang,Puhui Meng,Chaosen Zhang,Yunsheng Liu,Shawn Wang,Wenjing Li,Zhonglei He*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: NemotronLabs VoiceChat is an open, full-duplex speech-to-speech model that unifies listening, transcription, reasoning, tool-calling, and speech generation in a single streaming system, achieving strong real-time conversational performance on specialized benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing speech assistants and speech-to-speech models typically separate ASR, LLM reasoning, TTS, and tool-calling into distinct components, which introduces latency and makes it difficult to support natural full-duplex conversation where users and agents can speak over each other. The authors aim to build an open, integrated system that can maintain natural conversational timing, handle interruptions and backchannels, and support structured tool use while operating in real time.

Method: The authors design NemotronLabs VoiceChat as a unified streaming architecture that combines: (1) a streaming speech encoder; (2) a decoder-only language model with parallel output heads for agent text and structured function-call representations; (3) an auxiliary RNN-T branch for incremental user transcription; and (4) a streaming TTS decoder for speech generation. The model is trained and evaluated on multiple benchmarks, including Full-Duplex-Bench (1.0, 1.5, 3.0) and VoiceBench, focusing on pause-handling, interruption handling, backchannel behavior, and tool selection. The system is open-weight and designed for full-duplex interaction where listening and speaking occur concurrently.
