Result: On Full-Duplex-Bench 1.0, VoiceChat shows the lowest pause-handling takeover rates among open-weight systems, achieves 100% takeover when users interrupt, and obtains a 4.33/5 quality score for responses after interruptions. On Full-Duplex-Bench 1.5, it correctly resumes its response after user backchannels in 93% of cases. On VoiceBench, it reaches a normalized average score of 55.1. On Full-Duplex-Bench 3.0, it achieves an 82.5% F1 score on tool selection, though argument accuracy and full end-to-end tool use are still weaker.

Conclusion: NemotronLabs VoiceChat demonstrates that full-duplex conversational behavior, robust speech recognition and generation, general language understanding, and structured tool-calling can be effectively integrated into a single open speech-to-speech model without degrading real-time interaction quality. While tool argument accuracy and end-to-end tool execution need improvement, the system sets a strong baseline for open, real-time, full-duplex voice agents with native tool use.

Abstract: We introduce NemotronLabs VoiceChat, an open full-duplex speech-to-speech model with native tool-calling capabilities. NemotronLabs VoiceChat combines a streaming speech encoder and decoder-only language model with parallel specialized output streams for agent text and structured function calls, an auxiliary RNN-T branch for incremental user transcription, and a streaming TTS decoder. This design enables the model to listen, transcribe, reason, invoke tools, and speak within a unified streaming architecture while preserving the temporal behavior required for natural conversation. On Full-Duplex-Bench 1.0, NemotronLabs VoiceChat achieves the lowest pause-handling takeover rates among evaluated open-weight systems, 100\% takeover following user interruptions, and a 4.33/5 post-interruption response-quality score. On Full-Duplex-Bench 1.5, it resumes its response after user backchannels in 93\% of cases. NemotronLabs VoiceChat obtains a 55.1 normalized average on VoiceBench and, on Full-Duplex-Bench 3.0 (FDB 3.0), achieves 82.5\% tool-selection F1, while argument accuracy and end-to-end tool execution remain areas for improvement. These results demonstrate that full-duplex interaction, speech recognition and generation, general language capabilities, and external tool use can be integrated in a single open speech-to-speech model without sacrificing real-time conversational behavior.

</details>


### [60] [DiaVLo: Diagnosing Behaviours of Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22008)
*Lorenzo Corti,Jie Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DiaVLo is a diagnostic framework for vision-language models that specifies, detects, and explains their behaviours, highlighting alignments and misalignments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models can exhibit both desired and harmful behaviours, but there are few systematic methods to characterize and verify these behaviours and understand how they arise internally. Reliable deployment requires tools that can both describe what behaviours a model exhibits and connect these behaviours to underlying concepts in the model.

Method: DiaVLo combines human curation with the generative capabilities of VLMs to construct behaviour specifications—formalized descriptions of both desired and observed behaviours. It then compares these specifications to surface alignments and misalignments, and applies causal analysis to estimate which internal concepts and representations in the VLM most strongly influence particular behaviours. The framework is tested on multiple open-source VLMs under both classification and free-form generation settings.

Result: Across several open-source VLMs, DiaVLo produces behaviour labels that correlate with traditional model performance metrics while offering richer interpretive context. It successfully uncovers instances of clearly aligned and clearly misaligned behaviours, as well as systematic patterns in how models perceive, structure, and prioritize different concepts.

Conclusion: DiaVLo is an effective diagnostic framework for analyzing and explaining vision-language model behaviours. It not only detects whether behaviours are aligned or misaligned with desired specifications, but also identifies influential internal concepts that drive these behaviours, thereby providing actionable insights for understanding and improving VLM alignment and robustness.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) rely on storing and transferring appropriate information across their sub-components. Verifying that the VLMs exhibit desired behaviours, while avoiding harmful ones, is central to their reliable deployment. Yet, methods that identify VLM behaviours remain scarce. We present DiaVLo, a diagnostic framework that leverages human curation and VLMs' generation capabilities to construct specifications of desired and observed VLM behaviours, surfacing potential misalignments. Beyond this, DiaVLo also provides causal estimates to identify the most influential concepts steering VLM behaviours. We evaluate DiaVLo on several open-source VLMs under both classification and generation conditions. Our experiments show that DiaVLo produces behaviour labels that correlate with model performance and provide context for measured performance. DiaVLo surfaced behaviours that are clearly aligned and misaligned, alongside patterns in how VLMs perceive, organise, and prioritise concepts.

</details>


### [61] [Moral Entropy: Auditing Bias and Uncertainty in Moral Judgment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21992)
*Maciej Skorski*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a Bayesian framework, Moral Entropy, to treat annotator disagreement in computational ethics as informative uncertainty rather than noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing computational ethics pipelines collapse annotator disagreement on moral content (e.g., via majority or any-annotator rules), implicitly treating it as noise. This hides important information about true disagreement (aleatoric uncertainty) and about annotation quality or sufficiency (epistemic uncertainty), and may bias models and evaluation. The authors want a principled way to represent and audit this uncertainty.

Method: They introduce Moral Entropy, a Bayesian framework that maintains a full posterior distribution over the latent true label instead of a single aggregated label. They decompose posterior entropy into aleatoric and epistemic components, and then use this calibrated posterior as a ground truth to audit standard aggregation rules. They evaluate heuristic rules (any-annotator, majority, two-vote) against the posterior using entropy-based metrics (cross-entropy/KL, Brier score, expected calibration error) across three corpora and fifteen discourse domains.

Result: Auditing shows substantial, systematic bias in common aggregation rules. The any-annotator rule disagrees with the calibrated posterior on ~30% of items, with pooled errors dominated by false positives, while at the moral foundation (MFTC) level the pattern can invert (e.g., mean FPR 19.9% and FNR 38.9%). Majority and two-vote rules severely under-detect moral content, missing 63–83% of true positives according to the posterior.

Conclusion: Annotator disagreement in moral labeling carries meaningful signal that should be explicitly modeled rather than collapsed. Moral Entropy provides a Bayesian, uncertainty-aware calibration framework that can serve as a more faithful ground truth and reveals biases in standard label aggregation heuristics, with important implications for building and evaluating computational ethics systems.

Abstract: Most work in computational ethics treats annotator disagreement on moral content as noise to be voted away, collapsed into majority vote or the more permissive any-annotator rule the moment a single annotator flags an item. We argue this uncertainty should instead be modeled and learned from.
  We introduce Moral Entropy, a Bayesian framework that keeps a full posterior over the true label and decomposes its entropy into aleatoric uncertainty (irreducible disagreement about the moral content) and epistemic uncertainty (from insufficient or noisy annotation) -- and lets any heuristic consensus rule be audited against a calibrated ground truth via entropy methods such as cross-entropy/KL, Brier score, and expected calibration error.
  Across three corpora and fifteen discourse domains, auditing the standard aggregation rules against this posterior reveals bias that no current pipeline reports: the any-annotator rule disagrees with the calibrated posterior on roughly 30% of items -- pooled, almost entirely false positives, though the errors invert at the foundation level (19.9%/38.9% mean FPR/FNR on MFTC) -- while the stricter majority and two-vote rules miss 63-83% of true positives.

</details>


### [62] [RecreationWorld: Scalable and Verifiable Environments for Hybrid Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22000)
*Shuai Bai,Jiayong Deng,Yikun Fu,Chang Gao,Xuhao Hu,Mianqiu Huang,Yizhen Jiang,Yuheng Jing,Dehui Kong,Keliang Li,Ning Li,Wanli Li,Dayiheng Liu,Dunjie Lu,Changwei Luo,Que Shen,Zheyuan Wang,Zijian Wang,Jie Wu,Gao Wu,Zhihui Xie,Rui Xie,Haiyang Xu,An Yang,Jiakang Yuan,Yanming Zhang,Jiajun Zhang,Xi Zhang,Zhenru Zhang,Zhuo Zhen,Mingkang Zhu,Bowen Zhou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces RecreationWorld and RecreationBench, a cross-platform framework and benchmark for training and evaluating hybrid computer-use agents that combine graphical interaction with software development by recreating reference applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing computer-use agents have evolved along two largely separate paths: GUI interaction agents and agents that operate primarily through coding and command-line interfaces. However, real-world digital tasks interleave these modes tightly, requiring agents that can both explore interfaces and write code in an integrated way. There is also a lack of standardized, reproducible, and cross-platform benchmarks that reflect this hybrid nature and provide execution-grounded feedback. The paper aims to fill this gap by creating an environment where agents must infer and recreate the behavior of real applications across platforms, enabling better training signals and more realistic evaluation of hybrid capabilities.

Method: The authors design RecreationWorld, a five-platform framework (Ubuntu, macOS, Windows, Android, Web) in which an agent is given a running reference application and must discover its behavior and recreate a faithful implementation without a prescribed workflow. The environment supplies native GUI control and coding tools, and uses the reference as an oracle for hidden behavioral tests, yielding execution-grounded rewards. They generate large-scale trajectories by running agents on high-quality open-source applications and then train models on these trajectories. For evaluation, they construct RecreationBench, a suite of 250 diverse, held-out tasks with reference-grounded programmatic and visual assertions covering different depths of interaction. Each assertion is validated against the reference and via human review, and the suite is frozen for automatic scoring.

Result: Models trained with trajectories from RecreationWorld show improved performance across five out-of-distribution coding and hybrid computer-use benchmarks and demonstrate better tendencies to visually verify rendered outputs, suggesting the learned skills transfer beyond the recreation setting. On RecreationBench, GPT-6 Astra achieves the best reported score at 58.1% overall, but it fully passes all programmatic tests on only 2.8% of tasks, indicating substantial remaining difficulty. Agents are observed to more reliably reproduce static interface structures than dynamic interactions or computed outputs, and the applications they generate tend to be smaller and more monolithic compared to their reference implementations.

Conclusion: Hybrid GUI-and-code computer-use remains far from solved: even the strongest current agents struggle with reliably reproducing complex behaviors and passing comprehensive programmatic tests, despite modest benchmark scores. RecreationWorld and RecreationBench provide a scalable, reproducible, and cross-platform testbed with execution-grounded feedback that can both train and rigorously evaluate such agents. The framework exposes a clear gap between copying UI appearance and faithfully reproducing interactive and computational behavior, and the released environments and test suites are intended as community resources to drive progress on more capable, general-purpose computer-use agents.

Abstract: Computer-use agents (CUAs) have advanced along two separate lines: graphical interaction and software development through code and the command line. Real digital work requires both, interleaved rather than stacked end to end. We study hybrid CUAs that autonomously decide when to explore an interface, implement software, and run and visually verify their artifacts. We introduce RecreationWorld, a five-platform framework built around recreation: given a running reference, an agent must discover its behavior and build a faithful implementation with no prescribed workflow. RecreationWorld provides reproducible environments on Ubuntu, macOS, Windows, Android, and Web, plus a unified harness with native GUI control and coding tools. The running reference serves as an oracle for hidden behavioral tests, providing execution-grounded rewards. We scale trajectory generation with high-quality open-source applications. Models trained on these trajectories improve across five out-of-distribution coding and hybrid computer-use benchmarks and more frequently verify their rendered outputs, providing evidence of transfer beyond recreation. For held-out evaluation, we introduce RecreationBench, comprising 250 diverse tasks across domains and platforms. Reference-grounded programmatic and visual assertions cover action-conditioned outcomes at multiple interaction depths; each is validated on the reference and by human reviewers before the suite is frozen for automatic scoring. GPT-6 Astra leads at 58.1% overall, but passes all programmatic tests on just 2.8% of tasks. Agents reproduce static interface structure more reliably than interactions and computed outputs, while generated applications remain smaller and more monolithic than their references. We release the benchmark, environments, and test suites.

</details>


### [63] [QuranicMMLU: A Cognitively-Aware Benchmark for Evaluating Generative AI Solutions on Quranic Linguistic Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22038)
*Rawan El Ghali,Umm Kulsoom,Anas Madkoor,Dima Faris Alsaudi,Roaa Abdelmagid,Roaa Ibrahim,Raghad Mousa,Hamza Aljaji,Abdullah Khanafer,Abdallah Alkanani,Salah Feras Alali,Rawan Khaled Mohamed,Ehsaneddin Asgari*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: QuranicMMLU is a new benchmark to test generative AI on Quranic Arabic using a detailed linguistic and cognitive framework, revealing gaps between multiple-choice and open-ended performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Quranic NLP benchmarks mostly focus on generic QA or retrieval and do not systematically test fine-grained linguistic skills, cognitive difficulty, or verse complexity. The authors want a more rigorous, linguistically principled way to evaluate how well models truly understand Quranic Arabic at multiple levels and difficulty tiers.

Method: They design a five-pillar taxonomy (Phonology, Morphology, Syntax, Semantics, Pragmatics) with 31 detailed subcategories, including tajwid, root-and-pattern morphology, occasions of revelation, and cross-surah coherence. For each subcategory, they create questions that are stratified by Bloom’s taxonomy of cognitive levels and by verse perplexity (as a proxy for difficulty). Items are instantiated in both open-ended and multiple-choice formats. Large language models are first used as judges to propose answers and scores; these annotations are then manually reviewed and corrected. They compile a dataset of 980 high-quality, human-validated questions and use it to benchmark 12 different AI systems.

Result: The final benchmark contains 980 human-reviewed items, each with both open-ended and multiple-choice variants. When testing 12 systems, the model specialized in Islamic/Quranic content performs best overall. Across systems, multiple-choice accuracy is relatively high (average 84%), while open-ended response quality is notably lower (average 60%). Despite reasonably strong agreement between rankings from the two formats (Kendall’s τ = 0.73), the multiple-choice setting masks some model weaknesses that are exposed when answer options are removed.

Conclusion: QuranicMMLU provides a structured, linguistically grounded benchmark for assessing generative AI on Quranic Arabic that accounts for linguistic phenomena, cognitive challenge, and verse difficulty. The discrepancy between multiple-choice and open-ended performance shows that traditional accuracy metrics can overestimate model competence, underscoring the need for open-ended evaluation in this domain and establishing QuranicMMLU as a stricter, more informative testing framework for Arabic NLP on Quranic text.

Abstract: We introduce QuranicMMLU, a benchmark for evaluating generative AI on Quranic Arabic across multiple dimensions of linguistic complexity. Existing Quranic benchmarks center on general question answering and semantic retrieval, without probing specific linguistic competencies or stratifying by cognitive demand and verse difficulty. We construct a five-pillar Quranic taxonomy spanning Phonology, Morphology, Syntax, Semantics, and Pragmatics, with 31 leaves covering phenomena from tajwīd and root-and-pattern morphology to occasions of revelation and inter-surah coherence. For each leaf we generate questions stratified by Bloom's cognitive level and verse perplexity, then have LLM as a judge to independently answer and score every item and route the annotations to manual review. The resulting dataset comprises 980 human-reviewed questions, each issued in both open-ended and multiple-choice form. We benchmark 12 systems on these items and find that the Islamic-specialized model leads, yet every system scores higher on multiple-choice accuracy (average 84%) than open-ended answer quality (average 60%): the two rankings agree closely (Kendall's τ=0.73), but multiple-choice scoring hides failures that surface only once answer choices are removed. QuranicMMLU thus offers a rigorous, linguistically grounded framework for evaluating Arabic NLP in the Quranic domain.

</details>


### [64] [An Interpretable Memory Decision Controller for LLM Agents Based on Three-Signal Complementarity: Decoupling Confidence and Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22043)
*Yiming Zhang,Jinghong Zhang,Haoran Zhao,Yiren Ma,Chunlei Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a zero-parameter controller, the Memory Decision Layer (MDL), that decides whether retrieved memories in RAG should be trusted, significantly reducing hallucinations, especially under conflicting memories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory systems for LLMs focus on efficient retrieval, but pay little attention to deciding whether retrieved memories are trustworthy. Under conflicting or adversarial memories, standard RAG pipelines blindly inject retrieved information, which can increase hallucination rates beyond those of models without external memory. There is a need for a principled, efficient mechanism to assess and control the trust placed in retrieved memories, especially in high-risk tasks.

Method: The authors design the Memory Decision Layer (MDL), a zero-parameter, white-box controller placed between retrieval and generation in RAG. MDL uses a three-signal complementary encoder that integrates (1) relevance of retrieved memories, (2) reliability/consistency among them, and (3) task risk. Using QR-based orthogonal subspace projection, it builds an interpretable geometric representation of these signals plus a meta-working-memory signal. MDL then explicitly decouples confidence from consistency, introduces a risk inversion mechanism, and allows explicit abstention from using memory when it is deemed untrustworthy. The whole module operates via deterministic geometric operations without training and incurs minimal latency (~0.14 ms per decision).

Result: On multiple mainstream LLMs and open-source datasets, MDL substantially reduces hallucination under conflicting memories. Quantitatively, it cuts hallucination rates by about 56.04% in general conflicting-memory scenarios and nearly eliminates hallucinations in high-risk scenarios, while adding negligible computational overhead compared with embedding retrieval and being orders of magnitude faster than LLM-based self-evaluation calls.

Conclusion: Trust decisions over retrieved memories are crucial for safe and reliable RAG. The proposed MDL provides an effective, interpretable, and extremely efficient zero-parameter controller that can be plugged into existing RAG pipelines to assess memory trustworthiness and selectively abstain from using dubious memories. This reduces hallucinations—especially when external memory is conflicting—demonstrating that memory decision mechanisms, not just better retrieval, are key to robust LLM memory systems.

Abstract: Memory systems for large language models have focused predominantly on efficient retrieval, whereas the decision of whether retrieved memories should be trusted has received comparatively little attention. When the memory store contains conflicting positions, standard retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) blindly injects memories and amplifies hallucinations: in models susceptible to memory injection, the RAG hallucination rate under conflicting memories is markedly higher than that of a memory-free baseline. Inspired by memory signaling mechanisms in the prefrontal cortex, we propose the Memory Decision Layer (MDL), a zero-parameter memory decision controller situated between the retrieval and generation stages. Its core is a three-signal complementary encoder that fuses relevance, reliability, and task risk through QR-based orthogonal subspace projection and a meta-working-memory signal into an interpretable decision representation that quantifies the trustworthiness of retrieved memories. Building on this encoder, MDL explicitly decouples confidence from consistency and introduces risk inversion and explicit abstention. Evaluations on mainstream large language models and multiple open-source datasets show that MDL reduces the hallucination rate under conflicting memories by about 56.04% in general scenarios and approaches zero hallucination in high-risk scenarios. The controller is fully white-box: it relies purely on geometric operations, requires no trained parameters, and adds only about 0.14 ms per decision -- roughly 50x faster than the embedding-retrieval step that precedes it and four to five orders of magnitude faster than an LLM self-evaluation call.

</details>


### [65] [Cross-sector generalization of accident-process role classification in occupational accident narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22081)
*Aho Yapi,Pierre Latouche,Arnaud Guillin,Yan Bailly*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how well machine-learning models trained to classify parts of occupational accident narratives in one sector (construction) generalize to other sectors, finding that task-specific adaptation of language models yields strong, transferable performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Occupational accident narratives written in free text contain rich, detailed information essential for understanding accidents and preventing future ones, but their unstructured nature makes large-scale analysis difficult. Existing automated coding systems may struggle to generalize because terminology and writing styles differ across industrial sectors and organizations. The authors aim to test whether a role-classification system trained on one sector can reliably structure narratives from other sectors without additional tuning, which is crucial for scalable, cross-sector accident analysis and risk prevention.

Method: The authors build an expert-annotated French corpus where each factual unit in an accident narrative is assigned one of four roles: work situation (A0), explicitly reported unfavourable condition (A1), accident event or deviation (B), and reported consequence (C). They train role classifiers using 42,244 factual units from 6,040 construction-sector narratives only. Multiple modeling strategies are compared: using frozen pretrained language representations, task-specific fine-tuning of pretrained models, and fully supervised representation-learning approaches. The trained models are then evaluated, without retraining or tuning, on three unseen target corpora from metallurgy, chemistry–plastics, and an independently collected company dataset. Performance is measured primarily via balanced accuracy, and stability is assessed across repeated training runs.

Result: Task-specific adaptation methods—fine-tuning pretrained models or learning supervised representations—consistently outperform approaches that rely on frozen pretrained representations when transferring to new sectors. The top three task-adapted strategies achieve very similar and robust performance, with average balanced accuracies between 85.6% and 85.8% across the three out-of-domain corpora. This indicates strong cross-sector generalization despite domain shifts in terminology and writing style.

Conclusion: Accident-process role classifiers trained on construction narratives can successfully generalize to other industrial sectors when models are adapted to the specific classification task. Task-specific adaptation of language representations is crucial for effective cross-domain transfer, outperforming frozen embeddings. The demonstrated balanced accuracies around 86% across diverse unseen corpora suggest that robust, transferable assisted-coding systems are feasible. Such systems can reliably structure heterogeneous occupational accident narratives and thereby support expert review and cross-sector occupational risk prevention analyses.

Abstract: Occupational accident narratives contain valuable information about work situations, unfavourable conditions, accident events, and their consequences. Automatically structuring these narratives can facilitate large-scale accident analysis and support occupational risk prevention. However, the terminology and writing styles used to describe accidents vary considerably across sectors and organisations, raising questions about the ability of automated coding systems to generalize beyond their training domain. In this paper, we evaluate the cross-sector generalization of accident-process role classification in French occupational accident narratives. We construct an expert-annotated corpus in which factual units are classified into four roles: work situation (A0), explicitly reported unfavourable condition (A1), accident event or deviation (B), and reported consequence (C). The role classifiers are developed and selected exclusively on 42,244 factual units extracted from 6,040 construction-sector narratives and are then evaluated on unseen corpora from the metallurgy and chemistry--plastics sectors, as well as on an independently collected company corpus, without retraining or target-domain tuning of the role classifier. We compare frozen pretrained representations with task-specific fine-tuning and supervised representation-learning strategies. The results show that task-specific adaptation consistently improves cross-domain transfer over frozen representations. Across repeated training runs, the three leading task-adapted strategies achieved average balanced accuracies between 85.6% and 85.8% across the three target corpora. These findings support the development of transferable assisted-coding systems capable of consistently structuring heterogeneous occupational accident narratives for expert review and cross-sector prevention analysis.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [66] [RBS-Attention: Radius-Bounded Sparse Prefill for Long-Context Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20971)
*Chuxu Song,Jiuqi Wei,Zhencan Peng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces RBS-Attention, a training-free sparse-prefill method that mitigates mean dilution in long-context LLMs, greatly speeding up prefill while maintaining near-dense accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prefill in long-context LLM inference is a major bottleneck because dense self-attention must process very long prompts before generation, making inference slow and costly. Existing sparse block selection methods rely on block centroids, which can miss important tokens when relevant tokens are diluted among many irrelevant ones (mean dilution), degrading model quality. There is a need for a sparse attention mechanism that is faster yet robust to this failure mode without requiring retraining.

Method: The paper proposes RBS-Attention, a training-free sparse-prefill attention scheme with two selection branches. The centroid base branch selects blocks based on average (centroid) relevance, as in standard block-sparse methods. A complementary rescue branch uses the maximum key-block radius and its distribution conditioned on prompt, layer, and head to detect blocks that might be underestimated due to mean dilution. Each branch applies its own threshold to choose blocks, and their masks are combined to form the final block selection while retaining compatibility with block-sparse FlashAttention kernels. The method involves radius-adaptive, dual-branch selection that can be tuned via thresholds and block-size, without modifying model weights.

Result: On H100 GPUs with Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507-FP8 at 128K context, RBS-Attention yields a 20.65× standalone prefill-attention speedup, 11.92× vLLM prefill-attention speedup, and 5.97× end-to-end time-to-first-token speedup. On the dense Qwen3-32B model, it attains 88.65 RULER accuracy compared to 89.52 for dense attention, indicating minimal quality loss. Additional evaluations on LongBench-v2, InfiniteBench, and Video-MME further validate quality. Supporting experiments analyze how many relevant tokens are retained, compare different block selectors at equal sparsity, and study the impacts of block size, thresholds, and memory usage.

Conclusion: Radius-adaptive dual-branch block selection (RBS-Attention) is an effective, training-free approach to reducing prefill attention cost in long-context LLMs while preserving model quality. By combining centroid-based selection with a rescue mechanism informed by key-block radius statistics, it mitigates mean dilution, maintains compatibility with efficient block-sparse FlashAttention implementations, and delivers large speedups with small accuracy degradation across multiple long-context benchmarks.

Abstract: Long-context large language model inference is increasingly limited by prefill, where dense self-attention processes the entire prompt before generation begins. Sparse block selection can reduce this cost, but a block centroid may hide a highly relevant token among many irrelevant ones. We call this failure mode mean dilution and propose RBS-Attention, a training-free sparse-prefill method with two complementary selection branches. A centroid base branch captures average relevance, while a rescue branch uses the maximum key-block radius and its prompt-, layer-, and head-dependent distribution to identify blocks at risk of underestimation. Independently thresholding the two branches and combining their masks controls the contribution of rescue blocks while preserving regular block-sparse FlashAttention execution. On H100 GPUs, RBS-Attention achieves 20.65$\times$ standalone prefill-attention speedup, 11.92$\times$ vLLM prefill-attention speedup, and 5.97$\times$ end-to-end time-to-first-token speedup at 128K on Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507-FP8. On the dense Qwen3-32B model, it obtains 88.65 overall RULER accuracy versus 89.52 for dense attention; LongBench-v2, InfiniteBench, and Video-MME provide additional quality evaluation. Supporting experiments measure actual retention, compare selectors at matched density, and characterize block-size, threshold, and memory behavior. Together, these results support radius-adaptive dual-branch selection as an effective approach to long-context prefill.

</details>


### [67] [Attention-Aware Routing: Coupling Routing and Attention in MoEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20974)
*Despoina Kosmopoulou,Anastasios Tsetsilas,Efthymios Georgiou,Giannis Karamanolakis,Swastik Roy,Alexandros Potamianos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They enhance Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) routing by making it attention-aware, using only routing training while freezing the transformer, leading to better reasoning performance and insights into how routing and attention interact across layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard MoE routers decide expert assignments from token hidden states alone, which provides only limited and entangled contextual information. The authors want to exploit richer context signals—specifically attention patterns—without retraining the full model, to both improve performance (e.g., on reasoning tasks like GSM8K) and better understand how routing interacts with attention and depth in MoE LMs.

Method: They introduce Attention-Aware Routing (AAR), which augments the router input with temporal and spectral features derived from a sliding window of attention weights, representing a summary of the model’s contextual state that is disentangled from hidden states. The base transformer (including attention) is fully frozen; only routing parameters are trained, so that any effects can be attributed to routing changes. They apply AAR at different layers and analyze its impact on attention, generation behavior, and task performance.

Result: On OLMoE, AAR improves GSM8K performance by +3.37 percentage points over a baseline where only the original router is supervised fine-tuned. They empirically show that adjusting routing at layer l modifies the residual stream in a way that amplifies attention sinks at layer l+1, effectively reshaping attention patterns without modifying attention weights directly. AAR also reduces long, diverging generations on incorrect answers (shorter wrong outputs) while keeping the length of correct answers roughly unchanged. They find strong depth sensitivity: applying AAR across all layers can hurt factual retrieval, while applying it only in deeper layers sustains gains in mathematical reasoning.

Conclusion: Attention-informed routing is an effective and targeted way to improve MoE language models, especially for reasoning tasks, without changing the underlying transformer weights. Routing and attention form a coupled circuit, where routing modifications propagate via the residual stream to reshape attention indirectly. The depth at which AAR is applied is crucial: deeper-layer routing helps reasoning but broad application can harm factual retrieval. Thus, layer-selective AAR provides both a practical improvement technique and a probing tool to study where attention carries routing-relevant information across depth.

Abstract: In Mixture-of-Experts language models, the router typically selects and weights experts based on the token's hidden state, utilizing limited contextual information. We propose Attention-Aware Routing (AAR), which augments the router with temporal and spectral features extracted from a sliding window of attention weights that represent a summary of the model's contextual state, disentangled from the hidden state. Keeping the base transformer entirely frozen, we train only the routing parameters, isolating routing as the sole variable. AAR improves GSM8K by +3.37 pp over a routing-only SFT baseline on OLMoE. Beyond performance, we show that routing and attention form a coupled circuit: routing changes at layer l propagate through the residual stream to amplify attention sinks at layer l+1, reshaping attention without any direct update to the attention mechanism itself. Further, AAR reduces long diverging generation, with incorrect answers getting shorter, while correct answers remain unchanged in length. Finally, AAR is strongly depth-sensitive: applying it indiscriminately across layers can degrade factual retrieval, whereas mathematical reasoning gains persist when it is introduced deeper in the network. This sensitivity exposes a retrieval--reasoning tension across depth and makes layer-selective AAR a controlled probe of the routing-relevant information carried by attention at different layers.

</details>


### [68] [CaLR: Causal Latent Revision for Robust Diffusion Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20981)
*Wei Cai,Jian Zhao,Yuchen Yuan,Xuelong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Causal Latent Revision (CaLR), a framework that improves reasoning in language models by combining the strengths of autoregressive and diffusion language models via constrained latent optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive models tend to be locally greedy and can get stuck in suboptimal reasoning paths, while diffusion language models lack a strict causal structure that is important for step-by-step reasoning tasks. The authors aim to design a system that maintains strong global coherence and parallel generation (like DLMs) while enforcing causal, logically consistent reasoning (like well-structured AR models).

Method: The authors propose CaLR, which reformulates reasoning as constrained optimization in a latent space. They introduce a causal topology matrix (CTM) derived from an expert model to encode causal/structural dependencies between reasoning steps. Using implicit differentiation, CaLR performs gradient-based "thought revision" on intermediate latent representations during parallel generation so that outputs satisfy the causal constraints encoded by the CTM. This enables dynamic self-correction of intermediate steps without abandoning the parallel generative nature of DLMs.

Result: CaLR achieves state-of-the-art performance among diffusion language models on complex reasoning benchmarks and outperforms strong autoregressive baselines. It also shows better robustness and reliability in constrained reasoning tasks such as Sudoku, where logical consistency is critical.

Conclusion: By casting reasoning as constrained latent optimization and leveraging a causal topology matrix with gradient-guided revision, CaLR combines the benefits of diffusion and autoregressive language models. This yields more logically consistent, self-correcting reasoning trajectories and leads to SOTA results on challenging reasoning and constraint-satisfaction benchmarks.

Abstract: Autoregressive (AR) models suffer from local greediness, while diffusion language models (DLMs) often lack the strict causal structure required for reasoning. To combine the advantages and overcome the drawbacks of the dual, we propose Causal Latent Revision (CaLR), a framework that reformulates reasoning as constrained latent optimization. By adopting a causal topology matrix (CTM) from an expert model and implicit differentiation, CaLR performs gradient-guided ``thought revision" to enforce logical consistency, enabling dynamic self-correction of intermediate steps during parallel generation. Empirically, CaLR achieves SOTA DLM performance on complex benchmarks, surpassing strong AR baselines and demonstrating superior robustness in constrained tasks like Sudoku.

</details>


### [69] [LoRA Enhanced Contrastive Learning with SAS Vision Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21061)
*Dan Zimmerman,Frank E. Bobe,Amelia L. McCormack,Matthew Cook,Gregory D. Vetaw*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They adapt a large pretrained DINOv3 Vision Transformer to sonar target recognition using a parameter-efficient, three-stage fine-tuning pipeline and find that a single LoRA-based adaptation stage yields most of the gains, while later refinement stages add negligible benefit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Synthetic aperture sonar automatic target recognition is important but hard because target images are scarce, backgrounds are cluttered, and human assessment is costly. Existing deep learning models struggle to generalize from natural-image pretraining to underwater acoustic imagery and to distinguish true targets from look-alike clutter, so there is a need for adaptation methods that are both data-efficient and parameter-efficient.

Method: They take a DINOv3 Vision Transformer pretrained on natural images and adapt it to SAS ATR in three stages. Stage 1 freezes the ViT backbone and adds Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) modules, training only a tiny fraction of weights to bridge the domain gap to sonar imagery. Stage 2 applies hard-negative mining, preferentially training on clutter samples such as rocks and seabed structures that mimic man-made targets, and compares this to a random curriculum of equal size. Stage 3 uses supervised contrastive learning to enforce better separation between target and clutter in representation space. They evaluate on at-sea SAS data with a mission-level geographic split, fix test recall at 85%, compare models by area under the precision-recall curve (AUPRC), and repeat experiments over three random seeds.

Result: Using only LoRA with a frozen backbone (rank 4, 0.26% of weights trainable) boosts AUPRC from 0.300 to 0.679 ± 0.027. The additional stages offer no statistically meaningful improvements over appropriate controls: hard-negative mining changes AUPRC by −0.0045 ± 0.0119 relative to a size-matched random curriculum, and supervised contrastive learning changes AUPRC by +0.0002 ± 0.0096 relative to the prior stage.

Conclusion: A single, parameter-efficient LoRA adaptation stage is sufficient to obtain large performance gains when transferring a pretrained ViT to SAS ATR. Additional refinement via hard-negative mining and supervised contrastive learning does not significantly improve performance, likely because the encoder has already captured the relevant structure and class geometry. Stacked, multi-stage refinements are therefore unnecessary in this setting.

Abstract: Automatic target recognition (ATR) with synthetic aperture sonar (SAS) supports advanced naval capabilities, but deep learning is constrained by scarce target imagery, background clutter, and human-in-the-loop assessment. We adapt DINOv3 Vision Transformer (ViT) models to underwater SAS ATR using a three-stage parameter-efficient framework. Stage 1 uses Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) while freezing the ViT backbone, bridging the gap between natural-image pretraining and underwater acoustic propagation. Stage 2 uses hard-negative mining to strengthen the decision boundary against acoustic mimics, including rocks and sediment formations resembling man-made targets. Stage 3 uses Supervised Contrastive Learning (SupCon) to separate target and clutter representations. We evaluate at-sea SAS data using a mission-level geographic split, compare all arms at 85 percent test recall, and repeat each comparison over three random seeds. LoRA accounts for the primary effect, increasing area under the precision-recall curve (AUPRC) from 0.300 to 0.679 +/- 0.027 using the same frozen backbone. Rank 4 achieves this result while training only 0.26 percent of weights. Neither refinement stage exceeds its matched control: hard-negative mining changes AUPRC by -0.0045 +/- 0.0119 versus an equal-size random curriculum, and SupCon changes AUPRC by +0.0002 +/- 0.0096 versus the preceding stage. These null results indicate that mining occurred on data the encoder had already fit and that supervised stages had already imposed most target-clutter geometry. One efficient adaptation stage is sufficient; stacked refinement is not.

</details>


### [70] [Detecting Hallucination in LLMs: Tracing the Topological Signatures of Impaired Context Sharing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21096)
*Amir Jalilifard,Anderson Rocha,Eric Wong,Marcos Medeiros Raimundo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a curvature-based analysis of LLM attention graphs to detect hallucinated responses by revealing information-flow bottlenecks and impaired context sharing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often produce hallucinations, and existing hallucination-detection methods, especially attention-based or multi-response approaches, are either unreliable, require multiple passes, or do not deeply analyze the internal information-flow structure. The authors aim to find a principled, single-pass, architecture-agnostic way to distinguish hallucinated from faithful generations by studying how information flows inside the model’s attention mechanism.

Method: The authors model each attention pattern as an attention graph and compute Forman-Ricci curvature on its edges to quantify structural properties related to information flow and bottlenecks. They then design a method that aggregates curvature-based and related features to capture both semi-local and global characteristics of attention heads when producing a response. These features are used to differentiate hallucinated from non-hallucinated generations, with a focus on identifying patterns such as information bottlenecks, self-attention dominance, and diffused context usage, particularly in upper layers.

Result: Across several LLMs and standard hallucination-detection benchmarks, the proposed curvature-based, single-pass detector outperforms prior attention-based and multi-response baselines on two hallucination benchmarks and maintains competitive performance across different model architectures. The empirical analysis further shows strong correlations between specific attention-graph patterns—like impaired context sharing and information over-squashing—and hallucination events.

Conclusion: The topology of attention graphs, quantified via Forman-Ricci curvature and related information-flow measures, is a useful signal for detecting hallucinations in LLM outputs. Hallucinated responses are marked by disrupted context sharing—manifested as excessive self-attention, weak and diffuse connections to earlier tokens, and over-squashing of information, especially in the final transformer layer—suggesting that monitoring attention-graph structure can provide an efficient, architecture-agnostic tool for hallucination detection and potentially for designing more robust LLMs.

Abstract: In this work, we examine the topology of information flow patterns within attention graphs to effectively distinguish hallucinated from non-hallucinated responses. We analyze the Forman-Ricci curvature to identify structural patterns indicating information bottlenecks in attention graphs. We then introduce a method that captures both semi-local and global information-flow characteristics of attention heads associated with hallucinated responses. We evaluate our approach extensively across several LLMs and established benchmarks. Empirical results demonstrate that our proposed single-pass approach provides consistent improvements over existing attention-based and multi-response baselines across two hallucination-detection benchmarks, while achieving competitive performance across diverse LLM architectures. Further analysis reveals that impaired context sharing among tokens during causal generation is strongly associated with hallucination occurrences in LLMs. In particular, hallucinated responses are consistently characterized by an over-reliance on self-attention, diffused context retrieval from earlier tokens, or information over-squashing, especially in the final transformer layer.

</details>


### [71] [Decoupling Internal Representational Changes and Causal Importance in Fine-Tuned Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21113)
*Lingfang Li,Procheta Sen,Shubham Das,Danushka Bollegala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how fine-tuning changes the internal representations of large language models, comparing those changes to task-relevant components identified by EAP, and finds a mismatch between where representations change most and where important task components live, as well as interference between tasks that share components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuning is the standard way to adapt LLMs to new tasks, but it is unclear how this process actually modifies the model’s internal mechanisms (attention heads, layers, activations). Understanding this is important for interpretability, modularity, and safer/more efficient fine-tuning, especially when models are adapted to many tasks that may interfere with one another.

Method: The authors analyze LLMs before and after fine-tuning on specific tasks. They use EAP (which identifies task-relevant components such as attention heads and logit-level activations that most strongly drive task performance) to locate functionally important parts of the network. They then measure (1) how fine-tuning changes attention patterns and layer-wise activations, and (2) how these changes relate to the EAP-identified components, as well as comparing overlap of such components across different tasks.

Result: They find that EAP-identified components tend to cluster in specific layers, suggesting functional localization of task behavior. However, the layers with the largest changes in internal representations during fine-tuning do not align with the layers containing most of the EAP-identified components. Additionally, even when different tasks share a high overlap in EAP-identified components, this overlap does not yield positive transfer: in fact, fine-tuning on one task can degrade performance on another if the tasks are of different types (e.g., classification vs generation).

Conclusion: Fine-tuning induces substantial representational changes that only partially correspond to the components that are most responsible for task performance, challenging a simple picture where fine-tuning ‘edits’ just the functionally critical parts. Task-relevant mechanisms appear localized, yet cross-task overlap in those mechanisms does not guarantee transfer and can cause interference when tasks differ in nature. These findings have implications for designing fine-tuning and multi-task training strategies, highlighting the need for more targeted or modular approaches to avoid destructive interference across tasks.

Abstract: Fine-tuning has emerged as a widely adopted approach for adapting LLMs to a variety of downstream tasks. However, how it reshapes their internal mechanisms remains poorly understood. To address this, we investigate how fine-tuning alters internal representations in LLMs, including attention patterns and layer-wise activations, and examine whether these changes are linked to task-relevant components identified by EAP (e.g., attention heads and logit-level activations) that drive task performance. We find that EAP-identified components are concentrated within specific layers, indicating a degree of functional localisation in how models internalise task-specific behavior. Notably, the distribution of these components across layers is largely uncorrelated with the layers undergoing the most substantial representational changes during fine-tuning. Furthermore, we observe that overlap in EAP-identified components across tasks does not translate into cross-task performance transfer if the tasks are different in nature (e.g. classification vs. generative tasks). More specifically, fine-tuning on one task can lead to a degradation of performance on another when the two tasks exhibit a high degree of overlap in their EAP-identified components.

</details>


### [72] [TinyCeNN-LM: Quality-Gated Conversion of Pretrained Attention with CeNN-Inspired Cellular-Recurrent Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21139)
*Kabeh Mohsenzadegan,Vahid Tavakkoli,Kyandoghere Kyamakya*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes TinyCeNN-LM, a framework to conservatively replace attention layers in pretrained language models with CeNN-inspired cellular-recurrent layers, using strict quality gates to ensure compatibility and maintain performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simply swapping out attention modules in pretrained LMs often breaks downstream layers because later blocks expect very specific internal representations. There is a need for a principled, safe way to replace or compress attention for efficiency (e.g., cache reduction) without significantly degrading perplexity or corrupting internal feature spaces.

Method: TinyCeNN-LM introduces cellular-recurrent layers with bounded local processing, compact recurrent memory, routing, fusion, and an accept-or-rollback validation mechanism. The authors design a quality-gated post-training conversion pipeline that attempts to replace specific attention layers with one of three CeNN-style variants: Integrated Memory, MemoryFusion, and PDelta3-GDN2-CLVR+Local32. A strict PDelta3 conversion rule accepts converted layers only if they satisfy both representation fidelity thresholds (similarity between original and converted hidden states) and NLL/perplexity degradation bounds; otherwise, the conversion for that layer is rolled back.

Result: On SmolLM2-135M, the first three layers are tested: layers 0–2 pass both NLL and representation criteria with a small cumulative NLL increase of +0.01209, but layer 3 is rejected because its representations deviate too much despite having acceptable NLL. On Qwen3.5-0.8B, converted full-attention layers 3, 7, and 11 are accepted under the same quality criteria, yielding a total NLL change of +0.02073. The Integrated Memory variant achieves perplexity changes between −0.07% and +0.93% while reducing KV cache size by up to 6.01%. A small 200-item downstream sanity-check benchmark on the converted Qwen models yields 28.5%–32.0% overall accuracy, indicating that the models remain functional though not fully evaluated on broad benchmarks.

Conclusion: The experiments indicate that blindly replacing attention is unsafe, but a conservative, quality-gated conversion strategy can partially substitute attention layers with CeNN-like modules while preserving language modeling quality and reducing cache usage. The paper argues for structural conversion guarded by strict fidelity and NLL tests, rather than universal attention replacement or aggressive speed-focused modifications, as a more reliable path to making alternative architectures compatible with existing pretrained transformers.

Abstract: Replacing attention in a pretrained language model is a compatibility problem: a plausible substitute may alter representations expected by later layers. TinyCeNN-LM introduces a \emph{quality-gated post-training conversion} framework using CeNN-inspired cellular-recurrent layers with bounded local processing, compact recurrent memory, routing, fusion, and accept-or-rollback validation. Three implementations are studied: Integrated Memory, MemoryFusion, and PDelta3-GDN2-CLVR+Local32. Strict PDelta3 conversion accepts a layer only when representation and NLL criteria pass fixed thresholds. On SmolLM2-135M, layers 0-2 are accepted with cumulative $Δ\mathrm{NLL}=+0.01209$, while layer 3 is rejected despite acceptable NLL because representation fidelity fails. On Qwen3.5-0.8B, full-attention layers 3, 7, and 11 are accepted with final $Δ\mathrm{NLL}=+0.02073$. Integrated Memory keeps perplexity within $-0.07\%$ to $+0.93\%$ while reducing total cache by up to $6.01\%$. A sampled 200-item downstream sanity check gives $28.5\%$--$32.0\%$ overall accuracy for converted Qwen releases. The results support conservative, quality-gated structural conversion rather than universal attention replacement or speedup.

</details>


### [73] [Clinician-Grounded Quality Assurance for AI-Assisted Psychiatric Intake](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21149)
*King Shi,Amanda Li,Jonathan Ivey,Synthia Qia Wang,Guan Gui,Hyunseo Kim,Peter Zandi,Jason Straub,Jacob Taylor,Ananya Joshi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces InterviewPlayground, a clinician-grounded evaluation platform using simulated patients to assess AI systems for psychiatric intake, demonstrating its ability to compare an LLM interviewer with clinicians on clinically relevant metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Health systems need a routine, practical way to evaluate AI-assisted psychiatric intake tools against existing clinical standards. Traditional clinician interviews vary widely in style, and direct evaluation can be burdensome for clinicians. Therefore, there is a need for an evaluation framework that allows comparison across interviewing approaches, reduces clinician workload, and focuses on clinically meaningful performance for real-world deployment and quality assurance.

Method: The authors build InterviewPlayground, a memory-augmented patient simulator that supports open-ended AI interviewing. They author expert vignettes to instantiate interactive simulated patients, create a simulated intake platform to host these interviews, and design evaluation modalities tailored to psychiatric intake (e.g., recovery of clinically relevant items, nature of clinical inferences, and handling of safety concerns). In a pilot study, 6 clinicians conduct brief (25-minute) assessments, which are then compared to a GPT-based large language model acting as an intake interviewer on the same simulated patients and metrics.

Result: In the pilot comparison, the GPT-based LLM interviewer retrieved a higher proportion of clinically relevant items from the patient vignettes than clinicians (88.0% vs. 38.9%), indicating strong information-gathering capabilities. However, the LLM also made more clinical inferences not grounded in the interview content (56.8% vs. 27.8%), and it was less likely to fully characterize identified safety concerns (33.3% vs. 66.7%) compared with clinicians.

Conclusion: InterviewPlayground provides a practical, clinician-grounded platform to evaluate AI psychiatric intake systems on realistic, clinically relevant criteria while limiting clinician time burden. The pilot results suggest that LLMs may be effective at gathering information but currently exhibit issues with unwarranted inferences and insufficient elaboration on safety concerns. The framework thus enables health systems to implement ongoing quality assurance and guides future refinement of AI interviewers before and during deployment.

Abstract: Before patients can use AI-assisted psychiatric intake systems, health systems need practical ways to routinely evaluate these tools against their clinical standards for quality assurance. Because clinicians may use different intake styles, evaluation for this task must (1) support comparison across interviewing approaches, (2) minimize clinician burden, and (3) measure clinically relevant performance for health systems deploying these technologies. We present a clinician-grounded evaluation platform built around a memory-augmented patient simulator for open-ended AI interviewing, InterviewPlayground. We created interactive patients using InterviewPlayground with our expert-authored vignettes, constructed a simulated intake platform for the interviews, and designed evaluation modalities relevant to intake. In a pilot of 6 clinicians in a 25-minute assessment compared to a GPT-based LLM intake interviewer, the LLM recovered more of the clinically relevant items embedded in the patient vignettes (88.0% vs. 38.9%), but made more clinical inferences not based on the interview (56.8% vs. 27.8%), and characterized identified safety concerns less often (33.3% vs. 66.7%), setting the stage for deployed quality assurance for this task.

</details>


### [74] [Can Agents Design Better Chips with a Higher Level Abstraction?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21157)
*Zijian Ding,Yang Zou,Yizhou Sun,Jason Cong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes and evaluates an LLM-agent-based chip design workflow that works at a higher abstraction level (HLS) and then refines RTL, achieving significantly better FPGA implementations than direct RTL design by LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most LLM-based chip design approaches operate directly on RTL, which is low-level, difficult for LLMs to reason about, and may limit quality of results. The authors want to understand whether using higher-level hardware design abstractions, plus selective lower-level refinement, can enable LLM agents to produce better-performing hardware designs. They also want to systematically compare different agent-in-the-loop positions in the hardware design toolflow.

Method: The authors define and compare four LLM-agent chip design strategies: (1) Direct RTL Design, where the agent writes RTL code; (2) Agent-based HLS Design, where the agent writes high-level synthesis (HLS) descriptions; (3) Post-Compiler HLS Refinement, where the agent refines designs after compilation; and (4) Post-HLS RTL Refinement, where the agent optimizes the generated RTL. They then combine (2) and (4) into a composite workflow called Agent-based HLS with RTL Refinement (AHRR). Using FPGAs as the evaluation platform, they run an 11-task benchmark suite and measure performance (speed) and design characteristics to compare the workflows, complemented by qualitative case studies of how HLS abstractions and RTL refinements help the agent.

Result: Across 11 diverse benchmarks, AHRR achieves a 2.6x geometric-mean speedup over Direct RTL Design, showing that placing the agent at a higher abstraction level and then refining the lower-level representation produces substantially better hardware implementations. The case studies suggest that HLS provides structured design abstractions that encode domain knowledge, which the LLM can use effectively, while the final RTL refinement step exposes additional micro-optimizations that HLS alone misses.

Conclusion: The combination of LLM-driven HLS design with subsequent RTL refinement (AHRR) is an effective workflow for agentic chip design, outperforming direct RTL authoring by LLMs on FPGA benchmarks. The findings support the idea that LLM agents benefit from higher-level design abstractions supplemented by targeted low-level optimization, and the approach appears generally applicable across hardware design flows, not just FPGAs.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly being explored for chip design, but most existing approaches operate directly at RTL. We ask whether agents can design better chips by leveraging higher-level abstractions. We compare Direct RTL Design, Agent-based HLS Design, Post-Compiler HLS Refinement, and Post-HLS RTL Refinement, and combine Agent-based HLS Design with Post-HLS RTL Refinement as Agent-based HLS with RTL Refinement (AHRR). We use FPGAs as a practical, easy-to-deploy platform for end-to-end evaluation, but note that the design-flow tradeoffs we study are largely independent of the target technology. Across a diverse 11-tasks benchmark suite, AHRR achieves a 2.6$\times$ geometric-mean speedup over Direct RTL Design across our benchmark suite. Case studies show that HLS distills design knowledge into abstractions that agents can leverage, while RTL refinement recovers lower-level optimization opportunities. Together, these results make AHRR a promising workflow for agentic chip design. The code and evaluation artifacts are available at https://github.com/ZijD/AHRR.

</details>


### [75] [SpecOpt: Contact-Diff Reasoning for Agentic Molecule Optimization Toward Binding Specificity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21165)
*Thao Nguyen,Heng Ji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes SpecOpt, a framework to optimize the specificity of existing drugs by making small structural changes that improve binding to intended targets over known off-targets, guided by residue-aware docking interactions and validated on a new ChEMBL-based benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many small-molecule drugs cause side effects due to off-target protein binding, but most computational design methods focus on designing new selective molecules rather than improving the selectivity of existing drugs. There is a gap in methods and benchmarks for systematically modifying known compounds to enhance their target vs. off-target selectivity while preserving their core chemical identity and drug-likeness.

Method: The authors define a new task, specificity optimization (SpecOpt), where the goal is to find constrained structural modifications to an existing compound that increase its binding preference for an intended target over known off-targets. They build a benchmark from ChEMBL using curated drug-mechanism annotations to define intended targets and experimentally measured activities to identify off-targets. Methodologically, they develop an agentic framework that: (1) docks each compound to its intended and off-target proteins; (2) computes residue-aware atom-protein contact patterns and differential interactions between target and off-target poses; (3) feeds these structured differential interaction descriptions to a large language model to propose targeted structural modifications; and (4) filters proposed molecules by molecular similarity to the parent, ADMET properties, and improved docking-based selectivity between target and off-targets. They also conduct ablation studies to dissect the contribution of residue-level contact information versus simpler binary contact signals.

Result: On a benchmark of 915 compounds, the framework improves the target–off-target binding energy gap for 84.8% of cases, with the mean gap shifting from -0.72 kcal/mol (favoring off-targets on average) to +0.47 kcal/mol (favoring targets) while preserving high structural similarity to the original molecules (mean Tanimoto 0.72). Ablation experiments show that when residue identities are removed and only binary contact presence is provided, no improvement is seen on 29 tested compounds, demonstrating that residue-specific information is key to performance.

Conclusion: The study introduces and formalizes specificity optimization (SpecOpt) as a distinct, practically relevant molecular design objective focused on selectively refining existing drugs. It shows that residue-aware differential docking interactions, when coupled with an LLM-based design agent and strict filtering for similarity and drug-likeness, can systematically improve predicted target vs. off-target binding. This highlights the importance of fine-grained interaction information in guiding molecular edits and provides both a benchmark and a methodological template for future work on drug selectivity optimization.

Abstract: Off-target protein binding is a major source of adverse effects for small-molecule drugs, yet most structure-based molecular design methods focus on generating selective compounds de novo rather than improving the selectivity of existing, well- characterized drugs. We introduce specificity optimization (SpecOpt), a molecular design task that seeks constrained structural modifications to an existing compound that increase its binding preference for an intended target over known off-targets while preserving its structural identity and drug-like properties. To enable systematic evaluation, we construct a ChEMBL-derived benchmark from compound-target interaction data, identifying intended targets through curated drug-mechanism annotations and off- targets through measured activities. We then develop an agentic framework that docks each compound against its intended target and off-targets, compares the resulting poses through residue-aware atom-protein contacts, and provides these differential interactions to a large language model to propose targeted structural modifications. Candidates are retained only if they satisfy molecular similarity, ADMET, and target-off-target docking selectivity criteria. On 915 compounds, the agent improves the target- off-target binding gap for 84.8% of compounds, shifting the mean gap from -0.72 to +0.47 kcal/mol while maintaining a mean Tanimoto similarity of 0.72 to the starting compounds. Ablation studies identify residue-specific contact information as the critical optimization signal: replacing residue identities with binary contact indicators eliminates improvement on all 29 ablation compounds. These results establish SpecOpt as a distinct molecular design problem and demonstrate residue-aware differential interactions as an effective signal for improving the specificity of existing compounds.

</details>


### [76] [Implicit Rule Induction with Test-Time Task Embeddings in ARC-like Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21181)
*Adrien Deliège,Claas Beger,Marc Van Droogenbroeck,Melanie Mitchell*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a new two-step test-time training protocol for a Vision ARC model that better isolates and learns task rules as embeddings, leading to more interpretable, compositional, and effective reasoning on ARC-like benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ARC-style benchmarks test if models can solve novel reasoning tasks, but it is unclear whether success comes from truly inferring the intended transformation rule or from exploiting shortcuts or spurious patterns. In the Vision ARC model, a task-specific embedding is optimized at test time together with the backbone, so it is difficult to separate rule learning (induction) from rule execution, and to analyze what the embedding actually represents. The authors want a clearer, more interpretable framework that disentangles these aspects and allows them to study whether the learned representations align with true underlying rules.

Method: They modify the Vision ARC (VARC) framework by introducing a two-step test-time training (TTT) scheme. Instead of jointly updating the backbone and task embedding, they first optimize only the task embedding for a given test task (Embed-TTT stage 1). After the embedding converges, they freeze it and then finetune the backbone parameters (stage 2). They evaluate this protocol on ARC-AGI-1, ConceptARC, Mini-ARC, and specially constructed datasets whose underlying rules are known and parametric. They analyze: (1) how well the learned embeddings correspond to ground-truth rules (via linear probes and rule-wise structure recovery), (2) how embeddings support retrieval of similar tasks, and (3) whether the system exhibits compositionality, such as interpolation between rules. They also measure how much performance can be obtained when only the small task embedding (under 0.01% of parameters) is optimized, versus the full two-step pipeline.

Result: The two-step Embed-TTT protocol consistently improves the quality of task embeddings across benchmarks, compared to joint TTT. The learned embeddings align more closely with underlying transformation rules, support better retrieval of related tasks, and enable accurate linear decoding of known rules on controlled datasets. Qualitatively, on ARC-AGI-1, the embeddings capture more semantically meaningful relationships between tasks. Optimizing only the task embeddings—without changing most network weights—already solves a notable subset of tasks on ARC-AGI-1, ConceptARC, and Mini-ARC. When followed by backbone finetuning, the overall success rate further increases. On parametric rule datasets, Embed-TTT reconstructs the geometric structure of the rule space and manifests compositional behavior enabling interpolation between rules, though it still fails to extrapolate beyond the training distribution.

Conclusion: The proposed two-step test-time training protocol yields task embeddings that more faithfully represent underlying rules and support clearer separation between rule induction (in the embedding) and rule execution (in the backbone) in ARC-like tasks. This leads to improved performance, interpretability, and compositional generalization in-distribution, but limited out-of-distribution extrapolation. The findings suggest that ARC evaluations should more explicitly distinguish reasoning about known, interpolated rules from truly novel, out-of-distribution rules, and motivate future benchmarks and methods that better probe rule induction independently from execution capability.

Abstract: The Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus and related benchmarks evaluate whether AI models can solve novel reasoning tasks, but often leave unclear whether success reflects inference of the intended underlying rule or reliance on shortcuts. We address this gap by studying test-time task embeddings in Vision ARC (VARC), a model in which a pre-trained backbone is complemented by a trainable embedding representing the transformation rule. In the original VARC, test-time training (TTT) is jointly applied to the backbone and task embedding. Here we introduce a novel two-step TTT protocol: first finetune only the task embedding (Embed-TTT), then freeze it and finetune the backbone. Across ARC-AGI-1, ConceptARC, and two controlled datasets with known rules, Embed-TTT consistently yields improved task embeddings, ones that align better with underlying task rules, improve embedding-based retrieval, and enable accurate linear probing of known rules. Qualitatively, Embed-TTT identifies more semantically meaningful relations between test and train tasks on ARC-AGI-1. We also show that optimizing only task embeddings (less than 0.01% of model parameters) already solves a non-trivial fraction of ARC-AGI-1, ConceptARC, and Mini-ARC tasks, while the full two-step pipeline improves final performance. Finally, we show that Embed-TTT recovers the underlying geometric structure of parametric rules and learns compositional capabilities that enable rule-wise interpolation, but not extrapolation. These findings support a clearer separation between rule induction and rule execution in ARC-like evaluations, motivating benchmarks that better distinguish in-distribution from out-of-distribution rules.

</details>


### [77] [AI-GRACE: A Use-Case Operationalization Framework for Agentic AI: From Organizational Objectives and Obligations to Deployment Capabilities and Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21192)
*John Cuneo,David Chun,Gaurav Khanna*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI-GRACE is a governance and risk framework to operationalize trustworthy, agentic AI deployment from high-level obligations down to technical controls and evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As organizations deploy increasingly agentic AI systems, they need more than generic trust assessments; they require a systematic way to translate governance obligations and risk concerns into concrete technical requirements, controls, and monitoring tailored to each specific use case.

Method: The authors use design science to propose AI-GRACE, a use-case operationalization framework built from professional practice and a targeted synthesis of standards and literature. They structure it around setting objectives and obligations, assessing risks across seven domains, deriving assurance, control, and evidence requirements, and then mapping these to capability qualification, gap analysis, and logical architecture, guided by situational method engineering for contextual tailoring and reuse.

Result: The paper defines the AI-GRACE framework, including concepts like an Agent Operating Envelope to bound agent actions and define escalation conditions, and Risk-Aligned Independence Levels (RAIL) to characterize allowable agent autonomy. It shows how the framework can be applied via a fictional retail banking use case, demonstrating traceability from governance-level objectives to technical mechanisms and highlighting how to identify what is already in place versus what must be implemented.

Conclusion: AI-GRACE offers a structured, traceable approach for organizations to operationalize governance, risk, and assurance for agentic AI use cases, helping decide what to validate, control, and observe, and what remains unresolved. The authors argue that future empirical work is needed to evaluate whether using AI-GRACE actually improves decision quality, deployment efficiency, and reuse across use cases.

Abstract: Organizations deploying agentic artificial intelligence must determine more than whether a model is trustworthy; they must establish what to validate, control, and observe for a use case to deliver its intended outcome while meeting applicable obligations. This paper proposes AI-GRACE (Agentic Intelligence-Governance, Risk, Assurance, Controls, and Evidence) as a use-case operationalization framework connecting organizational governance with technical implementation. The proposal draws on professional observations and a purposive synthesis of standards and literature, using design science to frame the method contribution and situational method engineering to guide contextual tailoring and reuse. The framework establishes objectives and obligations and then assesses risks in seven proposed domains, including mission and value realization. It derives requirements for assurance before deployment, runtime controls, and evidence, which guide capability qualification, gap assessment, and a logical architecture. An Agent Operating Envelope specifies permitted actions and escalation conditions, while Risk-Aligned Independence Levels (RAIL) summarize the authorized independence. A fictional retail banking application illustrates the method. The contribution is a traceable basis for deciding what an organization must implement, what it already supports, and what remains unresolved. Empirical evaluation must establish whether it improves deployment decisions, efficiency, and reuse.

</details>


### [78] [Information-Gain Rewards over Diversity-Pruned Tests: GT-Anchored Verifier Co-Training for Reliable Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21208)
*Ana Nunez,Peyman Najafirad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes CoVer, a self-play reinforcement learning framework that co-trains a single language model as both code generator and test generator, using an information-gain reward and diversity-aware test selection to avoid trivial tests and concentrated sampling, significantly improving code-generation performance over strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-play approaches for code-generation RL let a single language model generate both code and tests, which should enable learning beyond fixed benchmarks. However, these methods often degenerate: they learn to create trivial or non-discriminative tests that maximize reward without improving coding ability (permissiveness collapse), and their randomly sampled tests cluster around common cases, increasing variance and reducing learning efficiency (concentration bias). The authors want a self-play framework that produces informative, diverse tests that genuinely improve code-generation quality.

Method: They introduce CoVer, a GRPO-based single-policy framework where the same model acts as coder and verifier (test author).
