For each self-generated test, they define an information-gain reward based on the mutual information between the test’s pass/fail outcomes and a graded correctness signal y∈[0,1]^m derived from ground truth; they gate this reward by the sign of covariance so that only positively discriminative tests (those that distinguish better from worse solutions) receive reward. To combat concentration bias, they apply a three-stage diversity-aware selection over a pool of candidate tests: filtering invalid tests, deduplicating by input string, and pruning tests with similar execution profiles, thereby constructing a behaviourally non-redundant test suite that increases the effective sample size of the IG estimator under a fixed execution budget. This is integrated into a GRPO training loop for the single LM policy.

Result: On five code-generation benchmarks (LiveBench, MBPP, LiveCodeBench, CodeContests, CodeForces), CoVer improves one-shot pass@1 by +5.8 points for a 7B model and +7.1 points for a 14B model compared to the Qwen2.5-Instruct backbone, and achieves the best macro-average performance among all compared methods at both scales. When used as the backbone model in the CodeT ranking pipeline, CoVer-7B further boosts performance by +3.5 points, showing benefits not only for code generation but also for solution ranking/selection.

Conclusion: CoVer effectively addresses the two main pathologies of self-play code-generation RL—permissiveness collapse and concentration bias—by rewarding tests based on information gain about graded correctness and enforcing diversity in the selected test suite. This leads to more informative self-generated tests, more stable and efficient learning, and substantial gains in code-generation benchmarks and ranking pipelines, establishing CoVer as a strong, general drop-in self-play backbone for both generation and selection tasks.

Abstract: Self-play methods that co-train a single language model as both coder and test author promise to move code-generation RL beyond fixed test suites, but they suffer from two coupled pathologies: permissiveness collapse, where pass-rate rewards are maximised by trivial, non-discriminative tests, and concentration bias, where i.i.d. sampled tests cluster on modal inputs and inflate estimator variance. We introduce CoVer (Co-trained Coder and Verifier), a single-policy GRPO framework that addresses both failure modes. First, an information-gain (IG) reward scores each self-generated test by the mutual information between its pass/fail vector and a graded, ground-truth-anchored correctness signal y [0, 1] m, gated by the sign of their covariance so that only positively discriminative tests receive reward. Second, a three-stage diversity-aware selection step prunes a candidate pool to a behaviourally non-redundant suite (invalidity, input-string, execution-profile filtering), raising the effective sample size of the IG estimator at fixed execution budget. On five benchmarks (LiveBench, MBPP, LiveCodeBench, CodeContests, Code-Forces), CoVer raises one-shot pass@1 by +5.8 points at 7B and +7.1 points at 14B over the Qwen2.5-Instruct backbone, and achieves the highest macro-average among all compared methods at both scales. As a drop-in backbone inside the CodeT ranking pipeline, CoVer-7B adds +3.5 points, demonstrating the dual benefit of co-training for both generation and selection.

</details>


### [79] [Ability-Residual Decoupled Modeling for Affective Cognitive Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21214)
*Boyuan Zhao,Meng Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a new affective cognitive diagnosis framework that separates stable cognitive residuals from affective factors to avoid contamination and improve prediction and affect alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In educational data, student responses are influenced not only by cognitive mastery but also by non-cognitive (affective) factors such as emotion and fatigue. Existing affective cognitive diagnosis methods assume that the base cognitive model fully captures ability, item, and concept effects, so residual errors are mostly affective. In practice, systematic cognitive biases (e.g., item calibration bias, concept bias, student-concept idiosyncrasies, and student–item matching) remain in the residuals, which then leak into the affect representation, corrupting affect estimation and hurting robustness.

Method: They design an ability-residual decoupled framework: first, they explicitly model unaccounted cognitive residuals through multiple components—student, item, concept, student–concept interactions, and a low-rank student–item term—on top of standard cognitive diagnosis backbones. Then, a separate affective module modulates guess and slip probabilities, operating on a cleaner residual signal. They introduce a Q-matrix-constrained concept residual attention mechanism that adaptively aggregates only those concept residuals relevant to each item. The framework is instantiated on six existing cognitive diagnosis models and evaluated on four real-world datasets.

Result: Across ASSIST2017, ASSIST2012, ASSIST2009, and Junyi datasets, and with six different cognitive diagnosis backbones, the proposed framework consistently improves response prediction compared with baselines. When affect labels are present, the learned affective representations show better alignment with ground-truth affect. Diagnostic experiments—including ablation, leakage probing, PCA visualization, long-tail performance analysis, and qualitative case studies—show that the residual components capture stable cognitive biases and that separating them reduces contamination in the affective branch.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling structured ability-related residuals before learning affective factors leads to less affect contamination, more robust and accurate cognitive diagnosis, and better affect alignment. The proposed residual-decoupled architecture can be plugged into various cognitive diagnosis backbones to systematically absorb stable cognitive biases and enhance both predictive performance and interpretability in affective cognitive diagnosis.

Abstract: Cognitive diagnosis infers students' concept mastery from response logs. However, students' responses are not determined by mastery alone: non-cognitive factors such as emotion, engagement, and fatigue can also affect performance. Affective cognitive diagnosis therefore extends conventional cognitive diagnosis by incorporating affective states. Existing methods often assume that the cognitive diagnosis backbone has already explained ability, item, and concept effects, so the remaining errors can be attributed mainly to affect. We argue that this assumption can be insufficient in real educational data: item calibration bias, systematic concept bias, personalized student-concept deviations, and latent student-item matching can form stable cognitive residuals. Without an explicit modeling pathway, these residuals may leak into affective representations, producing affect contamination. To address this problem, we propose an ability-residual decoupled framework for affective cognitive diagnosis. The model first captures unmodeled cognitive residuals through student, item, concept, student-concept, and low-rank student-item components, and then uses an affective module to modulate guess/slip effects. A Q-matrix-constrained concept residual attention mechanism adaptively aggregates only item-relevant concept residuals. Experiments on ASSIST2017, ASSIST2012, ASSIST2009, and Junyi with six cognitive diagnosis backbones show response-prediction gains across the reported comparisons and generally improved affect alignment when affect labels are available. Ablation studies, leakage probes, principal component analysis visualization, long-tail analysis, and case studies further indicate that ability residuals absorb stable cognitive bias, reduce cognitive contamination in the affective branch, and enhance the robustness and predictive accuracy of cognitive diagnosis models.

</details>


### [80] [A Fully Differentiable Neuro-Soft-Symbolic Framework for Perceptual Task Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21221)
*Hongyan Wei,Wael AbdAlmageed*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a differentiable neuro-soft-symbolic framework that unifies uncertain visual perception and symbolic task planning, enabling end-to-end learning and more efficient, accurate planning under perceptual uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional perceptual planning pipelines first convert raw perceptual inputs into hard symbolic facts and then perform symbolic planning. This discretization discards valuable uncertainty information and breaks the feedback loop from task-level planning performance back to perception, leading to suboptimal perception for the downstream task and inefficient or brittle planning. The authors aim to create a framework that can leverage uncertainty, remain differentiable end-to-end, and allow task objectives to shape the perceptual representations.

Method: The authors propose a fully differentiable neuro-soft-symbolic framework that integrates perception and planning in a single computational graph. Instead of hard symbols, the system maintains a continuous soft symbolic state. Domain rules are lifted into a differentiable soft transition operator (soft-TP), which enables gradient-based optimization of action logits over a short planning horizon. Because the entire pipeline is differentiable, gradients from the planning objective are backpropagated to the perception module, jointly tuning perception and planning. They evaluate the system on Blocksworld benchmarks and include an ablation where perception is frozen versus trainable during planning, as well as task-and-motion simulations to validate executability of the plans by a robot motion layer.

Result: On LatPlan-40 Blocksworld tasks, the proposed method solves 40/40 tasks, outperforming LatPlan’s 33/40. On PlanBench-600 tasks, it solves 596/600, slightly better than a reasoning-model baseline (587/600), while using substantially less computation and time. In an ablation focusing on perceptual uncertainty, allowing perception to be optimized via planning gradients raises success from 59% (frozen perception) to 83%. Task-and-motion simulations further show that the decoded task plans are compatible with downstream robotic motion execution in Blocksworld-like settings.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that a differentiable neuro-soft-symbolic integration of perception and planning can effectively exploit perceptual uncertainty, improve planning performance, and enable end-to-end optimization where task-level objectives refine perception. The approach achieves state-of-the-art or better results on standard Blocksworld benchmarks with improved computational efficiency, and its compatibility with task-and-motion execution suggests practical promise for robotic applications requiring tight coupling between perception and symbolic planning.

Abstract: Perceptual planning tasks require two key capabilities: accurately perceiving uncertain scenes and planning valid action sequences following logical rules. Conventional methods convert perception into discrete symbolic facts and then plan, discarding perceptual uncertainty and severing task-level feedback to perception. We introduce a generic, fully differentiable neuro-soft-symbolic framework that connects visual perception and task planning within a single computational graph. The framework maintains a continuous soft symbolic state, lifts domain rules into a differentiable soft-$T_P$ transition operator, and optimizes action logits over a short planning horizon. Gradients from the planning objective can also update the perception parameters, allowing task-relevant perceptual representations to be refined during planning. On Blocksworld, our method solves 40/40 LatPlan-40 tasks and 596/600 PlanBench-600 tasks, compared with 33/40 for LatPlan and 587/600 for the reasoning-model baseline, while requiring substantially less computation and time. In the perceptual-uncertainty ablation, our method improves the success rate from 59\% with frozen perception to 83\%. We further conduct task-and-motion simulations on Blocksworld scenes, providing an execution-level validation of the compatibility between decoded task plans and downstream robotic motion execution.

</details>


### [81] [CogGym: Towards Large-Scale Comparative Evaluation of Human and Machine Cognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21259)
*Lance Ying,Jinzhou Wu,Yingshan Susan Wang,Shivam Aarya,Luca M. Schulze Buschoff,Harry Chen,Katherine M. Collins,Andrea de Varda,Shuhao Fu,Sean Dae Houlihan,Akshay K. Jagadish,Guangyuan Jiang,Samuel Kiegeland,Tetsu Kurumisawa,Rongzhi Liu,Ryan Liu,Ningshan Ma,Kathryn McGregor,Younes Strittmatter,Polina Tsvilodub,Jacob Hoover Vigly,Sarah Wu,Enjie Xu,Yiling Yun,Kelsey Allen,Tyler Brooke-Wilson,Brian Christian,Evelina Fedorenko,Michael C. Frank,Michael Franke,Tao Gao,Samuel J. Gershman,Robert D. Hawkins,Jennifer Hu,Julian Jara-Ettinger,Max Kleiman-Weiner,Sydney Levine,Tal Linzen,Hongjing Lu,Timothy O'Donnell,Desmond C. Ong,Steven T. Piantadosi,Rebecca Saxe,Eric Schulz,Tianmin Shu,Felix A. Sosa,Ilia Sucholutsky,Tan Zhi-Xuan,Tomer Ullman,Fei Xu,Ilker Yildirim,Jian-Qiao Zhu,Thomas L. Griffiths,Tobias Gerstenberg,Kevin Smith,Joshua B. Tenenbaum*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CogGym is a standardized, scalable framework to compare human and AI behavior across many cognitive experiments, showing that larger LLMs align more with humans but still fall short of human-level reliability, especially on commonsense reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI and cognitive science both aim to understand and model human intelligence, but there is no scalable, unified way to rigorously compare AI models with humans across the broad range of cognitive tasks people can perform. Existing evaluations are fragmented, task-specific, and often not directly comparable to human experimental data. A framework is needed that systematically translates diverse cognitive experiments into a common format, allowing many models to be evaluated on the exact same trials as humans and quantifying how closely model responses match human judgments.

Method: The authors design CogGym, a unified framework based on cognitive science experiments that uses a semi-automated, human-in-the-loop pipeline to convert varied experimental paradigms into a task-agnostic Experiment Markup Language (EML). This standardization step allows reproducible, large-scale evaluation. For the initial release, they collect and standardize 258 commonsense reasoning experiments from 100 papers, covering text, image, and video tasks. They then evaluate 50 large language models on these experiments, compare model outputs to human responses, and analyze scaling trends and model–human fit using metrics such as R^2, including comparisons to human split-half reliability.

Result: CogGym successfully standardizes a large set of cognitive experiments into a common EML format and enables evaluation of 50 LLMs on matched human trials. The results show a clear scaling effect: larger, more recent AI models more closely reproduce human judgments across tasks. However, model improvements on these commonsense reasoning tasks are substantially slower than on formal reasoning benchmarks such as math and coding. Quantitatively, human split-half reliability is very high (R^2 ≈ 0.92–0.95 across modalities), whereas the best current models achieve only moderate R^2 values (0.59 for text, 0.58 for image, and 0.43 for video), indicating a substantial gap between model and human behavior.

Conclusion: CogGym provides a scalable, extensible evaluation framework for systematically comparing AI models and humans on a wide variety of cognitive science experiments. While contemporary large models show increasing similarity to human responses as they scale, they still fall well short of human-level reliability on commonsense reasoning across text, image, and video. The authors propose CogGym as a living benchmark that will grow with new experiments and models, enabling ongoing tracking of where AI behavior matches or diverges from human cognition and guiding progress toward more human-like artificial intelligence.

Abstract: Understanding and modeling human intelligence are parallel goals shared by artificial intelligence (AI) and cognitive science. As AI systems grow increasingly capable, in what ways do model responses resemble human responses, and where do they systematically diverge? The sheer breadth and diversity of the tasks humans can perform and think about pose a challenge for scalable and rigorous comparison between humans and models. We introduce CogGym, a scalable, unified framework grounded in cognitive science for systematically comparing model and human behavior on matched experimental trials. CogGym uses a semi-automated, human-in-the-loop pipeline to standardize diverse experimental paradigms into a task-agnostic Experiment Markup Language (EML), enabling reproducible and faithful comparison at scale. For initial release, we curate and standardize 258 cognitive experiments from 100 papers that focuses on human commonsense reasoning, and evaluate 50 large language models against human responses. We find a clear scaling trend where larger and more recent AI models better reproduce human judgments. Yet AI models' improvement on such common reasoning tasks is considerably slower than the gains observed on formal-reasoning benchmarks like math and coding, and model--human fit remains well below human splithalf reliability ($R^2 = 0.93$ on text, $0.95$ on image, and $0.92$ on video) with the best models achieving $R^2 = 0.59$ on text, $0.58$ on image, and $0.43$ on video experiments. We intend for CogGym to provide a living evaluation framework that continually incorporates new cognitive science experiments to characterize where model behavior resembles human behavior, where it systematically diverges, and how those patterns change as models and experiments evolve.

</details>


### [82] [PlaceReasoner-Beta: Reasoning-Driven Macro Placement and Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21263)
*Qiufeng Li,Chengxuan Wang,Rongqian Chen,Quan Cheng,Yihui Ren,Chia-Tung Ho,David Z. Pan,Tian Lan,Weidong Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new verifier-guided multi-agent VLM-based framework, PlaceReasoner-Beta, treats chip macro placement as a closed-loop reasoning task and achieves superior post-route timing and good wirelength on a new fully open benchmark, PlaceReasoner-Bench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Macro placement in VLSI is crucial but remains hard. Traditional methods rely on hand-crafted proxy objectives (like estimated wirelength) and one-shot numerical optimization, which makes it difficult to integrate visual layout context, domain expertise, and realistic downstream implementation feedback in a unified optimization loop. There is also a lack of reproducible, fully open, end-to-end benchmarks that evaluate on final routed quality instead of proxy metrics.

Method: They reformulate macro placement as a verifier-guided, closed-loop reasoning problem using a multi-agent system. A vision-language model planner proposes macro placements from images of the floorplan plus macro specs and connectivity. A geometric verifier filters and adjusts placements to ensure legality and follow expert geometric principles. A physical verifier further refines placements using early implementation feedback from the design flow. Finally, a post-route optimizer improves high-potential layouts using final post-route power-performance-area (PPA) metrics. They also build PlaceReasoner-Bench, an open benchmark of 8 RTL designs at 2 aspect ratios (16 tasks) with fixed floorplans and I/O, where only macro locations/orientations vary and are evaluated using routed PPA and DRC.

Result: On PlaceReasoner-Bench, PlaceReasoner-Beta achieves the best timing among all design-rule-clean methods on all square tasks. It reduces post-route total negative slack (TNS) by 61.2% for 1:1 aspect ratio designs and 53.0% for 2:1 aspect ratio designs compared to a classical baseline field. It also reduces routed wirelength on most designs even though wirelength is never explicitly optimized as an objective.

Conclusion: Treating macro placement as a closed-loop reasoning problem with verifier-guided multi-agent VLM planning, rather than pure black-box numerical optimization of proxy objectives, can significantly improve end-to-end physical design quality. Incorporating spatial reasoning over layout images and feedback from physical design tools leads to better timing and often better wirelength. The newly proposed PlaceReasoner-Bench provides a reproducible, open platform to evaluate such methods based on post-route PPA and DRC instead of pre-route proxies.

Abstract: Automated macro placement remains a fundamental challenge in VLSI physical design. Despite decades of research, existing approaches predominantly optimize hand-crafted proxy objectives, such as estimated wirelength, and typically produce placements through one-shot numerical optimization, limiting their ability to incorporate visual layout context, codified design expertise, and downstream physical-design feedback in a unified loop. We present PlaceReasoner-Beta, a verifier-guided multi-agent framework that reformulates macro placement as a closed-loop reasoning problem rather than black-box optimization. A vision-language model (VLM) planner generates candidate placements from the floorplan image, macro specifications, and connectivity structure; a geometric verifier enforces physical legality and expert placement principles; a physical verifier refines candidates using early implementation feedback; and a post-route optimizer further improves promising layouts using final PPA. To enable reproducible evaluation, we introduce PlaceReasoner-Bench, a fully open end-to-end benchmark built from open RTL designs, EDA tools, and technology libraries. It comprises 8 designs at two aspect ratios, yielding 16 tasks with fixed floorplans and I/O assignments, so methods differ only in macro positions and orientations and are evaluated using routed PPA and DRC rather than pre-route proxies. Across the benchmark, PlaceReasoner-Beta achieves the best timing among DRC-clean methods on all square tasks, reducing post-route TNS by 61.2% at 1:1 and 53.0% at 2:1 relative to the classical baseline field. It also shortens routed wirelength on most designs despite never explicitly optimizing it, demonstrating that reasoning over spatial structure under physical-design feedback can improve end-to-end layout quality beyond proxy-objective optimization.

</details>


### [83] [Efficient Benchmarking in Production: A Study of an Evolving LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21267)
*Yining She,Lei Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how to efficiently and repeatedly evaluate production LLM agents without always running the full, expensive benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production LLM analytics agents serve many users and evolve frequently, so they must be re-evaluated often. Full benchmarks are costly in time and compute, making recurring evaluations challenging. The authors want a cheaper but reliable alternative that still tracks true performance accurately over time.

Method: They use 574 historical benchmark runs from a real production analytics agent, splitting them into calibration and held-out periods. They empirically compare four recurring-evaluation strategies: (1) random sampling of benchmark questions, (2) reusing historical cached evaluations, (3) fixed representative subsets of questions, and (4) Item Response Theory (IRT) based multidimensional 2-parameter logistic (2PL) adaptive testing. They assess how well each strategy approximates full-benchmark scores versus cost (number of questions).

Result: Multidimensional 2PL adaptive testing delivers the best fidelity to the full benchmark: with only 200 questions (38.5% of the full benchmark), it achieves a mean absolute error of 1.03 percentage points relative to full-run scores. However, difficulty-stratified fixed subsets, while slightly less optimal in accuracy, are simpler to operate and still robust: they generalize without recalibration to five other LLM agent families and remain stable even when calibrated on windows as short as one day.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that IRT-based adaptive testing is the most accurate method for efficient recurring evaluation but that difficulty-stratified fixed subsets strike a better balance between accuracy and operational simplicity in real-world deployments. Based on their deployment of these methods to a production analytics agent, they propose practical recommendations for organizations needing frequent, low-cost, and reliable evaluation of evolving LLM agents.

Abstract: Production LLM agents are evaluated repeatedly as they evolve, but full agent benchmarks are costly to rerun. We study efficient recurring evaluation for a production analytics agent serving tens of thousands of monthly active users and report first-hand deployment experience. Using 574 historical runs of the production benchmark, split chronologically into calibration and held-out periods, we compare random sampling, historical caching, fixed representative subsets, and IRT-based adaptive testing. The results show that multidimensional 2PL adaptive testing achieves the best overall score fidelity: executing 200 questions, 38.5% of a full run, yields 1.03 pp of MAE. We nevertheless deployed difficulty-stratified fixed subsets because of their operational simplicity, and show they transfer without recalibration to five other agent families and remain stable across calibration windows as short as one day. Drawing on this deployment experience, we report practical recommendations for recurring production-agent evaluation.

</details>


### [84] [GameASG-Bench: Benchmarking Autonomous Software Generation for Game Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21293)
*Xiuhui Zhang,Yi Chen,Shusheng Xu,Fan Li,Huan Wang,Tongkai Yang,Binhang Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GameASG-Bench is a benchmark for testing whether autonomously generated game software meets behavioral requirements via multi-level automated checks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing autonomous software generation can produce runnable applications, but there is no guarantee that the internal behaviors of interacting components truly satisfy specified requirements. There is a need for a benchmark that embeds behavioral testability directly into the generation task, particularly for complex interactive domains like games.

Method: They design GameASG-Bench with an explicit evaluation interface defined before code generation, specifying allowed starting scenarios, player actions, stable snapshots, rejection rules, and invariants while leaving implementations open. The benchmark includes L1 static source-level checks and L2 browser-executed checks that combine semantic observations with real input and runtime evidence. They instantiate this as 47 browser-native game-generation tasks covering diverse genres and 2D/3D interactions, each having executable checks and a verified reference implementation. They then run experiments across nine agent stacks to study performance under varying tool access, turn budgets, reasoning effort, and harness configurations.

Result: Across agents, the best mean L2 check pass rate is 93.2%, but the best strict task success rate—requiring all relevant L1 and L2 checks—is only 55.3% (26 of 47 tasks). For DeepSeek-V4-Flash, giving full tool access and larger turn budgets increases the number of strict task successes, but success does not increase monotonically with added reasoning effort. Two different harnesses each achieve 18 strict task successes but only agree on 10 tasks that succeed under both, revealing inconsistencies.

Conclusion: GameASG-Bench shows that high average check pass rates can hide notable task-level noncompliance: many tasks still fail at least one critical requirement even when overall metrics look strong. The benchmark makes behavioral testability central to autonomous game software generation, and the empirical results highlight the importance of strict end-to-end success criteria, tool access, and harness design when evaluating ASG systems.

Abstract: Autonomous software generation (ASG) aims to turn human requirements into executable applications, but delivering these applications does not necessarily establish that their interacting components satisfy the specified behavioral requirements. We introduce GameASG-Bench, a benchmark that makes behavioral testability part of the generation task for game development. Our design declares an evaluation interface specification before generation, fixing legal starting scenarios, player-level actions, stable snapshots, rejection behavior, and invariants while leaving private implementations open. Concretely, we include: (i) static L1 checks that assess source-level compliance; and (ii) browser-executed L2 checks that combine semantic observations with real input and runtime evidence. We implement this protocol as 47 browser-native game-generation tasks spanning 12 primary genres and both 2D and 3D interaction, each with executable checks and an independently verified reference implementation. Our experiments answer four key questions about end-to-end agent performance, tool access and nominal turn budget, reasoning effort, and harness choice. Across nine agent stacks, the highest observed mean L2 check pass rate is 93.2%, yet the highest observed strict task success rate, requiring all L1 and applicable L2 prerequisite and core requirement checks, is only 55.3% (26/47 tasks). For DeepSeek-V4-Flash, full tool access and larger nominal turn budgets yield more strict task successes, while the strict task success rate is not monotonic in reasoning effort. Both tested harnesses achieve 18 strict task successes, but only ten tasks succeed under both. These results expose task-level compliance gaps that high average check pass rates actually obscure.

</details>


### [85] [LEGIT: Credentialing Protocol for Trustworthy AI Agent Marketplaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21325)
*Steve Drew,Jiayu Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LEGIT is a protocol to credibly certify and compare AI agents in emerging agentic marketplaces by binding performance, cost, and identity under resource constraints, and analyzing robustness against reputation manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In emerging marketplaces where autonomous AI agents offer task-solving services, buyers struggle to identify which agent is best for their specific tasks. Benchmark scores are hard to verify, often not comparable across domains, software versions, or budgets, and can be easily misrepresented. There is a need for a standardized, verifiable way to credential agents—capturing both quality and cost—while tying these credentials to persistent identities and guarding against gaming of reputation systems.

Method: The paper proposes LEGIT, a credentialing protocol that defines a structured, signed record for each evaluated agent configuration. Each credential binds together: the agent configuration, the task domain, the evaluation budget, and empirical performance and cost per solved task, plus evidence. The protocol also includes a reputation layer that aggregates past task outcomes and user feedback linked to the same identity. LEGIT supports cryptographic verification of credential records and provides optional visual profiles to help buyers interpret them. Additionally, the authors run evaluations of different agent configurations under varying budgets to study how performance and cost compare, and they analyze, under a specified Sybil-attack model, how large deposits and fees must be to deter or price reputation manipulation.

Result: Empirical evaluations show that agents with similar success rates on tasks can differ significantly in cost, and that relative comparisons between agents are sensitive to the evaluation budget used. The experiments demonstrate that binding credentials to specific configurations and resource limits is important for fair comparison. The Sybil-attack analysis yields quantitative requirements on deposits and fees such that attempts to manipulate reputation (e.g., via fake identities or coordinated attacks) become economically expensive or irrational under the assumed model.

Conclusion: LEGIT provides a structured way to certify AI agents’ performance and cost, tied to concrete configurations and resource constraints, and to accumulate verifiable reputations over time. The findings indicate that budget-aware, configuration-specific credentialing is necessary for meaningful comparison of agents in marketplaces. The economic analysis of reputation manipulation suggests that, with appropriate deposits and fees, LEGIT can make large-scale gaming of the system costly, thereby supporting more trustworthy agentic marketplaces.

Abstract: Agentic marketplaces are emerging where AI agents with varying capabilities autonomously complete specialized tasks for buyers. A major challenge of such marketplaces is that buyers cannot easily determine which agent will perform best on their tasks. Reported benchmark scores may be difficult to verify or compare across tasks, software, and budgets. We introduce LEGIT, a credentialing protocol connecting certification, reputation, and proposed marketplace allocation. Certification binds measured quality and cost per solved task to an agent configuration, task domain, evaluation budget, and evidence through a signed record. Reputation links records of past task outcomes to the same identity, subject to the reliability of the reported feedback. Buyers and agents can verify credential records and inspect optional visual profiles. Evaluations reveal cost differences between agent configurations with similar observed task success, and show that comparisons depend on the evaluation budget. These results support binding performance measurements to the tested configuration and resource limits. A complementary analysis quantifies the deposits and fees required for reputation manipulation under a stated Sybil attack model.

</details>


### [86] [Offline Multimodal Large Language Models for Decision Support in Air Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21390)
*Joao P. A. Dantas,Jelton A. Cunha,Gabriel Dietzsch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates using offline, retrieval-augmented large language models as decision-support tools for air-force image analysts, showing they can match human doctrinal test performance while operating faster and potentially reducing cognitive workload.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Air operations require analysts to apply complex doctrinal rules to text and images quickly, under strict security and low-connectivity constraints where cloud-based AI is infeasible. Analysts must manually consult doctrine and technical manuals, which is time-consuming and cognitively demanding. There is a need for offline AI tools that can provide doctrinal guidance, remain auditable and traceable to original sources, support multimodal inputs (text and images), and fit within secure, isolated environments.

Method: The authors design a modular retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) architecture that runs completely offline, ingesting doctrinal texts and images from technical manuals. The system retrieves relevant doctrine passages and uses an LLM to generate traceable, natural-language answers. They conduct a pilot study with four Brazilian Air Force image analysts. The study has two parts: (i) a doctrinal knowledge test on electronic-target identification where both human analysts and the AI system answer the same questions, and (ii) a task analysis measuring cognitive workload when analysts manually produce a reconnaissance target report (REMIR) without AI, using a workload rating scale to quantify dimensions such as mental demand and effort.

Result: In the pilot study, the manual REMIR reporting task is found to impose high cognitive load, with mean ratings of 6.0/7 for mental demand and 5.0/7 for effort, indicating that the process is demanding for analysts. On the doctrinal knowledge assessment, the proposed offline RAG system achieves the same score as human analysts (8 out of 10) but completes the assessment in 7.1 minutes on average, versus 26.5 minutes for humans, suggesting a significant efficiency gain while preserving accuracy on this test set.

Conclusion: Offline, retrieval-augmented LLMs can feasibly support air operations analysts in secure, disconnected environments by providing doctrinally grounded, traceable decision support. The pilot study establishes a performance and workload baseline, showing that the system can match human doctrinal test scores while operating much faster, and highlighting that current manual workflows are cognitively demanding. The authors propose a future evaluation protocol to systematically compare manual and AI-assisted workflows, aiming to quantify benefits in workload reduction, speed, and reliability in real operational scenarios.

Abstract: Air operations rely on complex rules, established procedures, and time-critical analysis under limited connectivity and strict security constraints. In such environments, analysts must combine written doctrine with images, often without access to external computing resources. This paper studies offline large language models as decision support tools, deployed in isolated and restricted environments to give analysts access to doctrinal knowledge that remains traceable to its original sources through natural language interaction. We describe a modular retrieval-augmented architecture suitable for operation without Internet connectivity, supporting both text and image input from technical manuals. As a first step toward evaluating this architecture, we report a pilot study with four image analysts of the Brazilian Air Force, combining (i) a doctrinal knowledge assessment based on their electronic-target identification doctrine, comparing human and proposed system performance on the same test, and (ii) a measurement of the cognitive workload involved in manually producing a reconnaissance target report (Relatório de Missão de Reconhecimento - REMIR) without AI assistance. The results show a demanding manual task, especially in terms of mental demand (6.0/7) and effort (5.0/7), while the proposed system matches the human score (8/10) and completes the assessment in 7.1 minutes (compared to a human average of 26.5 minutes), establishing a baseline for future AI-assisted evaluation. Finally, we describe a future evaluation protocol to systematically compare manual and AI-assisted workflows.

</details>


### [87] [DENSE: Distilling Agent Trajectories into Evidence-Grounded Shortcut Trees for Self-Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21423)
*Siyuan Liu,Fan Yu,Dongyu Ru,Yizhu Liu,Yifan Yang,Xuezhi Cao,Xunliang Cai,Yixin Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces DENSE, a method to distill rich feedback from unlabeled agent execution traces by constructing nested shortcut trees, and REFIT, an evaluation protocol for comparing feedback methods without using post-hoc outcome labels. DENSE improves task success and efficiency for downstream agent models while reducing reliance on external supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online agent systems generate large amounts of execution traces, but high-quality, task-specific feedback usually requires costly verification or expert annotations. The authors want to reuse these traces to guide future agents without relying on final outcome labels. They aim to capture intermediate signals such as partial progress, recovery from errors, and remaining requirements in a structured way, enabling self-refinement and more scalable learning from experience.

Method: They propose DENSE (Distilling Evidence from Nested Subtask Executions), which converts raw execution traces into evidence-grounded nested shortcut trees. The method: (1) identifies and compresses redundant attempts at subtasks into shortcuts; (2) uses recovery evidence to reconcile issues across hierarchy levels; and (3) summarizes completed branches while expanding unresolved ones, explicitly linking what has been accomplished to what remains. They also introduce REFIT, a source-paired evaluation protocol where agents reattempt the same tasks from shared initial trajectories, but with outcome labels hidden and environments reset, to compare feedback methods under post-hoc outcome blindness.

Result: On Terminal-Bench 2.1, DENSE achieves the highest strict pass rate among evaluated non-privileged feedback methods across four different recipient models. When agents rerun tasks using feedback distilled by DENSE, their strict pass rate increases by 7.12–15.64 percentage points compared to initial executions, while the number of observed recipient tokens is reduced by 19.0–43.6%. Ablation studies using GPT-5.5 show that the combination of nested subtask analysis, shortcut construction, and issue reconciliation is important for these gains.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that agent execution traces can be turned into reusable, structured feedback without relying on explicit post-hoc success labels. DENSE enables agents to self-refine by leveraging evidence of local progress and recovery embedded in prior trajectories. Together with the REFIT evaluation protocol, the work demonstrates that evidence-grounded trajectory reuse can significantly improve both effectiveness and efficiency of agents, reducing dependence on expensive external supervision.

Abstract: Online agent deployments produce abundant execution traces, while task-specific verification and expert annotation are costly to scale. We study how to distill these traces into reusable feedback without post-hoc outcome labels, drawing on their evidence of local progress, recovery, and unfinished requirements. We introduce DENSE (Distilling Evidence from Nested Subtask Executions), which organizes this evidence into evidence-grounded nested shortcut trees. DENSE compresses redundant attempts, reconciles issues across levels using recovery evidence, and summarizes completed branches while expanding unresolved ones, linking reusable progress to remaining obligations. We introduce REFIT, a source-paired protocol comparing feedback from shared initial trajectories under post-hoc outcome blindness, with environments and model contexts reset for fresh attempts at the same tasks. On Terminal-Bench 2.1, DENSE achieves the highest strict pass rate among tested non-privileged feedback methods across four recipient models. Relative to initial executions, strict pass rate improves by 7.12-15.64 pp, with 19.0-43.6% fewer observed recipient tokens in reruns. GPT-5.5 ablations support combining nested subtask analysis with shortcut construction and issue reconciliation. These findings point toward agent self-refinement through evidence-grounded trajectory reuse with less reliance on external supervision.

</details>


### [88] [GVPO++: Group Variance Policy Optimization for LLM Post-Training and On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21432)
*Kaichen Zhang,Yuzhong Hong,Junwei Bao,Hongfei Jiang,Yang Song,Dingqian Hong,Hui Xiong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes GVPO, a new, stable post-training method for LLMs that avoids importance sampling and has a unique optimal solution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing post-training methods like GRPO improve LLM reasoning and task performance but suffer from training instability due to importance sampling and lack a clean, guaranteed solution to the KL-constrained reward maximization problem.

Method: Derive an analytical solution to KL-constrained reward maximization and embed it directly into the gradient weighting scheme of a new algorithm, GVPO. Interpret the resulting gradient as minimizing the mean squared error between central distances of implicit and actual rewards, and extend the formulation to on-policy distillation and its variants.

Result: GVPO achieves a unique optimal solution to the KL-constrained reward maximization objective, removes the need for importance sampling by allowing flexible sampling distributions, and can optimize an extended family of on-policy distillation objectives.

Conclusion: GVPO provides a theoretically grounded and practically robust framework for LLM post-training and on-policy distillation, combining stability, exact optimality for the KL-constrained objective, and flexibility in designing diverse training objectives.

Abstract: Post-training plays a pivotal role in enhancing the reasoning capabilities and task-specific expertise of large language models (LLMs). Despite recent advances in post-training methods, such as Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), their practical deployment remains impeded by training instability arising from the reliance on importance sampling.
  We introduce Group Variance Policy Optimization (GVPO), a novel post-training method that integrates the analytical solution of KL-constrained reward maximization into its gradient weighting scheme. This formulation provides an intuitive interpretation: GVPO's gradient corresponds to the mean squared error between the central distance of implicit rewards and that of actual rewards. GVPO offers two key advantages: (1) it guarantees a unique optimal solution, exactly to the KL-constrained reward maximization objective, and (2) it enables flexible sampling distributions without requiring importance sampling.
  Beyond general post-training, we show that GVPO naturally extends to on-policy distillation (OPD). Furthermore, GVPO enables the optimization of a broad family of extended OPD objectives, providing a principled foundation for diverse objective design. By unifying theoretical guarantees with practical adaptability, GVPO establishes a new paradigm for reliable and versatile LLM post-training and on-policy distillation.

</details>


### [89] [Risk-Aware Occupancy for Safety-Oriented End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21470)
*Jiaxing Chen,Hengduo Zou,Yiren Zhao,Bolin Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes risk-aware occupancy, a dense BEV representation that encodes planning-relevant risk (static, map-based, and dynamic occupancy) and an E2E model ROIDrive that uses it to generate safer driving trajectories, achieving large reductions in open-loop collisions on nuScenes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing end-to-end autonomous driving systems mostly use sparse, object- and lane-based representations of the environment. In crowded, occluded, or unstructured scenes, such sparse representations struggle to robustly capture uncertain obstacles, drivable but risky regions, and complex multi-agent interactions, leading to safety risks. The authors want a representation that explicitly and uniformly captures planning-relevant risk in space and time, and a way to quantify and evaluate safety improvements.

Method: They introduce a dense, BEV (bird’s-eye-view) risk-aware occupancy representation that unifies: (1) global scene occupancy, (2) map-derived traffic constraints (e.g., lanes, rules), and (3) future dynamic agent occupancy (predicted positions of other traffic participants). This representation forms a 4D (x, y, time, risk) BEV map encoding risk cues for planning. They design an end-to-end network, ROIDrive, which has a dedicated branch to predict the risk-aware occupancy and then injects this risk representation into planning queries (in a transformer-like planning module) to produce safety-oriented trajectories. They also construct a benchmark, RiskOcc4D-nuScenes, based on nuScenes and occ3d-nuScenes, to evaluate safety through open-loop collision metrics under standard E2E driving protocols (UniAD, ST-P3).

Result: The risk-aware occupancy predictions significantly decrease estimated collision risk in open-loop evaluations. Specifically, on nuScenes, the method achieves a 52.9% relative reduction in collisions under the UniAD metric and a 35.0% reduction under the ST-P3 metric compared to baselines without such a risk-aware occupancy representation. These results indicate that explicitly modeling dense risk occupancy and feeding it into the planner improves the safety of the generated trajectories in standard E2E driving benchmarks.

Conclusion: Dense, risk-aware occupancy in BEV is an effective planning-centric representation for end-to-end autonomous driving. By unifying static scene occupancy, map constraints, and predicted dynamic agent occupancy into a single risk map and using it to guide trajectory planning, ROIDrive can generate safer trajectories than prior sparse, object-centric approaches. The proposed RiskOcc4D-nuScenes benchmark provides a way to quantify safety in open-loop settings, and the substantial collision reductions suggest that such risk-aware dense occupancy should be an important component of future E2E driving systems.

Abstract: Sparse representation formulates the environment perception for the end-to-end driving system as a set of discrete elements like objects and lane lines. This formulation meets safety risks in crowded, occluded scenes dealing with unstructured obstacles, uncertain regions, and intricate interactions. In this paper, we propose a dense representation, risk-aware occupancy, to characterize planning-relevant risks in an explicit and uniform manner. It jointly encodes global scene occupancy, map-derived traffic constraints, and future dynamic agent occupancy into a unified BEV map. The unified BEV map captures the risk evidence for trajectory planning in both spatial and temporal dimensions. We design an E2E network, ROIDrive, to realize risk-aware occupancy. It predicts risk-aware occupancy with an independent branch and injects it into planning queries for safety-oriented trajectory generation. In addition, to quantify the safety problem, we introduce RiskOcc4D-nuScenes built upon nuscenes and occ3d-nuscenes. Our risk-aware occupancy yields relative open-loop collision reductions of 52.9% under the UniAD metric and 35.0% under the ST-P3 metric on nuScenes.

</details>


### [90] [Driving on Registers, Reasoning on Risk: Risk-Aware Occupancy for Register-Based End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21486)
*Jiaxing Chen,Hengduo Zou,YuKai Qin,Yiren Zhao,Lidong Yu,Bolin Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces RRDrive, a risk-aware occupancy representation to improve multimodal trajectory prediction and selection in end-to-end autonomous driving, leading to better candidate quality, ranking reliability, and overall performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal trajectory prediction methods for end-to-end autonomous driving rely on sparse scene representations, which provide incomplete evidence about the environment. This sparsity causes two main issues: poor generation of candidate trajectories and unreliable ranking among geometrically similar trajectories. As a result, a significant portion of candidates are bad, and the best possible (oracle) trajectory is often ranked low. The authors are motivated to design a richer, denser scene representation that can better inform both trajectory generation and selection, especially in challenging scenes.

Method: The authors propose RRDrive, which uses a risk-aware occupancy representation that is dense, temporally aligned, and directly queryable by candidate trajectories. The representation encodes risk across space and time, forming a global occupancy structure. This structure guides multimodal trajectory generation and allows candidate-conditioned risk queries for fine-grained selection of trajectories. Additionally, they build RiskOcc4D-NAVSIM, a dataset or simulation environment with automatic risk annotations. To move beyond oracle access, they further design an external RiskOcc predictor, a perception module that predicts risk-aware occupancy from raw sensor inputs.

Result: RRDrive significantly improves both trajectory selection and candidate quality. The proportion of bad and poor candidates in the baseline is highlighted (19.74%), and the oracle-best candidate ranks around 33.9th. With RRDrive, the selected trajectory's PDMS reaches 0.951, a 1.5% relative improvement over the 0.937 baseline, and the average candidate PDMS improves by 7.7%. In challenging scenes, candidate PDMS improves by 30.2%. Additionally, the Spearman correlation among good candidates increases markedly from 0.26 to 0.67, indicating more reliable ranking. The external RiskOcc predictor achieves competitive performance, demonstrating that the risk-aware occupancy representation can be effectively estimated from sensor data.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that risk-aware occupancy is an effective dense scene representation for multimodal trajectory prediction in end-to-end autonomous driving. RRDrive leverages this representation to improve both the generation and ranking of trajectory candidates, especially in difficult scenarios. The construction of RiskOcc4D-NAVSIM and the development of an external RiskOcc predictor show that the approach is practical and not limited to oracle access. Overall, the representation leads to higher-quality, better-ranked trajectories and validates risk-aware occupancy as a promising direction for autonomous driving systems.

Abstract: Multimodal trajectory prediction improves behavioral coverage in end-to-end autonomous driving, but existing methods remain limited by sparse scene representations. Incomplete evidence leads to low-quality candidate generation and unreliable ranking among geometrically similar trajectories. On a register-based baseline, bad and poor candidates constitute 19.74% of the candidate set, while the oracle-best candidate ranks only 33.9th on average. We propose RRDrive, which introduces risk-aware occupancy as a dense, temporally aligned, and trajectory-queryable representation. Its global structure guides high-quality multimodal generation, while candidate-conditioned risk queries support fine-grained selection. We further construct RiskOcc4D-NAVSIM with automatic risk annotations. RRDrive achieves a selected-trajectory PDMS of 0.951, representing a 1.5% relative improvement over the baseline (0.937), and improves the average candidate PDMS by 7.7%. In challenging scenes, it improves candidate PDMS by 30.2% and increases the Spearman correlation among good candidates by 0.41, from 0.26 to 0.67. To move beyond this oracle setting, we further develop an external RiskOcc predictor, a perception module that estimates risk-aware occupancy directly from sensor inputs. The competitive performance validates the representation's feasibility.

</details>


### [91] [LogicTrack: Auditing Reasoning Trajectories of Large Language Models with Formal Logic Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21492)
*Jingyu Hu,Shu Yang,Weiru Liu,Di Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces LogicTrack, a neuro-symbolic framework that verifies each step of chain-of-thought reasoning using formal logic and theorem provers, improving both reasoning validity and final accuracy of LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing chain-of-thought (CoT) methods improve LLM performance but mostly rely on final-answer supervision. This allows models to reach correct answers via logically invalid intermediate steps, which is problematic for reliability, especially in high-stakes domains where the reasoning process must be sound and auditable.

Method: LogicTrack automatically translates each intermediate reasoning step into a symbolic logical form and checks its validity with automated theorem provers. It defines a Solver-Based Backtracking Reward (SBR), a step-wise score that reflects logical soundness and drives a backtracking tree search during inference. Additionally, the framework uses the generated audited trajectories and backtracking traces as supervised fine-tuning data so that models can learn to perform internal step-wise auditing themselves.

Result: Across 8 reasoning benchmarks and 7 different LLMs, using LogicTrack improves both (1) the proportion of reasoning chains that are logically verifiable step by step, and (2) the final answer accuracy (pass rate), showing better CoT quality and robustness compared to baselines.

Conclusion: Neuro-symbolic step-wise auditing via LogicTrack makes CoT reasoning more logically sound, transparent, and reliable. By integrating theorem-prover-based verification and SBR-guided search, and then distilling this behavior into fine-tuned models, the approach enhances both trustworthiness and performance of LLM reasoning, particularly for high-stakes scenarios.

Abstract: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning has been shown to improve the performance of large language models (LLMs), yet existing optimization methods largely rely on outcome-based feedback, leaving the logical validity of intermediate reasoning steps largely unverified. To address the gap whereby LLMs arrive at correct final answers through logically flawed intermediate reasoning chains, we propose LogicTrack, a neuro-symbolic framework that audits reasoning trajectories by auto-formalizing each reasoning step into symbolic representations and verifying it with automated theorem provers. LogicTrack introduces Solver-Based Backtracking Reward (SBR), a step-wise scoring mechanism that quantifies logical soundness and guides backtracking tree search at inference time. We further extend LogicTrack to construct supervised fine-tuning (SFT) data with backtracking traces from its trajectories, enabling fine-tuned models to internalize step-wise auditing as an intrinsic capability. Extensive experiments across 8 reasoning benchmarks and 7 LLMs demonstrate that LogicTrack effectively improves both the verifiability of reasoning chains and final answer pass rate, thereby enhancing overall CoT quality and trustworthiness in high-stakes domains.

</details>


### [92] [PolyBridgeBench: Benchmarking Multimodal LLMs for Physics-Grounded Bridge Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21493)
*Zicheng Zhao,Dongyin Chen,Rui Xu,Yinghui Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PolyBridgeBench is a new executable benchmark that tests whether multimodal LLMs can design, simulate, and repair load‑bearing bridges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal LLM benchmarks check visual understanding and some structural or physics reasoning, but they do not reveal whether models can generate full engineering designs that actually work when executed and can be fixed after failure. The authors want a benchmark that measures end‑to‑end engineering design ability, including reacting to failures revealed by simulation.

Method: They build PolyBridgeBench, where a model is given a visual scene plus structured engineering constraints and must output a complete bridge design as a graph of nodes, members, and materials. Designs are first checked by deterministic legality rules, then executed in a dynamic physics simulator. If the bridge fails, the model receives temporal visual evidence of the failure and a limited interaction budget to propose repairs. Metrics separately quantify (1) deterministic validity, (2) dynamic functional success in simulation, and (3) quality of post‑failure repair.

Result: Running six representative MLLMs on 189 bridge‑building levels shows that models can often pass the legality checks but still fail in dynamic simulation, revealing a large gap between syntactic correctness and actual functionality. Performance is very sensitive to the available material budget, and models struggle to successfully repair failing designs under the main strict interaction‑budget setting.

Conclusion: PolyBridgeBench reveals that existing MLLMs are far from robust end‑to‑end engineering designers: they can produce legal but dynamically unsound bridges and have weak abilities to diagnose and repair failures given simulated feedback. The benchmark offers a more realistic, executable evaluation of multimodal engineering design and highlights key shortcomings for future model development.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models, or MLLMs, perform well at visual understanding and structured generation, yet these capabilities do not establish whether an engineering design will work when executed. Existing benchmarks assess spatial reasoning, structural validity, or physics-grounded construction, but they do not determine whether MLLMs can synthesize complete load-bearing structures and repair them after simulator execution exposes a failure. We introduce PolyBridgeBench, an executable benchmark for multimodal bridge design. A model receives a visual scene and structured engineering constraints and generates a complete node--member--material topology. Deterministic legality checks gate execution in a native dynamic physics simulation. Following an execution failure, the benchmark returns temporal visual evidence from the failed rollout and evaluates repair under a fixed interaction budget. Separate measurements of deterministic validity, dynamic functional success, and post-failure recovery identify the stage at which design fails. Experiments with six representative MLLMs across 189 levels expose a substantial gap between deterministic validity and dynamic success, pronounced sensitivity to material budgets, and limited post-failure recovery under the primary strict-budget setting.

</details>


### [93] [The Communication Bottleneck: A Round-Trip Study of Tree-Structured Expression Serialization in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21509)
*Xavier Suau,Alex Ferrando de las Morenas,Luca Zappella,Samy Bengio*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how well language models can encode and decode tree-structured arithmetic expressions when forced through natural-language descriptions, using a generator–extractor round-trip protocol to quantify information loss and trainability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models often “reason” using chain-of-thought or free-text messages, implicitly encoding structured, often tree-like information (e.g., arithmetic or logical compositions) into natural language. However, it is unclear how much of this compositional, tree-structured information is faithfully preserved when serialized into text and then re-interpreted. The authors want to measure this loss precisely, understand whether it is mainly caused by the generation or the interpretation side, and see whether targeted fine-tuning can improve this natural-language communication channel for hierarchical structure.

Method: They design a round-trip evaluation protocol for tree-structured arithmetic expressions. First, a generator model takes a procedurally generated arithmetic expression (with known tree structure) and converts it into a natural-language word problem. Then, a separate extractor model sees only the word problem and attempts to reconstruct the original arithmetic expression. Because the expressions are symbolic trees, exact equivalence can be checked using symbolic methods, giving an oracle for correctness. They apply this protocol across all pairwise combinations of sixteen language models, building a communication matrix that separates generation quality (row effects) from extraction quality (column effects). They analyze failure cases with respect to tree depth, operator count, branching patterns, and model family. They further fine-tune open-weight models on ~3600 examples that match the operators and tree shapes used in evaluation, and also test a disjoint-domain setting with new operators and vocabulary to probe generalization.

Result: The experiments show that the natural-language channel for tree-structured content is lossy and asymmetric: swapping which model is generator vs extractor can change accuracy by as much as 60.4 percentage points. The highest accuracy (92.9%) comes from pairing two different models—one best at generation and another best at extraction—rather than using the same model for both roles. Analysis indicates that at least 73.6% of round-trip errors come from the generation step, and that difficulty correlates with structural properties of the expression (number of operators, tree depth, right-branching) instead of with model family. Fine-tuning on around 3600 in-domain examples substantially improves performance for every open-weight model, pushing them all above the performance of an untrained Gemini-3.1-Pro under matched semantics. In a harder, disjoint-domain setting with new operators and vocabulary, fine-tuning still improves all open-weight models, though a performance gap to top frontier models remains.

Conclusion: Tree-structured expression serialization into natural language is a core bottleneck for language models communicating hierarchical structure: the process is significantly lossy, especially on the generation side, and sensitive to tree complexity. Nonetheless, the channel is trainable: modest amounts of targeted fine-tuning appreciably enhance both in-domain and disjoint-domain performance across open-weight models, though they still lag behind frontier systems. Overall, the work highlights that improving how models verbalize and re-parse structured content is key to advancing chain-of-thought style reasoning and multi-model communication via natural language.

Abstract: When language models reason in chain-of-thought or exchange free-text intermediates, they serialize structured information into natural language. How much tree-structured compositional content survives this bottleneck? We propose a round-trip protocol that answers this question empirically for tree-structured expressions. A generator converts a procedurally generated arithmetic expression into a word problem, a separate extractor recovers the expression from the word problem alone, and symbolic equivalence provides an exact oracle. Evaluating all pairwise combinations of sixteen models yields a communication matrix whose marginals separate generation quality from extraction quality. Three main findings emerge. First, the channel is lossy and asymmetric: swapping which model generates and which extracts shifts accuracy by up to 60.4 points, and the best pair reaches 92.9% by combining different models on each end rather than the same model on both. Second, at least 73.6% of round-trip failures originate at generation, and difficulty is driven by tree structure (operator count, depth, right-branching) rather than model family. Third, the channel is trainable: ~3600 fine-tuning examples that share the evaluation's operators and tree shapes lift every open-weight model above untrained Gemini-3.1-Pro, an upper bound under matched semantics. A disjoint-domain regime with new operators and vocabulary also raises every open-weight model, confirming the gain is not an artifact of matched semantics, though a gap to the frontier remains. Together these results identify tree-structured expression serialization as a primary limiting factor when models communicate hierarchical structure through natural language.

</details>


### [94] [Learning-to-Optimize as the Missing Architectural Layer of AI-Native Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21519)
*Giambattista Amati,Federica Mangiatordi,Pierpaolo Salvo,Emiliano Pallotti,Simone Angelini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Learning-to-Optimize (L2O) as a missing architectural layer in AI-native communication networks, where optimization algorithms act as offline knowledge generators to train neural surrogate models for fast, near-optimal decisions at runtime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-native communication networks require embedded intelligence for autonomous and low-latency operation, but current architectures lack a systematic way to generate, transfer, and exploit optimization knowledge within AI models. There is a gap between classical optimization engines and distributed AI components that must act in real time.

Method: The authors conceptualize L2O as an architectural paradigm and define a generic four-stage workflow: (1) offline optimization to solve network problems, (2) knowledge generation where solutions and related artifacts are captured as supervisory data, (3) surrogate learning in which neural models (e.g., Graph Neural Networks) are trained on this data, and (4) runtime inference where these surrogates make fast decisions. They demonstrate the paradigm on an NR-V2X relay-selection problem using a MILP solver to generate training data for a GNN.

Result: In the NR-V2X relay-selection example, the GNN surrogate, trained on MILP-generated solutions, is able to reproduce near-optimal relay-selection decisions in real time, showing that optimization knowledge can be successfully distilled into neural models that meet strict latency constraints.

Conclusion: Learning-to-Optimize can serve as a foundational architectural layer that bridges optimization and AI-native intelligence in communication networks. By redefining optimization as an offline knowledge generator for neural surrogates, the framework enables low-latency, near-optimal decision making across heterogeneous communication and computing systems, positioning L2O as a key enabler for future AI-native networks.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) is becoming a fundamental design principle of future AI-native communication networks, enabling autonomous resource management, adaptive control, and zero-touch network operation. While current AI-native architectures increasingly embed intelligence across network functions, they provide little guidance on how optimisation knowledge should be systematically generated, transferred, and exploited by AI models. This paper argues that the Learning-to-Optimize (L2O) represents the missing architectural layer between optimisation and AI-native intelligence. Rather than viewing optimisation merely as an online decision engine, the proposed paradigm redefines optimisation algorithms as offline knowledge generators that produce high-quality supervisory information for neural surrogate models. The resulting models inherit optimisation expertise while enabling low-latency runtime inference suitable for dynamic network environments. A generic four-stage L2O workflow is introduced, comprising optimisation, knowledge generation, surrogate learning, and runtime inference. Unlike existing Learning-to-Optimize approaches, which primarily focus on algorithm acceleration, the proposed framework establishes L2O as an architectural abstraction applicable across heterogeneous communication and computing systems. The proposed paradigm is illustrated by an NR-V2X relay-selection problem, in which optimisation-generated solutions from a Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP) solver are used to train a Graph Neural Network that can reproduce near-optimal decisions in real time. The presented perspective positions Learning-to-Optimize as a key architectural enabler for future AI-native networks.

</details>


### [95] [Dual-Interest Sequential Product Recommendation With Multi-Granular SSM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21548)
*Shuiying Liao,P. Y. Mok*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose DSRec, a dual-interest cross-SSM model that uses separate long-term and short-term state space encoders plus a residual cross-fusion mechanism to better capture item polysemy and temporal dynamics for sequential recommendation, outperforming prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sequential recommenders using Transformers are powerful but computationally costly, and existing SSM-based approaches treat each item with a single static role, failing to model item polysemy and different user intents across temporal scales (long-term preferences vs short-term session intent). The paper aims to build an efficient model that captures both long-range dependencies and dynamic, context-dependent item semantics across multiple time granularities.

Method: They design DSRec, which splits a user’s interaction sequence into two semantic streams: a long-term interest branch and a short-term interest branch. Items are encoded into long-term interest embeddings via historical aggregation to represent stable preferences, and into short-term interest embeddings highlighting recent session intent and inter-click time intervals. Each branch is processed with a dedicated State Space Model encoder: a full-sequence Mamba SSM for long-term modeling, and a time-modulated SSM whose state transition is conditioned on temporal gaps for short-term modeling. A residual cross-fusion module periodically exchanges information between the branches to align their representations while keeping them semantically disentangled. The final user representation integrates both branches for next-item prediction.

Result: On standard public sequential recommendation benchmarks, DSRec achieves higher accuracy metrics (e.g., Hit Rate, NDCG) than previous state-of-the-art Transformer- and SSM-based baselines, showing that explicit dual-interest modeling and time-modulated SSMs improve recommendation quality.

Conclusion: Explicitly disentangling long-term and short-term interests and modeling them with dedicated, interacting SSM branches leads to more expressive and cost-efficient sequential recommendation. DSRec captures item polysemy and temporal dynamics better than prior methods, suggesting dual-interest cross-SSM architectures as a promising direction for future sequential recommender systems.

Abstract: Sequential recommendation aims to predict the next item a user will interact with based on their historical behavior. Advances in Transformers have significantly improved sequential recommendation but are still limited by cost efficiency. Although State Space Models (SSMs) have recently enabled efficient long-range modeling, most existing methods encode each item with a single static contextual role, overlooking the phenomenon of item polysemy. In fact, the same item often plays different semantic roles depending on user context, and existing methods are limited in capturing dynamic behavior across different temporal granularities. In this work, we propose DSRec, a novel dual-interest cross-SSM model that explicitly disentangles item roles across long-term and short-term semantic context. Sequential items are encoded into long-term interest embeddings that capture stable preferences via historical aggregation, and a short-term interest branch that emphasizes local session intent modulated by inter-click time intervals. These interest embeddings are processed through distinct SSM encoders: a full-sequence Mamba for long-term modeling, and a time-modulated SSM that dynamically adjusts state evolution based on temporal gaps. To enable effective cross-granularity alignment, we adopt a residual cross-fusion mechanism that exchanges contextual information between the two branches while preserving semantic independence. Experiments on public benchmarks demonstrate that DSRec outperforms other state-of-the-art methods.

</details>


### [96] [Beyond Accuracy: Centroid-Guided Contrastive Loss for Structured Fraudulent Job Posting Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21599)
*Syed Ali Ahmed,Malaika Raza,Muhammad Shoaib Siddiqui,Muhammad Rafi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a new loss function, Centroid-Guided Contrastive Loss (CGCL), that jointly improves classification accuracy and clustering structure in the latent space for fraudulent job posting detection, achieving SOTA on the EMSCAD dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing fraudulent job posting detectors struggle to both classify accurately and learn a well-structured latent space that captures nuanced differences among fake posts. Prior methods typically optimize for classification alone or use weak clustering regularization, leading to suboptimal decision boundaries and poor interpretability or robustness. The paper aims to design a loss that tightly couples classification with strong latent-space clustering to better separate genuine and fraudulent job postings and capture intra-class structure.

Method: They introduce Centroid-Guided Contrastive Loss (CGCL), a loss that combines supervised classification with a centroid-based contrastive clustering objective. For each class, centroids are learned in latent space. During training, representations are guided by a top-k push-and-pull mechanism: samples are pulled toward their own class centroid(s) and pushed away from the most similar centroids of other classes. This dense, centroid-driven formulation is applied jointly with the classifier, so that latent-space structure and decision boundaries co-evolve. CGCL is plugged into a standard neural model for text-based job posting classification and trained end-to-end.

Result: On the EMSCAD public benchmark for fraudulent job posting detection, the CGCL-based model achieves state-of-the-art performance, outperforming existing baselines in classification metrics (e.g., accuracy and related measures) while also showing more compact, well-separated latent clusters. Visualizations and quantitative clustering metrics confirm improved latent structure compared to prior methods.

Conclusion: CGCL effectively unifies classification and clustering within a single loss, enabling models to learn both accurate decision boundaries and meaningful latent-space organization. This leads to better detection of fraudulent job postings and richer representation of subtle variations within fake and real classes. The approach is general and can be applied to similar tasks that need both high discriminative performance and structured representations; code is publicly released for reproducibility.

Abstract: Fraudulent job posting detection aims to identify job advertisements that are corrupted either through fake content, misleading information, or negative intent, disrupting the online eco-system of job-seekers and employers. Existing studies in this domain lack effective methods to simultaneously achieve high accuracy and meaningful structure of latent-space representations that capture subtleties among fake posts. To this end, we propose Centroid-Guided Contrastive Loss (CGCL), a loss function which unifies classification with densely formulated clustering to consistently reshape latent-space through a centroid-driven top-$k$ push-and-pull mechanism. The complementary nature of CGCL enables the model to enforce accurate decision boundaries and maintain high clustering compactness, effectively capturing both class separability and latent structure. Extensive experiments demonstrate the state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance of our method on EMSCAD, a public benchmark dataset. The code associated with this work is available at: https://github.com/ali-ahmed925/CGCL_code

</details>


### [97] [Reducing Barriers to Academic Support: Evaluating a Course-Specific RAG System for Addressing Help-Seeking Disparities in Higher Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21600)
*Andy Gray,Jake Hobbs*

Main category: cs.AI
