TL;DR: The paper introduces and evaluates Beacon, a course-specific RAG-based AI assistant designed to give private, module-aligned support to computing students, reducing help-seeking barriers without replacing lecturers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Students need academic support to succeed, but many hesitate to ask for help due to anxiety, fear of judgment, or low confidence. In computing, where programming tasks are cumulative and demanding, this problem is acute. While students increasingly use general-purpose generative AI, such tools can be inaccurate, lack course context, and misalign with expectations. There is a need for a trustworthy, course-specific AI support system that aligns with teaching materials and encourages independent learning while lowering barriers to help-seeking.

Method: The authors used a design-based research approach to iteratively design and refine Beacon, a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) system grounded in approved course materials. They evaluated Beacon using mixed methods at a Higher Education institution: questionnaires and semi-structured interviews with both students and staff to understand perceptions of alignment, trustworthiness, and its role in the support ecosystem.

Result: Students perceived Beacon’s responses as tightly aligned with module content and more trustworthy than open, general-purpose AI systems. They appreciated the use of pseudocode and scaffolded explanations rather than being given direct solutions. Users still expressed caution about fully trusting AI outputs without verification but tended to see Beacon as a useful first port of call before reaching out to lecturers or official resources.

Conclusion: Course-specific RAG-based AI systems like Beacon can effectively occupy an intermediary role between independent study and formal academic support. When carefully designed and grounded in course materials, such systems can lower barriers to help-seeking, increase access to guidance, and complement—rather than replace—the pedagogical role of lecturers.

Abstract: Access to academic support is a key determinant of student success, yet students experience it unequally: some readily seek help from lecturers or tutors, while others hesitate due to anxiety, fear of judgement, uncertainty about expectations, or low confidence in their understanding. This may be especially evident in computing education, where programming tasks are cumulative and cognitively demanding. Although students increasingly turn to general-purpose generative AI tools, these can produce responses that are inaccurate, insufficiently contextualised, or misaligned with module expectations. This study presents and evaluates Beacon, a course-specific Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) system providing private, immediate, module-aligned academic support. Grounding responses in approved teaching materials, Beacon was designed to lower barriers to help-seeking while encouraging independent learning. Using a design-based research approach, Beacon was developed iteratively and evaluated via mixed methods, combining questionnaires and semi-structured interviews with students and staff at a Higher Education institution. Students described Beacon's responses as closely aligned with module content and more trustworthy than unrestricted generative AI tools, valuing its use of pseudocode and scaffolded explanations over direct solutions. Although participants remained cautious about trusting AI-generated responses without verification, they viewed the system as a valuable first point of support before consulting lecturers or official resources. The findings suggest that carefully designed course-specific AI systems may reduce barriers to academic support by occupying an intermediary space between independent study and formal support. Rather than replacing educators, educational AI may be most valuable when it broadens access to guidance while preserving the pedagogical role of lecturers.

</details>


### [98] [Calibrating Teacher--Student Discrepancy for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21619)
*Qiangqiang He,Jin Li,MingCai Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Calibrated On-Policy Distillation (Cal-OPD), which filters out teacher’s own deviations from the distillation signal so students learn only genuine capability gaps, improving reasoning performance even with a reduced supervision signal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard on-policy distillation uses the likelihood difference between teacher and student as a learning signal, but this difference mixes true capability gaps with idiosyncratic errors and shifts from the teacher model. Privileged OPD amplifies this problem because privileged information makes the teacher diverge more from its base behavior, causing the student to imitate teacher-side deviations instead of just learning to be more capable. This contamination of the signal can limit or misdirect student improvement, motivating a method that isolates and removes teacher self-deviation from the distillation target.

Method: They propose Cal-OPD, which first estimates the teacher’s self-deviation region by applying both positive and negative privileged interventions to the teacher and measuring how its token-level likelihoods change. This defines a range of natural teacher variability that is considered non-informative deviation. During distillation, they then calibrate the teacher–student discrepancy by truncating or filtering out the part of the discrepancy that lies within this estimated deviation region, keeping only the excess difference that likely corresponds to real capability gaps. Training is carried out with this calibrated discrepancy as the optimization signal instead of the raw OPD signal.

Result: On mathematical reasoning benchmarks and across multiple model scales, Cal-OPD achieves better reasoning performance than standard OPD and its privileged variants. Notably, it does so while only using about 52–65% of the original teacher–student discrepancy magnitude as the learning signal, indicating that much of the raw OPD signal is noisy teacher deviation that can be safely discarded without hurting—and even while improving—student learning.

Conclusion: Teacher–student token-level discrepancies in OPD are not a clean measure of capability gaps because they are contaminated by teacher self-deviations, especially in privileged OPD settings. By explicitly estimating and subtracting a teacher self-deviation region via privileged interventions, Cal-OPD can focus the distillation process on genuine capability differences, leading to more efficient and effective training of reasoning models even with a significantly reduced supervision signal.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) improves reasoning models by learning the token-level discrepancy between a stronger teacher and an on-policy student. However, this discrepancy does not purely reflect the capability gap between the teacher and the student: it also contains deviations arising from the teacher itself, which are consequently mixed into the observed teacher--student discrepancy and indiscriminately learned by standard OPD during training. This issue is further exacerbated by privileged OPD, where privileged information induces larger teacher-side likelihood shifts, thereby encouraging the student to learn more of the teacher's own deviation. We introduce \textbf{Calibrated On-Policy Distillation (Cal-OPD)}, which estimates the teacher's self-deviation region through positive and negative privileged interventions and calibrates the original teacher--student discrepancy by retaining only the component that lies beyond this region. Experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks show that, while retaining only about 52--65\% of the original teacher--student discrepancy as the optimization signal, Cal-OPD consistently outperforms standard OPD and its variants across model scales.

</details>


### [99] [One Prompt Does Not Fit All: Self-Meta-Evolve for Personalized Information Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21626)
*Hongliang Li,Lu Wang,Yong Xu,Hanyang Chen,Zhitao Hou,Xiaoting Qin,Song Ge,Qingwei Lin,Dongmei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a hierarchical framework, Self-Meta-Evolve, for adapting prompts of large language models to individual enterprise users for information extraction, using interaction feedback and persona-driven evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In enterprise environments, many different users need to extract and reorganize information from the same documents, but each user has unique preferences and requirements. Existing prompt optimization methods use a single, globally optimized prompt, which does not capture this user heterogeneity and thus performs suboptimally for specific users. There is a need for a scalable way to personalize prompts for many users while leveraging their feedback.

Method: The authors formulate enterprise information extraction as a per-user prompt adaptation problem under interaction feedback. They introduce Self-Meta-Evolve, a hierarchical dual-loop framework that maintains a separate structured prompt for each user. The inner loop edits each user’s prompt using their persona-conditioned feedback, while the outer loop updates a meta-prompt by distilling patterns from effective edits across users. They also construct a persona-driven IE benchmark with 292 simulated enterprise users, generated via a pipeline grounded in O*NET occupational taxonomies, to train and evaluate the framework.

Result: On the persona-driven IE benchmark, Self-Meta-Evolve reaches a 74.58% success rate, outperforming the strongest existing prompt optimization baseline by 13.56 absolute percentage points, and it already reaches 52.54% success within just two adaptation iterations. In a double-blind human study with twenty real professionals, prompts adapted by Self-Meta-Evolve are preferred over static baselines in 71% of pairwise comparisons.

Conclusion: Per-user adaptive prompt optimization with a hierarchical self-evolving framework is more effective than global, static prompts for enterprise information extraction. Self-Meta-Evolve leverages user feedback and meta-learning over prompt edits to achieve better personalization and performance, validated both on a large simulated persona benchmark and in a real-user study.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed for enterprise information extraction (IE), where the same document must be reorganized differently for each user. Existing prompt optimization methods, however, rely on a single prompt optimized against a global objective, which is misaligned with the inherent user heterogeneity of real workplaces. We formulate enterprise IE as per-user prompt adaptation under interaction feedback and propose Self-Meta-Evolve, a hierarchical framework that maintains a dedicated prompt for each user and continuously refines it through a dual-loop process: an inner loop that edits structured prompts based on persona-conditioned feedback, and an outer loop that evolves the meta-prompt itself by distilling successful editing patterns. To enable scalable training and evaluation, we release a persona-driven IE benchmark of 292 simulated enterprise users, paired with a reproducible persona-generation pipeline grounded in O*NET occupational taxonomies. On this benchmark, Self-Meta-Evolve achieves a 74.58% success rate, outperforming the strongest prompt-optimization baseline by 13.56 absolute points, and reaches 52.54\% within only two iterations. A double-blind human study with twenty real professionals further confirms that prompts adapted by our framework win against static baselines in 71% of pairwise comparisons.

</details>


### [100] [Accelerating Dense LLMs via L0-regularized Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21672)
*Zhenyu Zhang,Jiudong Yang,Zhaowen Tao,Meng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes L0-MoE, a lightweight Mixture-of-Experts method that accelerates large language models using L0-regularization, achieving up to 2.5x speedup with minimal performance loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are powerful but their inference is slow and expensive. Existing acceleration techniques tend to degrade performance, and standard MoE architectures demand large computational resources. There is a need for an approach that speeds up dense LLMs while preserving accuracy and keeping resource usage low.

Method: The authors design L0-MoE, a lightweight Mixture-of-Experts framework that uses L0-regularization to sparsify expert usage in dense LLMs. They construct a cluster confusion matrix to guide domain-aware dataset curation and employ dynamic batching strategies to improve training efficiency. This combination allows them to selectively activate experts while keeping the model and training pipeline efficient.

Result: On experimental benchmarks, L0-MoE delivers up to 2.5x inference speedup compared to dense LLMs, while maintaining competitive performance. It surpasses other LLM acceleration baselines in the accuracy–speed trade-off, indicating that its sparsity and routing mechanism effectively retain model quality.

Conclusion: L0-MoE is an effective and resource-efficient method to accelerate dense LLMs. By leveraging L0-regularization, domain-aware data curation via a cluster confusion matrix, and dynamic batching, it provides substantial speedups with minimal accuracy loss, offering a promising alternative to conventional acceleration and heavy MoE architectures.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) achieve strong performance but suffer from slow and costly inference. Existing acceleration methods often lead to noticeable performance degradation, while Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models require extensive computational resources. In this paper, we propose L0-MoE, a lightweight MoE approach using L0-regularization to accelerate dense LLMs nearly without performance loss. Our method introduces a cluster confusion matrix for domain-aware dataset curation and applies dynamic batching for efficient training. Experiments show that L0-MoE achieves up to 2.5x speedup over dense models while maintaining competitive performance, outperforming existing LLM acceleration baselines.

</details>


### [101] [GUARD: Natural Forgetting in Large Reasoning Models via Guided Answer-Reasoning Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21677)
*Zeyu Yan,Guanghao Zhou,Minghui Qiu,Ming Gao,Cen Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces GUARD, a framework for machine unlearning in large reasoning models that enforces safe, coherent forgetting trajectories in chain-of-thought reasoning, and proposes a new metric (NFRS) to evaluate the quality of such replacements beyond simple leakage control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With large reasoning models that generate explicit chain-of-thought, simply suppressing or redirecting unsafe or private content is insufficient, because sensitive information can appear in intermediate reasoning steps and naive unlearning can cause hallucinations, unstable refusals, or malformed outputs. There is a need for methods that shape how a model forgets unsafe knowledge, producing coherent, non-disclosing reasoning paths that end in robust refusal answers, and for metrics that evaluate the quality of these replacement trajectories, not just whether leakage occurs.

Method: The authors propose Guided Answer-Reasoning Distillation (GUARD). They first convert unsafe, model-generated disclosures into safe-exit trajectories: chain-of-thought traces that do not reveal protected information and end with a stable refusal-style answer. They then guide a frozen large reasoning model using special guidance tokens to follow these safe-exit trajectories. Finally, they distill this guided behavior into the model’s trainable parameters, aligning its internal reasoning and final outputs to the desired forgetting behavior. In addition, they design the Natural Forgetting Reasoning Score (NFRS), a metric that evaluates post-unlearning outputs on structural stability of reasoning, linguistic fluency, and the absence of hallucinated or unsupported substitute facts.

Result: On benchmarks including R-TOFU and a harmful-intent setting derived from STAR-1, GUARD significantly reduces unsafe and privacy-related disclosures in two popular distilled large reasoning models. At the same time, it maintains or preserves their general reasoning capabilities, as measured by standard utility metrics, and achieves higher scores on the proposed NFRS, indicating more natural, coherent forgetting trajectories compared to prior unlearning methods.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that effective machine unlearning for large reasoning models must shape entire forgetting trajectories rather than only suppressing specific tokens or representations. GUARD provides a practical way to transform unsafe reasoning into stable refusal-style behavior and to distill this behavior into the model, while the NFRS metric offers a nuanced evaluation of replacement quality. Together, these contributions advance safe unlearning in chain-of-thought models without sacrificing reasoning performance.

Abstract: Recent advances in large reasoning models (LRMs) have made machine unlearning more challenging, as protected facts or unsafe rationales may surface in intermediate chain-of-thought (CoT) traces before the final answer is produced. Existing unlearning objectives typically suppress the target content or redirect internal representations, but they never specify how the post-forgetting trajectory should continue, which can lead to hallucinated substitutes, malformed boundaries, or repetitive outputs. We argue that LRM unlearning should instead learn a natural forgetting trajectory: a coherent non-disclosing CoT followed by a stable refusal-style answer that replace the original disclosure. To this end, we propose Guided Answer-Reasoning Distillation (GUARD), which converts model-generated unsafe disclosures into safe-exit trajectories, aligns a frozen LRM via guidance tokens, and distills the guided behavior into model parameters.To address the lack of metrics for replacement quality beyond leakage, we further introduce Natural Forgetting Reasoning Score (NFRS), which captures structural stability, fluency, and unsupported substitutes in forgotten outputs. Extensive experiments on R-TOFU and a STAR-1-derived harmful-intent setting show that GUARD substantially reduces unsafe and privacy disclosures across two widely adopted distilled LRMs while preserving reasoning utility. Codes are available at https://github.com/zeyu-Yan/GUARD

</details>


### [102] [Listen Before You Speak: Response Planning from Listener Facial Reactions for Conversational Speech Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21683)
*Yunji Chu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ReACT-TTS, a text-to-speech framework that uses the listener’s immediate facial reactions to better plan emotional and prosodic aspects of conversational speech responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conversational speech is influenced not just by dialogue history and text content, but also by the listener’s real-time reactions. Existing TTS systems largely ignore these pre-response visual cues, limiting their ability to generate contextually appropriate, emotionally aligned speech. The authors aim to leverage the listener’s immediate facial dynamics to improve conversational response planning and speech naturalness.

Method: They propose ReACT-TTS, a two-stage framework. First, it takes a one-second pre-response facial sequence of the listener (and dialogue context) to predict the upcoming utterance’s emotion and prosody (e.g., valence-arousal-dominance and style). This includes temporal modeling over the visual sequence and comparison against a text-only baseline and other visual variants, as well as tests with correct vs. mismatched (cyclic) listener reactions. Second, the predicted response style is conditioned into a Grad-TTS backbone to generate the final speech waveform. Experiments are conducted on a strict dyadic MELD protocol with multiple random seeds.

Result: Temporal visual conditioning with the one-second listener facial sequence improves mean macro-F1 and VAD concordance over a Text-only baseline, while accuracy remains about the same. Among visual approaches, full temporal modeling performs best, and an explicit early-vs-late separation of visual cues is not needed. Using correct listener reactions yields better performance than cyclically mismatched reactions. In a human study with 20 speech researchers evaluating contextual appropriateness, 76% preferred the Temporal model, 9% preferred Text-only, and 15% had no preference. The integrated system with Grad-TTS successfully realizes end-to-end speech reflecting the predicted style.

Conclusion: Pre-response listener facial dynamics provide complementary information for planning conversational speech responses. Modeling these short-term visual cues temporally leads to more contextually appropriate and emotionally aligned TTS output than using text context alone or weaker visual baselines, and can be effectively integrated into a modern neural TTS backbone like Grad-TTS.

Abstract: Conversational speech depends on dialogue context and the listener's immediately preceding behavior. We propose ReACT-TTS, a two-stage framework that uses a one-second pre-response listener facial sequence to plan the next utterance's emotion and prosody before speech realization. On a strict dyadic MELD protocol, Temporal conditioning yields higher mean macro-F1 and VAD concordance than Text-only across ten seeds, while accuracy remains essentially unchanged. Ablations show that temporal modeling performs best among the visual variants and that an explicit early-to-late difference is unnecessary; correct listener reactions also outperform cyclic mismatches on average. In a contextual-appropriateness study with 20 speech researchers, 76% of judgments prefer Temporal, 9% Text-only, and 15% report no preference. We further connect the predicted response style to a Grad-TTS backbone for end-to-end speech realization. Overall, the results support pre-response listener dynamics as complementary cues for conversational response planning. The source code is available at https://github.com/CYJ1/ReACT-TTS_public.

</details>


### [103] [World Modeling in Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21748)
*Pierre Beckmann,Matthieu Queloz,Andre Freitas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies why a navigation-oriented transformer (TaxiGPT) shows behavioral errors that look like a bad world model and finds that the model actually has a fairly coherent internal map, with failures arising from representational interference rather than absence of world modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers often judge whether transformers have a "world model" based on behavioral tests. TaxiGPT, trained on random walks in Manhattan, shows navigation mistakes that some interpret as proof that it lacks a coherent internal map. The authors are motivated to test this claim mechanistically: does the model internally represent geography and navigation structure even when its behavior is flawed? More broadly, they aim to replace yes/no judgments about world models with a mechanistic understanding of how such models are implemented and where they fail.

Method: They train a transformer, TaxiGPT, on random walks through Manhattan and then apply mechanistic interpretability techniques. These include analyzing internal representations for intersections, streets, and positional information; identifying a "goal compass" representation; and running causal interventions to test which components are necessary for localization and navigation. They study how intersection features are superposed in the network and how affordance-based grouping (packing intersections with the same legal moves into similar representations) affects behavior. Finally, they define mechanistic indicators of world-modeling capacity and examine how these develop at different training stages.

Result: They find that TaxiGPT robustly encodes intersections, streets, and the agent’s current position, and it maintains a representation of the goal direction (a goal compass). This shows the model has a structured internal map-like representation. However, they also discover that features of different intersections are superposed in ways that sometimes interfere with accurate localization, leading to navigation errors that previously looked like evidence of an incoherent map. Affordance packing mitigates failures by clustering intersections according to allowed moves, thereby limiting the impact of localization errors. Their mechanistic indicators reveal that different aspects of world modeling emerge at distinct times during training.

Conclusion: Behavioral errors alone are not reliable evidence that a transformer lacks a world model; models can have reasonably faithful internal representations while still failing due to interference or other mechanistic issues. For TaxiGPT, the key failure mode is interference between superposed intersection features, not an absence of map structure. The authors argue that research should move from asking whether models have a world model to mechanistically dissecting how world modeling is implemented, how its components interact, and where in the computation pipeline failures arise.

Abstract: Behavioral failures can make a transformer appear to lack a world model even when it has learned faithful representations of its environment. We demonstrate this in TaxiGPT, a transformer trained on random walks through Manhattan whose failures have been interpreted as evidence of an incoherent internal map. Through mechanistic analysis and causal interventions, we show that the model represents intersections and streets, tracks its position, and uses a goal compass to navigate. We trace its failures to interference between superposed intersection features, which disrupts localization within the internal map. Affordance packing, which groups representations of intersections with the same legal moves, helps limit the consequences of these errors. Finally, we propose mechanistic indicators that we use to compare models and show that world-modeling capacities emerge at different stages of training. Our findings motivate a shift from asking whether a model has a world model to mechanistically studying its world modeling: the interacting capacities through which it represents its environment and uses those representations to guide behavior.

</details>


### [104] [ECG Mirage: Revealing and Mitigating the Underutilisation of ECGs in Vision-Language Models for Clinical Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21755)
*Jinning Liang,Mingcheng Zhu,Tingting Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies and analyzes a failure mode in multimodal clinical vision–language models (VLMs) for emergency department predictions, where ECG inputs appear useful but are not truly exploited, and proposes visual prompt tuning to make models meaningfully use patient-specific ECGs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Emergency department decisions depend on many heterogeneous data sources, especially ECGs. Vision–language models can technically ingest both text and images, but strong headline performance may hide the fact that they ignore or mishandle the ECG signal. This is dangerous in clinical settings, where we need to know whether a model truly uses patient-specific ECGs rather than spurious correlations. The authors therefore want to diagnose and quantify when VLMs do not actually depend on the correct ECG, and explore how to enforce better ECG usage without retraining large backbones.

Method: The authors define a new failure mode called “ECG Mirage,” split into ECG neglect (ECG adds no benefit) and ECG confusion (matched ECG does not outperform mismatched ECGs). They design an evaluation where, for a fixed clinical note and prediction target (ICU admission or clinical deterioration), they compare VLM predictions under three conditions: (1) matched ECG, (2) mismatched ECG from a patient with discordant outcome, and (3) no ECG. They apply this to four VLMs on the MDS-ED dataset. To mitigate ECG Mirage, they keep the VLM backbone frozen and train four restricted visual prompts using supervised learning followed by conditional direct preference optimization to encourage dependence on the correct ECG.

Result: Across four existing VLMs on the MDS-ED dataset, predictions with matched ECGs do not consistently outperform either no-ECG inputs or outcome-discordant mismatched ECGs for ICU admission or deterioration, indicating ECG Mirage. After training restricted visual prompts with supervised learning plus conditional direct preference optimization while freezing the backbone, the tuned models attain balanced accuracies of 70.6% for ICU admission and 67.5% for deterioration. Crucially, they increase the performance gap between matched and mismatched ECGs to about 16.5 percentage points for ICU prediction and 5.5 points for deterioration, showing more meaningful and specific use of the ECG signal.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that current multimodal clinical VLMs can exhibit ECG Mirage—appearing to use ECGs while providing little or misdirected benefit from the true patient ECG. By systematically comparing matched, mismatched, and no-ECG scenarios, the authors show this is a real and measurable problem. They propose visual prompt tuning, applied on top of frozen VLM backbones, as an efficient way to encourage genuine dependence on patient-specific ECGs, yielding better alignment between model predictions and the intended multimodal behavior.

Abstract: Emergency department (ED) decision-making relies on heterogeneous clinical information, including patient history, vital signs, laboratory results, and electrocardiograms (ECGs). Vision--language models (VLMs) can jointly process these modalities, but strong predictive performance does not necessarily imply meaningful use of the correct patient's ECG. We term this failure mode ECG Mirage: apparent multimodal capability without useful dependence on patient-specific ECG information. We distinguish two forms: ECG neglect, where ECGs provide little predictive benefit, and ECG confusion, where matched ECGs outperform no-image inputs but not mismatched ECGs. To evaluate these behaviours, we compare predictions obtained with matched ECGs, outcome-discordant mismatched ECGs, and no-image inputs while holding the clinical text and prediction targets fixed. Across four VLMs on MDS-ED, matched ECGs provide no consistent advantage for either ICU admission or clinical deterioration prediction. We then train four restricted visual prompts using supervised learning followed by conditional direct preference optimisation, while keeping the VLM backbone frozen. The resulting models achieve balanced accuracies of 70.6% for ICU admission and 67.5% for deterioration and increase the matched-versus-mismatched performance gap to approximately 16.5 and 5.5 percentage points, respectively. Overall, our study identifies ECG Mirage in multimodal clinical prediction and introduces visual prompt tuning as an efficient mitigation strategy.

</details>


### [105] [LLM-Generated Feature Pools for Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21801)
*Youssef Attia El Hili,Malik Tiomoko,Corinne Ancourt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that a simple, well-tuned statistical pipeline can rival complex neural and foundation models on univariate time series anomaly detection, and that the choice of candidate statistics matters more than the selection strategy, with LLM-generated feature pools enabling competitive performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how far a simple, interpretable statistical approach can go on univariate time series anomaly detection when evaluated under a strict, fair protocol, and to test whether careful feature engineering and selection can challenge state-of-the-art neural and foundation models.

Method: They build a pipeline that: (1) computes a small set of statistical features over sliding windows of a univariate time series; (2) scores each window with a robust, transductive anomaly model based on median absolute deviation (MAD); (3) uses a held-out tuning split per domain to select a subset of features. They then analyze performance sensitivity to selection strategies, aggregation schemes, and the underlying candidate feature pool. Finally, they expand the candidate pool by prompting a multimodal LLM with in-context example windows from each domain, using the generated statistics as additional features and re-running the selection process.

Result: On the TSB-AD-U benchmark, the pipeline achieves 0.529 per-series VUS-PR, outperforming the best neural (0.45) and traditional statistical (0.44) leaderboard entries and coming within 0.06 of the strongest pretrained foundation model, some of which use more supervision. Ablation studies show that different selection strategies change performance by only 0.031 VUS-PR, aggregation choices by 0.096, while modifying the candidate feature pool alters performance by 0.226, indicating that the feature pool is the primary performance driver. LLM-generated feature pools perform on par with hand-crafted ones under the same selection strategy, and combining both pools consistently improves results across twelve generator seeds, reaching 0.588 VUS-PR, which matches the best leaderboard entry.

Conclusion: A relatively simple statistical anomaly detection pipeline, when paired with a strong and diverse pool of candidate statistics, can match or exceed the performance of far more complex neural and foundation models on univariate time series benchmarks. The dominant performance factor is the composition of the feature pool rather than the subtleties of selection or aggregation. Multimodal LLMs can effectively help construct such pools by generating useful domain-specific statistical features from example windows, and combining LLM-generated with hand-crafted statistics yields the strongest, state-of-the-art performance.

Abstract: We study how far a simple statistical pipeline can go on univariate time series anomaly detection under a strict selection protocol. The method extracts a small pool of statistics over sliding windows, scores each window with a transductive robust (MAD) model, and selects a feature subset per domain on a held-out tuning split. On TSB-AD-U it reaches $0.529$ per-series VUS-PR, above the best neural ($0.45$) and statistical ($0.44$) entries on the public leaderboard and within $0.06$ of the strongest pretrained foundation model, several of which use more supervision than ours. Ablations locate the cause: across three selection strategies and a hindsight oracle the score moves by $0.031$, and across the aggregation grid by $0.096$, while changing the candidate pool moves it by $0.226$. The candidate pool sets the ceiling; the search over it is second-order. We therefore generate a pool per domain by prompting a multimodal LLM with in-context example windows from that domain. The generated pools match the hand-crafted one under matched selection, and the two cover different domains: selecting over their union improves on the generated pool in all twelve generator-seed pairs and lifts the pipeline to $0.588$, matching the performance of the best entry on the leaderboard.

</details>


### [106] [MIST: Multimodal Survival Prediction with Genomic-Guided Histology Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21811)
*Muhammet Sami Yavuz,Sabri Mustafa Kahya,Richard R. Chen,Jana Lipkova,Benedikt Wiestler*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MIST is a multimodal survival prediction framework that uses genomic-guided attention over histology representations to improve robust survival prediction across external cancer cohorts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal survival models that combine whole-slide histology images and genomic data can enhance prognostic accuracy, but current fusion strategies struggle with cohort shift, overfitting, and computational demands. Existing approaches often fuse modalities only at the final stage, missing fine-grained interactions between molecular and histologic patterns, and can be brittle when deployed on new external datasets.

Method: The authors propose MIST, a multimodal survival prediction method where genomic features are encoded as tokens that attend to compact histology context tokens derived from a pretrained foundation model on whole-slide images. Instead of late fusion, genomic tokens query these visual tokens through an attention mechanism to enrich molecular representations with histologic context before survival prediction. Training uses discrete-time survival modeling and several robustness-oriented strategies: genomic feature masking to regularize reliance on specific genomic inputs, whole-slide image (WSI) dropout to handle missing or noisy images, and a paired WSI–genomics contrastive alignment loss that encourages consistent cross-modal representations.

Result: In four external validation cohorts covering colon, renal, lung, and glioblastoma cancers, MIST achieves higher external concordance index (C-index) than standard multimodal fusion baselines in the primary comparisons, indicating better generalization under cohort shift and improved survival risk stratification performance.

Conclusion: Genomic-guided histology attention—where genomic tokens selectively attend to compact visual context from WSIs—offers a computationally efficient and externally robust fusion strategy for multimodal oncology survival prediction. The empirical gains across diverse cancer cohorts suggest that structured cross-modal interaction, coupled with regularization and contrastive alignment, is more effective than simple late fusion for integrating genomics and histology in outcome modeling.

Abstract: Multimodal survival models can combine complementary prognostic information from whole-slide images and genomic profiles, but effective fusion remains challenging amid external cohort shift and computational complexity. To address these challenges, we propose MIST, multimodal survival prediction with genomic-guided histology attention. MIST represents genomic features as tokens and allows them to query compact foundation-model-derived histology context tokens before survival prediction. This design enriches molecular information with histology context rather than merging separately encoded modalities only at the final stage. Training combines discrete-time survival prediction with genomic feature masking, WSI dropout, and paired WSI-genomics contrastive alignment. Across four external evaluations in colon, renal, lung, and glioblastoma cohorts, MIST improves external C-index over standard fusion baselines in the primary comparisons. These results support genomic-guided histology attention as a compact and effective strategy for multimodal oncology outcome prediction. Our code is available at https://github.com/samiyavuuz/MIST .

</details>


### [107] [EnterpriseVal: Quantifying the Efficacy, Reliability and Value of Generative AI in the Enterprise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21841)
*Abbas Raza Ali,Muhammad Ajmal Siddiqui,Moona Zahid*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper introduces EnterpriseVal, a system to evaluate enterprise GenAI use cases, bridging gap between model benchmarks and real business deployment decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite strong LLM performance on public benchmarks, many enterprise GenAI and agentic initiatives fail to show measurable business impact or are cancelled. Current benchmarks answer what models can do in general, not whether a specific workflow in a specific enterprise context is fit, reliable, safe, and economically worthwhile. There is a need for a rigorous, workflow-level evaluation framework aligned with enterprise constraints, risk, and value.

Method: The paper proposes EnterpriseVal, a use-case-level evaluation framework consisting of: (i) formal specification of the use case and the full socio-technical configuration (model, prompts, retrieval, tools, guardrails, human oversight), including autonomy level and consequence tier that define required evaluation intensity; (ii) a metric catalogue covering fidelity, utility, efficiency, reliability, assurance, and oversight; (iii) a grading protocol combining blinded expert human judgment with calibrated LLM-as-judge scoring using prediction-powered inference to scale evaluation; (iv) a two-tier threshold gate, expressed as an executable algorithm, mapping metric vectors with confidence bounds into REJECT/CONDITIONAL/SCALE deployment decisions; and (v) a value-and-risk model where reviewer catch rate is empirically measured and incorporated. They run a pilot across three workflows in a global bank to demonstrate feasibility.

Result: In a pilot deployment in a global bank across three workflows, EnterpriseVal produced measurable results. For a credit-memo drafting use case, human-graded citation precision for the best model reached 88% with a hallucination rate of 1.6%, surpassing predefined gates of 70% citation precision and 5% hallucination rate. For a procedure-transformation workflow, analyst refinement effort dropped from an estimated 27.4 hours to 2.9 hours per document. The system yielded actionable REJECT/CONDITIONAL/SCALE decisions for the evaluated workflows and quantified both value and risk under the specified socio-technical setups.

Conclusion: EnterpriseVal offers a structured, use-case-centric approach to evaluating GenAI systems in enterprise settings, filling the gap between generic benchmarks and real-world deployment decisions. By formally specifying workflows and socio-technical configurations, using a rich metric catalogue, scaling expert assessment with LLM-as-judge methods, and mapping results to deployment decisions via an explicit gating algorithm and value-risk model, it enables more reliable, economically grounded go/no-go decisions. The authors clearly distinguish what is already established, what is supported by pilot evidence, and what remains hypothetical, and they outline the experiments needed for full validation of the framework.

Abstract: Frontier language models now produce professional deliverables that expert graders judge to match human work on a substantial share of economically valuable tasks, yet most enterprise GenAI initiatives fail to show a measurable business effect and a large fraction of agentic projects are expected to be cancelled. We argue that this is substantially a measurement problem: public benchmarks answer "what can the model do?", whereas a deployment decision requires "is this workflow fit, reliable, safe and worth scaling - here, on our data, under our controls?". We present EnterpriseVal, a use-case-level evaluation system that closes this gap. It comprises (i) a formal specification of the use case and of the frozen socio-technical configuration under test, model, prompts, retrieval, tools, guardrails and human oversight, with an autonomy level and consequence tier that jointly set the required evaluation intensity; (ii) a metric catalogue spanning fidelity, utility, efficiency, reliability, assurance and oversight; (iii) a grading protocol that scales blinded expert judgement with calibrated LLM-as-judge scoring through prediction-powered inference; (iv) a two-tier threshold gate, stated as an executable algorithm, that maps metric vectors with confidence bounds to REJECT/CONDITIONAL/SCALE decisions; and (v) a value-and-risk model in which the reviewer catch rate is a measured parameter. We report a pilot across three workflows in a global bank. In credit-memo drafting, human-graded citation precision reached 88% and hallucination rate 1.6% for the best model against gates of 70% and 5%; in procedure transformation, analyst refinement effort fell from an estimated 27.4 to 2.9 hours per document. We separate established results, documented pilot evidence, the proposed system and open hypotheses, and specify the experiments required for full validation

</details>


### [108] [AutoRecLab: Describe the Experiment, Get the Code!](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21863)
*Moritz Baumgart,Philipp Meister,Justus Krell,Michael Schmidt,Bela Gipp,Joeran Beel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoRecLab is an autonomous Python-based lab that turns natural-language research ideas for recommender-systems experiments into executable code, using RAG, static type checks, and execution-steered search, and can run complex studies cheaply and reliably.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing and implementing empirical evaluations in recommender-systems research is labor-intensive, requires coding expertise, and is prone to implementation errors. Researchers must manually translate high-level experimental ideas into correct, reproducible pipelines. With the growing complexity of RecSys workflows and available tools, there is a need to automate this translation from natural-language experimental designs to reliable, executable experiments.

Method: AutoRecLab takes a natural-language description of a RecSys research idea and automatically derives explicit experimental requirements. It then iteratively builds a prototype experiment, validates it, and expands it into a complete experiment. The system integrates three key components: (1) retrieval-augmented generation to query and integrate relevant documentation, (2) static type verification to ensure code correctness and interface compatibility, and (3) execution-steered tree search to explore and refine alternative implementation paths based on runtime feedback and success signals.

Result: In a demonstration use case, AutoRecLab autonomously implemented a study converting explicit user feedback into implicit feedback for recommender evaluation. In a baseline comparison involving six recommendation algorithms and three datasets, AutoRecLab successfully executed 8 out of 9 full experimental runs. Using the GPT-5.4-mini model as the backbone, the average computational/API cost was about $1 per successful run, indicating both reliability and cost-effectiveness.

Conclusion: AutoRecLab shows that it is feasible to autonomously generate and execute non-trivial recommender-systems experiments directly from natural-language descriptions. By combining RAG, static type checking, and execution-steered search, it reduces manual coding effort and lowers the risk of implementation errors, while remaining inexpensive to operate. This suggests a promising direction for automated, scalable, and accessible RecSys experimentation workflows driven by high-level research ideas.

Abstract: Empirical evaluation is central to recommender-systems (RecSys) research, but turning experimental designs into executable code remains a manual and error-prone task. We present AutoRecLab, a Python-based autonomous RecSys lab that automates RecSys experiments from natural-language prompts. Given a research idea, AutoRecLab derives explicit experiment requirements, builds and validates a prototype, and iteratively expands it into the requested full experiment. The workflow combines retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) for documentation lookup, static type verification, and execution-steered tree search. In our demonstration, AutoRecLab autonomously implements an explicit-to-implicit feedback conversion study. In a baseline comparison across six algorithms and three datasets, 8 of 9 runs succeed at an average cost of approx- imately $1 per run with GPT-5.4-mini.

</details>


### [109] [What Should We Ask Next? Retrieval-Aware Question Learning under Partial Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21924)
*Lyucheng Qian,John Yuehan Zhang,Pingyu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose RAVEL, an online reinforcement learning framework that chooses questions during interactive person re-identification to improve retrieval over multiple rounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In interactive retrieval under partial evidence, an agent must ask clarifying questions to gather information that improves subsequent retrieval. Existing systems typically learn which questions to ask by imitating a fixed, offline ordering of candidate question–answer (QA) pairs. This ignores that a question’s true value depends on the actual answer obtained and its downstream impact on the ranked retrieval results. The authors aim to move beyond static, imitation-based question ordering toward a method that directly optimizes question selection for retrieval performance in an online, interaction-aware way.

Method: They introduce RAVEL, a retrieval-aware online reinforcement learning framework tailored to interactive person re-identification. The system starts from a supervised question generation model, then operates in a closed loop: at each interaction round, it observes the current Top-4 retrieval candidates and chooses the next question to ask. The environment returns an answer, the retrieval is updated, and the agent receives rank-based feedback reflecting retrieval quality. Using this feedback, RAVEL optimizes its question-selection policy via reinforcement learning over multiple rounds of interaction. The framework also incorporates weak supervision signals such as candidate discriminativeness and perceived usefulness, but treats them only as initial guidance rather than the main training objective.

Result: On the Interactive-PEDES benchmark, RAVEL achieves progressively stronger retrieval performance across five rounds of user–system interaction, outperforming baselines that rely on static or imitation-learned question ordering. The learned policy significantly improves ranking quality, particularly on difficult initial queries. Analysis of the learned behavior shows that RAVEL shifts its questioning strategy toward more localized, open-ended attribute questions, which tend to supply higher-value evidence for distinguishing among visually similar candidates.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that interactive retrieval benefits from treating question selection as an online reinforcement learning problem tied directly to retrieval outcomes, rather than as an offline ordering task. By integrating rank feedback from the full question–answer–retrieval loop, RAVEL learns to allocate its limited questioning budget to the most informative, localized attributes, leading to better person re-identification over multiple interaction rounds and especially for challenging cases. This demonstrates the effectiveness of retrieval-aware RL for interactive vision-language retrieval tasks.

Abstract: Interactive retrieval under partial evidence is a sequential information-acquisition problem: an agent must decide which question will create the most useful evidence for the next retrieval update. Existing systems train this decision by imitating an offline ordering of candidate QA pairs, although question value is determined by the response it elicits and its downstream effect on retrieval. We establish that candidate discriminativeness and perceived usefulness provide weak supervision for this objective, then introduce RAVEL, a retrieval-aware online reinforcement learning framework for interactive person re-identification. RAVEL initializes from supervised question generation, observes the current Top-4 candidates directly, and optimizes the question policy with rank feedback from the full question-answer-retrieval loop. Experiments on Interactive-PEDES show that RAVEL delivers progressively stronger retrieval performance across five interaction rounds. Further analysis shows that RAVEL reallocates the questioning budget toward localized open-ended attributes, which provide more useful retrieval evidence and yield the largest gains on initially difficult queries.

</details>


### [110] [AutoViewMem: Self-Configuring Orthogonal Views for Conversational Long-Term Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21940)
*Zijie Cao,Xijun Qu,Zhicheng Gu,Xiaoshu Chen,Duanyang Yuan,Yanning Hou,Sihang Zhou,Jianxing Gong,Jian Huang,Yang Mei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoViewMem is a framework that restructures long-term conversational memory for LLM agents into multiple low-overlap semantic views, enabling cleaner write-time organization and more effective top-K retrieval, which improves long-horizon QA and personalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents need long-term memory to stay consistent and personalized across long interactions, but current memory systems usually store everything in a single or coarse-grained representation. This mixed representation blends heterogeneous information like preferences, events, constraints, and temporal updates, causing semantic interference. As a result, simple top-K retrieval becomes noisy, and relevant evidence is often poorly ranked. The authors are motivated to design a memory system that avoids this interference while keeping the retrieval pipeline simple.

Method: The authors propose AutoViewMem, a data-driven framework that first discovers candidate semantic views from past interaction traces, then selects a compact set of complementary, low-overlap views. These views define how to structure and separate information at write time. When new interactions occur, the system performs structured extraction of memories into these semantic views, preserving provenance. Thus semantic disentanglement is done at write time rather than at retrieval time. Memory is indexed per view, so standard top-K similarity search can be applied within or across views without complex routing. Additionally, an offline consolidation step periodically compacts and reconciles the stored memories to improve consistency and reduce redundancy.

Result: On the LoCoMo and PersonaMem benchmarks, using Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-14B as backbone LLMs, AutoViewMem outperforms strong long-term memory baselines on long-horizon question answering and personalization tasks. It achieves these gains while maintaining a relatively simple inference-time retrieval pipeline based on standard similarity search, indicating that the representation-first, multi-view memory design leads to more effective use of long-term conversational history.

Conclusion: AutoViewMem demonstrates that organizing conversational memories into self-configuring, low-overlap semantic views at write time reduces semantic interference and enables more accurate and focused retrieval with standard top-K similarity search. This representation-first approach allows LLM agents to better handle heterogeneous long-term information, improving long-horizon QA and personalization without requiring complicated retrieval-time routing or iterative mechanisms. Offline consolidation further enhances memory compactness and consistency, making AutoViewMem an effective and practical long-term memory solution for LLM agents.

Abstract: Long-term memory is essential for large language model (LLM) agents to maintain consistency and personalization over extended interactions. Existing memory systems typically rely on fixed granularities or static schemas, but these designs struggle when heterogeneous information, such as preferences, events, constraints, and temporal updates, is embedded in a single mixed representation. The resulting semantic interference makes top-K retrieval sensitive to noise and often leaves relevant evidence poorly ranked. We present AutoViewMem, a data-driven framework that organizes long-term conversational memory into self-configuring, low-overlap semantic views before indexing. AutoViewMem discovers candidate views from interaction traces, selects a compact complementary view set, and uses these views to guide write-time structured extraction of provenance-grounded memories. This representation-first design moves semantic disentanglement from retrieval time to write time, allowing standard top-K similarity search to retrieve focused evidence without explicit routing or iterative retrieval. We further apply offline consolidation to improve memory compactness and consistency. Experiments on the LoCoMo and PersonaMem benchmarks, under both Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-14B backbones, show that AutoViewMem improves long-horizon question answering and personalization over strong memory baselines while preserving a simple inference pipeline.

</details>


### [111] [Learning Cardiac Features: ECG Biometrics Across Time and~Exercise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21962)
*Luca Thiebaud,Paul Chauchat,Mustapha Ouladsine,Stéphane Delliaux*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how robust ECG-based biometric identification is under realistic stress and over time, showing low error rates and evidence of a stable cardiac signature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most ECG biometric studies use single-session, resting data, so they do not test whether identification remains reliable when a person’s physiological state changes (e.g., during exercise) or across different recording sessions. There is a need to understand if ECG biometrics can withstand real-world temporal and physiological variability, which is crucial for secure authentication, protecting cardiac data, and for use as a pretext task in self-supervised learning on ECGs.

Method: The authors train a Siamese ResNet architecture with a late multi-lead fusion strategy on a large ECG dataset collected from cardiopulmonary exercise tests. They design an exercise- and time-aware evaluation protocol to test intra-session (rest to exercise peak) and cross-session performance. They also benchmark their model on public datasets, including CYBHi, to compare against existing approaches.

Result: The proposed system achieves an intra-session rest-to-peak equal error rate (EER) of 1.7% under exercise-induced stress and a state-of-the-art 3.9% EER on the CYBHi dataset, indicating strong robustness to both physiological and temporal variability compared with prior work.

Conclusion: The experiments show that ECGs contain an intrinsic cardiac signature that remains discriminative even under substantial physiological (exercise-induced) and temporal changes. This supports the viability of ECG biometrics for realistic, real-world conditions and suggests that such signatures can be reliably exploited both for secure authentication and as a rich representation source in self-supervised learning.

Abstract: Electrocardiograms (ECGs) carry subject-specific patterns enabling reliable individual discrimination, forming the basis of ECG biometrics. Beyond authentication, this paradigm holds significant potential to secure sensitive cardiac data and to serve as a pretext task in self-supervised learning. Yet, most studies remain confined to singlesession, resting data, leaving robustness to temporal and physiological variations largely untested. We address this gap by evaluating ECG biometrics under realistic conditions involving exercise-induced stress and cross-session variability. A Siamese ResNet with late multi-lead fusion strategy is trained on a large ECG dataset extracted from cardiopulmonary exercise tests and evaluated with a exercise-and time-aware protocol, as well as on public benchmarks. This first extensive assessment of ECG biometrics under combined physiological and temporal variability achieves an intra-session rest-to-peak EER of 1.7% and stateof-the-art 3.9% on the CYBHi dataset. Findings support the presence of an intrinsic cardiac signature resilient to physiological and temporal drift.

</details>


### [112] [A Lie Detector Test for Language Models: Reading Knowledge a Model Won't Reveal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21996)
*Hiskias Dingeto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces PIR, a method to detect when a language model internally recognizes the correct answer even if it outwardly hides or withholds it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models may “sandbag”: they can know an answer internally but intentionally respond incorrectly or refuse to answer. From external behavior alone, we cannot tell whether a model is ignorant or strategically concealing knowledge. This gap matters for safety audits, capability evaluations, and verifying techniques like unlearning, where we must know if knowledge is truly gone versus just being suppressed.

Method: The authors adapt the psychological/forensic Concealed Information Test to LLMs. They create Probe of Internal Recognition (PIR), which presents a question and several candidate answers to a model, then inspects its internal activations to see which option it “recognizes” as correct. PIR is reference-free: it does not rely on a trusted reference model or labeled ground-truth corpus. They evaluate PIR on multiple LLM families (Gemma, Qwen, Llama, Mistral, Phi), across different concealment setups (prompted deception, trained sandbagging, password-locked checkpoints, circuit-broken checkpoints). They also extend PIR from multiple-choice settings to free-form generation, and manipulate models via unlearning to compare internal recognition when knowledge is present versus removed.

Result: PIR can identify the answer that the model internally recognizes with balanced accuracies of 0.70–0.87, compared to 0.28–0.40 for questions where the correct answer is truly unknown to the model and 0.25 chance. Under explicit concealment conditions (prompted deception, trained sandbagging, password locks, circuit breaking), PIR still detects the recognized answer with high accuracy (0.85–0.93). When models are subjected to unlearning and genuinely lose knowledge, the PIR signal drops to the same level as for questions they never knew in the first place. The authors also show that the PIR signal is causal and provides information beyond black-box behavioral outputs, and that it generalizes from multiple-choice formats to free-form generation tasks.

Conclusion: PIR reliably distinguishes between a model that will not answer and one that cannot because it lacks the knowledge. This enables audits for sandbagging and tools to verify whether unlearning methods have truly removed knowledge. Since PIR works without reference models or labeled ground truth and remains effective even under deliberate concealment, it offers a practical and robust means to read out internal recognition from LLMs and to complement purely behavioral evaluation approaches.

Abstract: Large language models can hold knowledge they do not report. A model may sandbag on a capability evaluation, or answer against what it internally knows, and its outputs alone cannot tell whether it is hiding an answer or simply does not have one. We borrow the Concealed Information Test, a forensic method that identifies guilty knowledge by presenting a suspect with the true detail among plausible decoys and measuring a stronger response to the item they recognize. Our method, Probe of Internal Recognition (PIR), does the same inside a model. It presents a question with its candidate answers and reads, from the model's internal states, which candidate the model recognizes as correct. PIR is reference-free, needing no honest reference model and no labeled truth corpus. Across eight models from five families (Gemma, Qwen, Llama, Mistral, and Phi), PIR recovers the recognized answer at 0.70 to 0.87 balanced accuracy, well above the 0.28 to 0.40 unknown-item baseline and the 0.25 chance rate. It stays readable across every form of concealment we test, from prompted deception and trained sandbagging to external password-locked and circuit-broken checkpoints, with recognition between 0.85 and 0.93. When the model hides a known answer, recognition stays high. When unlearning removes the knowledge, recognition drops to the level of a question the model never knew. PIR therefore separates a model that will not answer from one that cannot, which supports sandbagging audits and unlearning verification. The signal is causal, adds information beyond black-box behavioral cues, and extends from multiple-choice questions to free-form generation.

</details>


### [113] [CodeMidas: Scaling Agentic Coding RL Environments from Code Itself](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22068)
*Bowen Ye,Lei Li,Shicheng Li,Zihao Yue,Linghao Zhang,Hanglong Lv,Yuanxin Liu,Wenhan Ma,Hao Tian,Rang Li,Jinhao Dong,Yikai Zhao,Xiangwei Deng,Hailin Zhang,Liang Zhao,Qi Liu,Lingpeng Kong,Tong Yang,Fuli Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CodeMidas automatically turns existing open-source code into thousands of reinforcement-learning environments to train stronger coding agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training coding agents with RL needs many diverse, well-verified tasks. Existing approaches mostly mine issues, commits, or other development artifacts, which limits task diversity and scale. The authors want a way to systematically convert the huge amount of existing open-source code into RL-ready tasks without relying on extra metadata, enabling broader, more scalable RL training for coding agents.

Method: They build CodeMidas, a multi-agent pipeline that uses only source code from open-source projects. Agentic components: (1) explore codebases to infer behavioral specifications of implemented functionality; (2) synthesize tests grounded in actual executions of the original code; (3) validate, filter, and refine candidate tasks via execution checks and repeated solution rollouts. This produces executable RL environments where agents interact by writing or editing code and get feedback from the generated tests. They then train the MiMo-V2.5 coding model with GRPO (a policy-gradient RL method) on these tasks. They run ablations on task quantity/quality and analyze trajectories to study behavioral changes in the trained agents.

Result: CodeMidas produces a dataset of 5,545 training tasks from 3,185 open-source codebases spanning 23 languages and 15 technical domains. RL training of MiMo-V2.5 on these tasks with GRPO yields consistent gains on five varied benchmarks: issue repair (DeepSWE: +11.7%), whole-program synthesis (ProgramBench: +17%), and terminal-based tasks (Terminal-Bench v2.1: +8.5%), among others. Ablations show that more and higher-quality tasks steadily boost performance, and trajectory analysis reveals improved exploration and self-verification behaviors in the RL-trained agent.

Conclusion: Source code alone can serve as a scalable, rich foundation for constructing RL environments for code generation/repair. By automatically mining and validating tasks directly from open-source repositories, CodeMidas enables large-scale RL training that measurably improves coding agents across diverse software tasks and leads to more sophisticated search and verification behaviors.

Abstract: Training capable coding agents via reinforcement learning (RL) requires diverse tasks with reliable verifiers. Open-source codebases offer a rich source of such tasks, while existing methods typically rely on development artifacts such as issues and commits, limiting the range of tasks that can be extracted. To better scale RL environments, we present CodeMidas, an agentic pipeline that turns implemented functionality in existing codebases into executable RL environments using source code as its only task-specific input. CodeMidas allocates agentic compute to every stage of environment construction: agents explore implemented functionality to formulate behavioral specifications, construct tests grounded in execution of the original code, and validate and filter candidate tasks through execution checks and repeated solution rollouts. The resulting dataset has 5,545 training tasks from 3,185 open-source codebases spanning 23 programming languages and 15 technical domains. Training MiMo-V2.5 on these tasks with GRPO improves performance on all five diverse benchmarks, covering issue repair (DeepSWE + 11.7%), whole-program construction (ProgramBench +17%), and terminal work (Terminal-Bench v2.1 +8.5%). Ablations show that increasing the number of high-quality training tasks improves performance. Trajectory analysis shows the RL-trained agent demonstrates better behaviors like increasing codebase exploration and more diverse self-verification. These results establish source code as a scalable foundation for constructing RL environments that improve coding agents across diverse software tasks.

</details>


### [114] [Designer-RSI: Evolving Procedural Memory from User Traffic for Agentic Graphic Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22086)
*Hongyang Du,Lan Yan,Christian Flores,Asim Kadav*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a continual adaptation framework where a frozen large model uses a growing bank of natural-language procedures to operate complex design software, greatly improving success on professional graphic design tasks without updating model weights or using human labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Professional graphic design tasks require long-horizon, tool-heavy interaction with design software, producing structured, editable artifacts. These tasks lack a reliable automatic oracle for final quality, making it hard to train or adapt agents via standard supervised or reinforcement learning. The authors aim to enable continual improvement of an LLM-based design agent in this challenging, noisy-feedback setting, while keeping the base model frozen and avoiding costly human annotation.

Method: A frozen "frontier" LLM controls professional design software with over 230 tools. Around it, the authors build an external procedural memory made of natural-language design skills (procedures). The system operates on real user briefs and trajectories, automatically graded where possible. The memory widens by learning new procedures for recurring subtasks not covered by existing skills, and deepens by revising existing procedures using evidence from both successful and failed executions. A replay gate only accepts edits that fix failures without harming previously successful behaviors. Over multiple adaptation rounds, this skill bank grows and is refined, but the underlying model weights remain unchanged.

Result: Starting from 76 documentation-derived skills, the procedural memory grows to 139 skills after five adaptation rounds on 1,406 real briefs and 1,869 auto-graded trajectories. On the GenEval2 benchmark for design execution, success for Claude-Sonnet-4 rises from 72.7% to 99.3%, with an 11.99-point improvement in generation quality. Across four specialized design benchmarks on Claude-Sonnet-4 and Claude-Opus-4.6, the skill-augmented agent achieves win rates of 61.8% and 67.6% against a baseline without skills. On a held-out benchmark of 200 briefs, using only widening or only deepening yields win rates of 49.4% and 48.6% over the no-skill agent, while their combination reaches 58.5% (statistically significant with p = 0.025).

Conclusion: The paper concludes that an external procedural memory of natural-language skills enables effective continual adaptation of an LLM-based design agent in complex, long-horizon tasks under noisy, partially unverifiable feedback. By widening (adding new procedures) and deepening (refining existing ones) with a replay gate to prevent regressions, the system substantially improves execution success and design quality without updating model weights or relying on human labels. This suggests procedural memory as a practical path to make agentic LLM systems more capable and robust in professional creative domains.

Abstract: Professional graphic design is a long-horizon agentic task in which structured, editable artifacts emerge from many interdependent actions, yet outcomes admit no reliable programmatic oracle. We introduce a continual adaptation framework in which a frozen frontier model operates professional design software through more than 230 tools, while an external procedural memory of natural-language skills accumulates and refines reusable design procedures from experience. The memory widens by acquiring procedures for recurring uncovered subtasks and deepens by revising existing procedures against their own successful and failed executions, while a matched replay gate admits only changes that repair failures without regressing observed successes. Five rounds over 1,406 real user briefs and 1,869 automatically graded trajectories, with no weight updates and no human labels, grow the bank from 76 documentation-derived skills to 139 and raise GenEval2 execution success on Claude-Sonnet-4 from 72.7% to 99.3% (+11.99 points in generation quality), with 61.8% and 67.6% win rates against the no-skill agent across four specialized design benchmarks on Claude-Sonnet-4 and Claude-Opus-4.6. We further show the two mechanisms are effective in combination: on 200 held-out briefs from user-traffic benchmark, widening or deepening alone reaches a 49.4% / 48.6% win rate over the no-skill agent, while their combination reaches 58.5% (p = 0.025). Procedural memory offers a practical route to continual adaptation of agents under noisy, unverifiable feedback.

</details>
